# A.20. Executing Entity information

## Component 1, 2 & 4: AE i.e Infrastructure Development Company Limited (IDCOL) also the EE

### Component 3: Local Financial Institution (LFI) will be the EE namely South East Bank Limited, City Bank Limited, BRAC Bank Limited and GULC Finance Limited or any other financial institution which lends directly to the RMG manufacturers.

### Component 5: SREDA, Power Division of Bangladesh Energy Development Company Limited (BEDCL) as AE. SREDA Renewable Energy Development Authority (SREDA), GUL will be the Investment Promotion Agency (IPA).

## A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

CO₂ emissions per capita of Bangladesh increased from 0.18 metric tons in 1997 to 0.46 metric tons in 2014, having grown at an average rate of 3% annually. This is a ramifications of current economic growth due to industrial expansion. In order to support the present industrial and economic growth, energy production is soaring in the country, causing a higher fossil fuel emission.

The industrial sector in Bangladesh accounts for 47.8% of the commercial energy consumption, primarily driven by textile & RMG industries. The textile industry (52%) is the second largest energy consuming industrial sub-sector with 12.40% consumption after Garment sector (15.40%). The GoB's Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan states that manufacturing industries in Bangladesh are responsible for 38% of total energy consumption, which can be reduced by 20-30% through better energy management. The impetus towards efficient end use of energy in the industrial sector is lagging mostly due to inadequate financial incentives and lack of technical expertise. If the current industrial energy intensity continues at its present rate, it will be difficult for Bangladesh to meet its target that GoB face difficulties to manage the rising energy demand, and subsequently achieve GHG emission reduction targets under the UN Paris Climate accord.

Hence, the proposed programme aims to support business establishments in the textile and RMG sector with technical resources and financial incentives opportunities for energy saving technology upgrades. The intervention will create a favorable market environment and scalable business model for investment in energy efficiency improvements, leading to sizeable energy savings and accompanying carbon dioxide (CO₂) emission reductions. The project is aligned with Bangladesh's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC); i.e., 15% GHG emission reduction compared to the Business-As-Usual scenario by 2030. The textile & RMG sector contributes about 47% of total energy consumption and thus, it also contributes to the GoB target to achieve 10% of energy consumption reduction in the industry sector by 2020 and 20% improved energy intensity by 2030.

Overall programme will be implemented through an integrated package of concessional financing for textile & RMG borrowers, and technical assistance for creation of enabling environment by covering areas such as capacity building, awareness, support in loan disbursement and monitoring and evaluation of the program parameters. The Programme comprises of five interrelated and closely coordinated components:

| Component | Description |
|-----------|-------------|
| 1         | Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 by SREDA, Power Division |
| 2         | Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 by SREDA, Power Division |
| 3         | Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 by SREDA, Power Division |
| 4         | Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 by SREDA, Power Division |
| 5         | Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 by SREDA, Power Division |

**Page 7**

Component 1: USD 133.0 million financing for Energy Saving Equipment & Technology for Textile sector

Component 2: USD 2.05 million GCF Technical Assistance (TA) to develop enabling environment for EE investment in textile sector

Component 3: USD 200.0 million financing for Energy Saving Equipment & Technology for RMG sector

Component 4: USD 2.30 million GCF Technical Assistance (TA) to develop enabling environment for EE investment in RMG sector

Component 5: USD 1.15 million GCF Technical Assistance (TA) to strengthen regulatory & institutional framework at the national level to overcome the operational constraints related to implementing EESG in the country

In case of Component 1, 2 & 3, IFC will be the Executing Entity for Component 7, 8 & Under Component 4, IDCOL will receive GCF funding available in the form of concessional loans to the end users who are in this case the private textile manufacturing entities. And the borrower will seek disbursements from IDCOL, both in USD and BDT for energy efficient equipment & technology in line with the capacity proposed to be deployed every year over the project implementation period. Under Component 2, IFC will also provide support for execution of activities under Technical Assistance (TA).

Just like component 2, component 04 will be executed by IDCOL which consists of activities proposed as Technical Assistance (TA) in the Project Implementation Plan (PIP). The TA is required for successful implementation of Component 03 so to bring about a systemic change in the RMG sector.

In case of Component 3, IFC will channelize funds from GCF in the form of loan, to the Executing Entities (EEs) i.e. Lifs viz: BRAC Bank City, Bank South East Bank and IDCOL or any other Lifs as per project implementation plan. The EEs will then disburse these loans to the private RMG textile manufacturing entities. In some instances, IDCOL will lend directly to the RMG manufacturers. The Accredited Entity (AE), IDCOL, will receive fund from GCF and channel it to its Executing Entities (EEs). The loan application process is similar to that of Component 1. EEs will be required to submit a loan application pipeline every year over the project implementation period. The transaction through the channel will be in USD.

Component 05: This component will address the policy level issues for creation of enabling environment at national level of the country. It will target to strengthen regulatory & institutional framework, at the national level to overcome the operational constraints related to implementing EESG in the country. Sustainable and Renewable Energy Development Authority (SREDA) under Ministry of Power, Energy & Mineral Resources, GdB, will be the implementing partner.

Under Component 05, the GCF funding in the form of concessional senior loans will support the end users of textile and RMG sector activities by deploying a credit line to ensure that long term concessional financing is available in the market to overcome the barriers of high up-front costs, and longer payback periods associated with energy efficient equipment and technology. Every project under this modality and the addressed sectors of Component 01 and 03 will be different as mentioned above, but the overall objective will be the same which is to progress investment opportunities for energy saving technology

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 5 OF 155

upgrades in the country. Furthermore, GCF non-reimbursable resources (technical assistance), comprising under Component 02 and 04 aims to improve market perception about energy efficiency and provide support to the relevant stakeholders at all stages of implementing energy efficient measures. It is envisaged to create an enabling environment for promoting energy efficiency in Bangladesh by addressing the barriers that are behind the investment for energy efficiency i.e. Textile & RMG and borrowers, thereby helping disseminate the information related to energy efficiency and resolve any queries that the unit owners may have. Policy makers and other stakeholders will be supported through capacity building workshops and training sessions in developing enabling policies, regulations to help overcome these barriers. It is envisaged that without such enabling environment, it would be difficult for the private sector to invest in energy efficient measures and the stakeholders in undertaking energy efficiency measures. Also, given that the energy efficiency market in Bangladesh is in its infancy, such support activities would be critical to enable market transformation and growth. The proposed programme will also focus on strengthening institutional capacity of the government as a critical component to ensure greater uptake of energy efficiency measures during the programme duration in the two sectors, as well as ensure sustainability of energy efficiency market for both the sector beyond the programme.

The programme overall is expected to reduce or avoid about 14.53 MtCO2e of GHG emission reductions over the lifetime of the investments in both the sectors. The programme is first of its kind for the country considering specific focus on mitigation through energy efficiency of two largest energy intensive industries i.e. Textile and RMG sector, which together account for about 70% of total industrial energy consumption and energy intensive industries of the country. The paradigm shift potential for the proposed programme lies in the project's focus on overcoming barriers & constraints faced by the private sector (including the Local Finance Institutions) by providing knowledge & capacity building training locally as well as creating enabling environment to promote EE&Cs investments in textile and RMG sector of Bangladesh. Nationwide impact will be achieved through strengthening component 05, by Government owned institution SREDA, to strengthen regulatory & institutional framework at the national level to overcome the operational constraints related to implementing E&E&C in the country.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Context and Concept (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### Climate rationale:
Global warming is the most alarming phenomenon of the present time and to combat it, it is high time to take all possible measures. The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) was adopted in September 2015. Bangladesh ratified the Paris Agreement on 21 September 2016 and its NDC can now be found on the UNFCCC's interim NDC Registry<sup>1</sup>. Bangladesh has committed to reduce GHG emissions in the power, industry and transport sectors by 5% below business-as-usual (BAU) GHG emission levels. The country aims to achieve a reduction of 30-40% from BAU by 2030 if sufficient and appropriate support is received from developed countries.

Energy consumption, especially in the industrial sector is a significant factor for the GHG emissions which mainly originate from the power generation carried out by the industries on a priority basis. In Bangladesh, the textile sector accounts for 47.8% of the commercial energy demand, predominantly in the form of natural gas and electricity. However, as per GdB's Energy Efficiency & Conservation Master Plan 2030<sup>2</sup>, the industrial sector is expected to contribute more towards economic activities. Implementation of energy efficiency and conservation (EE&C) measures within the industrial sector will help in reducing GHG emissions from this sector by 5%. This is explained by the diagram below which gives the break-up of energy consumption across different industrial sectors under business-as-usual (BAU) scenario and an EE&C scenario which take in to account maximum energy efficiency potential for the industrial sector<sup>2</sup>.

## Figure 2: Energy consumption by sectors (source: SREDA energy balance 2016-17)

The feasibility study conducted for the textile sector suggests, the larger textile units are typically 10 to 15% more efficient than small-scale units. The RMG sector is also showing a trend of moving towards large units in terms of size. Hence, under Component 01, IDCOL based on successful due diligence we consider financing a segment of small and medium units as part of the programme.

The RMG sector also showed similar trend, in terms of energy efficiency, small factories having small-scale investments and hence lower operating revenues and short-term profits, rather than undertaking initiatives which will help improve productivity and increase profits in the long run. Expenses on energy efficiency is seen as an additional financial burden and is rarely undertaken. Therefore, under Component 03, IDCOL based on successful due diligence we consider financing a segment of small and medium units through high consultation and to create access to concessional finance for small and medium units on its successful due diligence.

The proposed programme aims to support entrepreneurs and business establishments in the textile and RMG sectors by financing energy efficiency improvements and/or implementing other enabling technology upgrades. The programme will create a favourable market environment and scalable business models that are conducive to energy efficiency improvements, leading to scalable energy savings and accompanying greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reductions of 14.03 million CO2 over the 20-year lifetime of the project investments.

## Snapshot of Textile Industry in Bangladesh

The modern story of the growth of textile industry in Bangladesh starts with the establishment of the Bangladesh Textiles Corporation (BTCM) on March 26, 1972 set up with the point of observing, planning and controlling the exercises of nationalization of factories of Bangladesh and building up and growing new enterprises in the field of textiles.

In addition to BTCM, Bangladesh Textile Mills Association (BTMA) is the prime national trade organization representing Yarn, Fabric Manufacturers and Textile Product Processors mills of the country under private sector and public sector are registered under BTMA. BTMA has been registered in 1983 and at present, 1470 Member Mills are now active under BTMA.

| Types of Factory | Numbers | Installed Capacity | Annual Capacity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Yarn Manufacturing Mills | 430 | Spindles: 13.17 Million Rotor: 52,211 Mtr. Shuttle & Shuttless Looms: N/A | 2943 Million Kgs. |
| Fabric Manufacturing Mills | 802 | Spindles: 6.3 Million Rotor: 52,211 Mtr. Shuttle & Shuttless Looms: N/A | 3707 Million Mtrs. |
| Dyeing-Printing-Finishing Mills | 244 | N/A | Fabric Processing: 3448 Mln. Yarn Dying: 398 Million Kgs Knit Dying: 572 Million Kgs Woven Dying: 240 Million |
| Total | 1476 |  |  |

Textile industries are the one of the fastest growing and amongst the most important industries of Bangladesh and contributes to a significant portion of the nation's economy, employment and global profile. Textile sector contributes more than 13% of Bangladesh’s GDP while also supporting 86% of the country’s export earnings. The textile industry has been a major contributor in the country’s economic growth as a testament to the huge importance of the sector in the country’s economy. The export of clothing/garment in Bangladesh has increased from USD 2 billion in 1974 to USD 30 billion in 2018 with an average annual growth of ~8%.

Even though textile sector is a growing industry in Bangladesh, however it is still not self-sufficient in terms of serving the demand of the local garment sectors. The local textile mills can meet only 65 percent of the country’s domestic demand for yarn and fabric to serve the woven sector, according to data from the BTMA. Bangladesh needs USD 1.17 billion of national investment into the primary textile sector to reduce its import dependence for fabrics for the export-oriented garment sector.

The industry (along with the garment industry) is one of the largest employers in the country, generating more than 85% of the country’s industrial employment and employing over 4 million. Out of this a significant proportion are women employed in the textile sector. The textile sector has around 10-20% male employees and women employed but textile in particular has around 20-30% female employees according to the feasibility study. Thus any improvement in the conditions in this sector will play an important role in the fight against poverty.

Energy in the textile industry is mostly used in the forms of electricity, as a common power source for machinery, cooling and temperature control systems, lighting, office equipment etc; diesel oil as fuel for boilers which generate steam: liquefied petroleum gas, coal, and natural gas. A breakdown of the processes is done to study the various process deployed in textile units and examine energy consumed in each process.

With a yearly average growth of 8% in the textile export, energy demand of this sector is exponentially rising. The historical trend analyzed in the feasibility study identified that the total industrial energy consumption (IEC) of RMG industry has been increasing at an annual rate of about 7%. This is mainly due to expansion and modernization of the textile sector. In such case improved energy efficiency will play a critical role, along with sustainable energy, thereby minimizing the anthropogenic greenhouse gas (GHG) emission by the industry as a major energy user.

### Snapshot of RMG Industry in Bangladesh

The roots of the RMG sector in Bangladesh, lies in late 1970s when the success of garment manufacturers in catering to export market. The sector gradually became fragmented and formed into industrialization of the country. In 1983, the Bangladesh Export Promotion Council (BEPAC), one of the earliest trade associations in the country, in 1983 helped in structuring the sector and increase its contribution to the country's economy, as well as ensure all legitimate rights and privileges of the garment workers leading to the formation of Garment Workers Union.

The sector, which comprises over 4500 factories, has been a leading contributor to the economy, accounting for 11% of the country’s GDP in 2017-18[1]. It is the largest exporting industry in Bangladesh and has experienced an exponential growth. The contribution in total export increased from nearly 4% in 1983-84 to 84% in 2017-18, amounting to USD 30.62 billion. The sector has witnessed a growth of nearly 7.3% CAGR in last 5 years i.e., 2013 to 2018.

While the sector continues in its growth trajectory, there are a number of hurdles in the horizon which need to be overcome to reach USD 50 million export by 2031 set by the national body for garments manufacturers in Bangladesh - Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA). These impediments include rising energy costs, increasing worker wages, low labor productivity, graduation from LDC status, and increased competition.

The sector plays a key role in employment generation and in the provision of income to the poor – it employs nearly 4.4 million workers[1]. The sector has also played a significant role in the socio-economic development of the country with women comprising nearly 80% of the total workforce in the sector[2]. It also plays a pivotal role in the export economy, as more than 95% of the garments manufactured in Bangladesh are exported. Printing, pressing, printing, labels are manufactured in Bangladesh.[3] This had helped open up employment opportunities through indirect economic activities, thereby contributing to the country’s overall socio-economic development, women empowerment and poverty alleviation.

# RMG Sector Energy Baseline

The garments sector combined accounts for about 15% of total energy consumption in Industry sector of Bangladesh i.e. 2107kTOE/y[3] which translates to 7.5% of the country's total emissions i.e. 6.7 MCO2e approximately. The energy is consumed in a set of production processes which include fabric production (carding, combing, spinning), yarn production (dyeing, mercerizing), weaving (warp knitting, weft laying, cutting, sewing, wet processing, spot cleaning/laundry, fusing, pressing and packaging). Based on the end products, the combination of processes may vary for specific units. In order to understand the energy requirement in each process, a survey was conducted among RMG factories in Bangladesh. The results based on the information generated from the survey, the share of energy consumed in RMG across type of process is given below.

| Figure 08: Energy Share Across RMG Processes | Figure 09: Energy Mix Across RMG Sector |
| --- | --- |
| ![Process Control](#) | ![Electricity 15% Diesel 11% Natural gas 7%] |

The energy consumption in the sector is skewed towards use of natural gas, with grid electricity having a minor share. Diesel is mostly used to generate electricity as a back-up and its use depends on non-availability of electricity or when there are power cuts. In small units, diesel is used for generators to fulfill power needs. In large units, there are captive natural-gas power plants that supply electricity. While natural gas is used in its primary form as boiler fuel, diesel is almost exclusively used for backup (in most cases as standby power).

## Table 03: Brief Description of RMG Unit Processes

| Operation | Process description | Method |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fabric production | • Weaving: Involves interlacing two or more yarn systems crossing each other vertically. Woven textiles are used in all textile sections (apparel, home textiles, and automotive). On a loom, the warp yarns (lengthwise) run parallel to the machine while the weft yarn is inserted into the lengthwise-oriented warp yarns (sheds).<br>• Knitting: In knitting, fabric is formed by interlocking or intermeshing loops of one or more sets of yarns. Knitting is performed using one of two processes; with a single needle or on several different types of machines.<br>• Nonwovens: Fabrics made of fibers without weaving or knitting are called nonwovens. Nonwovens are essentially sheet or web structures formed by bonding together loose fibers, filaments, or filaments using mechanical, thermal, chemical bond, or solvent means. | Automatic/manual |
| Design/sketch | • Pattern manufacturing involves designing the product containing measurement defining a particular style. | Automatic/manual |
| Pattern design | • Sample pattern is designed based out of the style concept developed from design/sketch.<br>• Pattern provides the design template on the basis of which the sample garment is produced.| Automatic/manual |
| Sample | • The sample garment is produced from the first pattern to analyze pattern fit and design by a panel of designers, pattern makers and sewing specialists. <br>• Any modifications, if required, are incorporated in the pattern. | Manual |
| Production Pattern | • Pattern design is used for creating production patterns for mass production. | Automatic/manual |
| Grading | • Patterns are created based on all (S, M, L, XL or size 10, 12, 14, 16) or else there are standard patterns of and so on for different figure and sizes. | Automatic/manual |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 17 OF 155

## Fabric Relaxing
- "Relaxing" refers to the process that allows the material to relax and contract prior to being manufactured.
- This process allows fabric to shrink so that further shrinkage during customer use is minimized.

### Marking
- Marker making is the process to sketch on fabric.
- Markers are laid in such a way that minimum possible fabric gets wasted during cutting operation

### Spreading, Laying and Cutting
- After successfully spreading the fabric and putting all the markers on it according to the marker table is cut accordingly.

## Sorting/Bundling
- Bundling of pattern shaped cloth according to size and design.
- Requires much precision since having bundles of different sizes and colors can be confusing.

## Sewing
- Stitching/sewing undertaken after cut pieces are bundled according to size, color and quantities required for the final product.

## Wet Processing
- Includes de-sizing, scouring, bleaching, dyeing, printing and finishing etc. that involve adding colors to fabric.
- Performed along with a variety of finishing steps that provide certain desired characteristics to the end product.

## Quality Inspection
- Complete quality checking of product to assess whether it is compliant with quality requirements.

## Spot Cleaning and Laundry
- Cleaned by removing any dirt, oil or other stains on the sticker and taken to a spot-cleaning area where the garment is cleaned using steam, hot water, or chemical stain removal operations.
- Based on client requirements, some products require to be fully laundered in on-site laundry or with an off-site green-certified off-site laundry operations.
- Common laundry facilities are equipped with at least two types of machines: washers, spinners and dryers. Some facilities also have the capability to perform special treatments, such as stone-or acid-washing.

### Automatic/manual

# Fusing and pressing

- Finished product released by quality control goes for final pressing.
- The ironing station consists of an iron and an ironing table with steam applied to it.
- In most facilities, the ironing platforms are equipped with a ventilation system that draws steam through the fabric as it is being pressed.

## Folding and packing

- The finished garments are then folded in a specific dimension, sometimes using a template.
- Garments may be packed into boxes or other kind of tags are attached to garment after folding.
- Different types of packing accessories are used to keep the garment in a desired shape.

sustained growth in the sector. While onset of COVID-19 will definitely impede growth in near future, demand in the medium and long term is expected to increase and the sector will continue its growth trajectory.

Given such a situation, GHG emissions will also increase with increased production levels. Given current level of penetration of energy efficient equipment, which is negligible, it is expected that emissions growth will follow the same pattern as the growth in production. Hence, a greater focus on increasing the five-year rolling penetration of energy efficient equipment would be a key method of stemming the growth of emissions for the sector.

Financial Market baseline for EEAC:

There are several loan facilities for energy efficiency and conservation promotion, however higher lending interest rate and the time required for appraisal have hampered smooth implementation of loan projects. In this context, both minimizing the cost of loan procedure through simplification of loan application and speedily approving the disbursement of loans with a greater focus on low interest rate and loan scheme will promote energy efficiency and conservation in Bangladesh further.

In Bangladesh progress with industrial energy efficiency has been slow. This situation is expected to prevail with the current market and technical challenges. Hence, interventions in the proposed program aims to increase the adoption of energy efficient machinery in the textile and RMG sectors, which has 25%-30% of energy saving potential.

Considering the high cost of changing machinery process optimization is more preferable to the concerned stakeholders. However, the replacement of new machinery can be financially justifiable via low interest loan so that total payment amount for energy efficient equipment (equipment cost and financial cost) break even within a short period.

The programme will result in a real and visible paradigm shift in the textile and RMG sector of Bangladesh and advance it towards low-carbon sustainable development by limiting the country's GHG emissions, as specifically recommended in the Nationally Determined Contributions 2015, the Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCSAP) and the National Energy Efficiency Action Plan (NEEAP) under the Paris Plan 2030. The programme is expected to result in direct emission reductions of 0.727 tCO2e annually by focusing on a scaling up investment in energy efficiency improvement. However, gradually the annual GHG emissions amount will increase as the energy mix will not contain greater share of coal and oil, as domestic natural gas production is declining.

# COVID-19 impact on the Textile and RMG industry:

As of 15th May 2020, Bangladesh has reported 20,065 confirmed cases and 298 deaths following the identification of the first imported covid-19 case on March 8th.

On March 23rd, the government declared a general holiday from March 29th to April 4th, which was extended several times. On March 27th, all non-essential businesses were closed; commercial banks are allowed to operate shorter hours. Individuals have been requested not to leave their homes except to collect daily necessities and emergency supplies, and to wear masks when outside. On April 10, The Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA) and the Bangladesh Bureau of Imports and Exports Association (BIMEA) announced in a joint statement that the readymade garment (RMG) factories would resume operations from May 24th.

Directives by the Bangladesh government stated that textile & garment factories, which supply some of the biggest brands in the world and produce 84% of the country's total exports, would be allowed to resume operations after April 25th. After providing facilities complies with health guidelines and maintain physical distancing.

On 13 May, it was reported that the government is likely to extend the shutdown of most private and public workplaces until 30 May.


## Impact:

By far the economic impact of the health crisis, the two main channels through which the Bangladesh economy will be impacted are remittances and exports of textile & apparels. Remittances represent over 5 percent of GDP ($16.4 billion in FY 19), and a majority of migrant workers are based in Gulf countries that are currently under lockdowns. Exports of textiles & apparels account for about 20% of the country's total export, or nearly one-third of the country’s exports. As Bangladesh, the second-largest apparel producer after China, is set to lose orders worth $1 billion due to the pandemic, it will be a major blow to the economy.

The largest brands and retailers due to CoVid-19 outbreak, shared by Bangladesh Knitwear Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BKMEA) and the President of BKMEA. The impact on the RMG sector is likely to be the consolidation or postponement of several billion US dollars in orders from major retailers in importing countries, though top global apparel brands announced on April 1st that they would receive shipments of garments from Bangladesh. However, the pandemic has already caused a significant disruption in the garment sector, risks creating unemployment and deepen poverty. The urban poor will be hardest hit as their livelihoods are directly dependent upon RMG factories. The extent of the impact of the pandemic will depend on the duration of the crisis and the mitigation measures taken.

There have been 3 distinct phases, which charts the impact of COVID in textile & particularly RMG sector of Bangladesh. A sample study of 316 RMG units by Penn State Center for Global Workers’ Rights (CGWR) provides a detailed account of how the COVID situation has shaped the key events for each of these phases.

Details are discussed below:

### Phase one - Material supply

With the lockdown in China, the single largest source of raw materials (Chinese fabric import accounts for 40% of total imports for RMG sector**), RMG units reported delays in raw material shipment or significant increase in raw material prices. This set in to motion the first phase of the pandemic, which lasted from March 25th to April 19th.

The second phase began on April 20th and lasted until May 3rd. During this period, most RMG units received payment by buyers. Almost all RMG units did not receive any extra payments from buyers on account of increased raw material prices.


---
**Page 25**
---

2. Phase three – Delayed payments: In the second phase, global incidence of lockdowns affecting the bottom of buyers, resulting in delay to payments to RMG units. RMG units were experiencing varying levels of delay, relative to contractually stipulated terms. In most cases, the delay was within 30 days.

Phase three – Order cancellation: Further aggravation of market demand due to continued lockdown, led to cancellation of orders by buyers. While there have been reported variations in cases, mostly RMG units operating inside India have refused to pay for raw materials or protection costs incurred by RMG units.


### Supportive measures:

As is evident from the discussions above, the COVID pandemic has adversely impacted both demand and supply side of the textile and RMG sector in Bangladesh thereby resulting in cash crunch. National Government has already announced a stimulus package worth Tk 10 billion to help the sector. The latter expects to deploy USD 160 million over the next 18 months to help countries protect the poor and vulnerable from the impact of COVID-19.

While traditional markets are falling, demand for protective personal equipment has surged and prices of PPE products have risen dramatically since the beginning of the COVID-19 outbreak: a six-fold increase for N95 masks. This is due to the fact that the production capacity in Bangladesh is currently bottleneck in the market with backlog of 4–6 months for supply orders only. This provides an opportunity for local producers to diversify their product range and enter into new markets. The demand for personal protective equipment (PPE) is expected to continue in the short to medium term i.e. 1-3 years, given the state of pandemic. Such product diversification can help RMG units to tide over the pandemic in short term.

However, product diversification will also require capital investment; production of specialized equipment requires a different set up and technology. The RMG sector is currently facing cash crunch due to the current scenario, making such adjustments in scale would require support. While Bangladesh government has already announced stimulus package worth Tk 10 billion to help the sector, it may not be sufficient to bridge this demand gap.

### Concluding remarks:

IDCOL is seeking concessional finance from Green Climate Fund (GCF) to promote energy efficiency in the textile and RMG sector of Bangladesh. Traditionally, funding from GCF has targeted such projects that can deliver immediate impact on climate change. However, the current scenario presents a unique opportunity for GCF to extend this mandate and bundle an additional supporting financing instrument, i.e. grant, with their traditional loan to help beneficiaries to tide over the immediate aftermath of COVID. The RMG sector is currently facing cash crunch due to the current scenario, making such adjustments in scale would require capital investment; production of specialized equipment requires a different set up and technology. IDCOL has already identified RMG units that are interested in energy efficiency technology, GCF can help promote business recovery in RMG factories and help them enter into new markets.

Though the impact of COVID-19 is being felt in the short run, the growth is expected to recover over the medium term. Textile and RMG industry, the main backbone of the country, is expected to overcome this crisis as a result of various measures taken by the government and support from international developmental organizations.

### Energy Efficiency and Conservation Promotion Financing Schemes in Bangladesh

* **Energy Efficiency Finance Program (IEEEP):** Among the loan facility for EEAC promotion focused on industry sector in Bangladesh, Global Climate Partnership Fund (GCPF) was one of the earliest funds. GCPF which was initiated in 2011 is the loan facility aiming to extend loans to projects owners for buildings and plants improving energy efficiency by more than 20% compared with the baseline. The fund has been used for financing solar water heating, solar lighting, wind power generation and biomass power generation. However, provision of GCPF loan has been limited with just one (1) project which was completed in 2013. In 2014, the IEEFP of 2nd phase was launched as a follow-up to the first phase. The fund is now closed for new user was raised. As is shown in Table-5, lending rate in BDT denomination was set as about 12%, which is higher than long-term rate for interest on loans from commercial banks. Secondly, the loan facility of USD 30 million is limited. Thirdly, condition of the loan which needs to meet more than 20% conservation of energy is a big hurdle.

* **Energy Efficiency Finance Program (IEEEP):** Asian Development Bank (ADB) initiated Industry Energy Efficiency Finance Program (IEEEP) in 2011. IEEFP aims at promoting energy efficiency and conservation equipment while GCPF extended loan for not only energy efficiency and conservation equipment but renewable energy equipment. The team found out that IEEFP has been used for financing of technical assistance and loan facility of USD 30 million. However, it is informed that no loan has been extended and ADB canceled most of the fund.


### Partnership for Clean Textile (PaCT):

* **PaCT:** PaCT is being implemented as “Partnership for Clean Textile” (PaCT). PaCT is playing a leading role in driving the long-term competitiveness and environmental sustainability of the textile wet processing sector, by addressing water pollution and energy efficiency issues through the adoption of best practices. These best practices lead to declining resource consumption, saving profits, and an enhanced image in the eyes of customers and investors.

Outline of PaCT project is as follows: Over the years, IFC has drawn on its global and local experience and expertise to support the textile sector in Bangladesh. The total value of IFC’s committed portfolio in Bangladesh is around USD 1.52 billion (IFC invested USD 800 million in 2017 and USD 720 million in 2018).

* **IFC:** IFC is promoting sustainable growth and private sector development in Bangladesh through investing in critical infrastructure, boosting financial inclusion, enhancing textiles competitiveness and supporting reforms to make doing business easier for private sector.


---
**Page 27**
---

However, some of the key points to be noted for the PaCT programme are as follows:

* **PaCT or Resource Efficiency Financing and not a dedicated energy efficiency financing:** Through PaCT, IFC is playing the role of investment facilitator. It doesn’t have dedicated credit lines but rather provides technical assistance to promote resource efficiency in Industries.
* **Banks and financial intermediaries (FIs) are involved in the process; they have partner bank who reviews the project and then submit it to IFC for review:** The IFC team will review the projects and select resource efficiency projects. After the review, the bank lends customized finance to factories to invest in the selected projects.
* **PaCT advocacy helped create a USD 200 million Green Transformation Fund that the Bangladeshi government uses to support low-cost financing for resource efficiency in the textile industry which is not successful primarily because of the cumbersome disbursement process.**

### Bangladesh Bank (Central Bank) Funds:

**Green Transformation Fund (GTF):**
The Green Transformation Fund was established by Bangladesh Bank in 2016. The size of fund is USD 200 million and is intended to facilitate access to financing for export-oriented textile & garment products and technology, including equipment and machinery they import. These are environment-friendly attributes such as energy efficiency, renewable and so on.

* **The initial lending rate to IFI was set at 3 Month Libor + 2.25% which was lowered to 3 Month Libor +1% from August of 2017:** The team estimates lending rate to end-users from IFI will reach 6-8%. So far only an estimate has been made and no loan has been disbursed.
* **Following issues are pointed out from financial institution on GTF:**
    * (i) Since the loan facility is USD denominated and Libor-based floating rate loan, borrowers will be affected by change of exchange rate and interest rate;
    * (ii) The loan period is 10 years which is longer than what they can afford;
    * (iii) About half year to 1 year is spent for loan decision; and
    * (iv) Completion of plant and equipment is the condition of loan disbursement.

### Bangladesh Bank Re-financing Scheme

The central bank launched the BDT 2 billion green banking reference scheme in 2009 that provides loans for projects under the following sectors: Solar power, wind energy, biogas and efficient treatment plant sector (waste water treatment plant), Pet bottle recycling plants, vermicomposting, hydro power. Solar Battery recycling plants, LED bulb manufacturing plants, setting up of hybrid Hoffman kiln in brick manufacturing units under the scheme. The interest rate is 6-9% for projects under the green banking reference scheme and 5-7% for other energy, biogas and efficient treatment plant sector reference scheme. More sectors have been brought under the green banking reference scheme to build a more environment-friendly economy. Under the scheme, the central bank provides financial institutions with 100% interest rate subsidy provided they meet the schemes at 8 - 9 % interest. The cumulative amount refinanced under the scheme up to June 2018 stood at BDT 374.57 million. In FY18, total disbursement under the BB’s refinancing increased by 91 percent to BDT 664.57 million which was BDT 246.80 million in FY17.

Long Term Financing Facility (LTFF) under Financial Sector Support Project (FSSP) 33

BB intends to provide long term financing for private sector firms, mainly mitigate manufacturing firms under the Financial Sector Support Project (FSSP) financed by the International Development Association (IDA).

The main objective of this facility is to provide long term funding for private sector firms. The financing facility will be provided in US dollars and will be used to finance projects that contribute to sustainable and environmentally sustainable development outcomes.

Estimated total fund of LTFF is USD 292.50 million (BDT 18,734,600,000) with a maximum loan amount USD 50.30 million (BDT 30,222.50 million). The combined US dollar and BDT fund is USD 254.00 million (BDT 199,390,000) in FY18. The facility will be provided for a term of 3 to 10 years in US dollar and provided to the FPs authorized by BB to deal in foreign exchange on lending/refinancing to the private sector firms.

Joint Crediting Mechanism (JCM) [5]

The mechanism was introduced, bringing about of bilateral document between Bangladesh and Japan in March 2013. This JCM facilitates diffusion of leading low carbon technologies, products, systems, services, and infrastructure as well as implementation of mitigation actions, and contributing to sustainable development outcomes. The JCM is a voluntary arrangement that allows for the inclusion of emission reductions or removals from Japan in a quantitative manner, by applying measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) methodologies, and uses them to achieve Japan's emission reduction target.

Under this scheme, Japanese and Bangladesh participants work in close co-operation to facilitate financial flows to projects. The JCM is based on the principle of project-by-project approach for reduction of GHG emissions. Under this mechanism, 3 projects have been approved out of which 2 projects are related to energy efficiency i.e. installation of high efficiency loom at weaving factory and high-efficiency centrifugal chiller for AC system in clothing bag factory.

### Resource Efficiency Financing

The partner banks review the investment proposals, evaluate the costs and benefits of implementing the energy efficiency projects. After approval, the bank lend customized finance to support the investment in the resource efficiency projects and also provides linkage with relevant technology providers.

Note: IFC PACT advisory helped create a USD 104 million Green Transformation Fund for the Bangladesh government case to support low-cost financing for energy efficiency in the textile sector.

IFC's Global Trade Supplier Finance (GTSF) programme also offers suppliers financial incentives to improve finance access and working capital management. The GTSF programme provides suppliers with tools that helps suppliers manage working capital, convert sales receivables to immediate cash, and access low-cost financing.

As mentioned above, although there are several loan facilities for energy efficiency and conservation promotion, high lending interest rate and the time required for appraisal have hampered smooth implementation of projects.

Demand for ICA EAEPC fund remains high due to its attractiveness in lower lending rates and fast interest scheme (4-6%) and simplified loan application procedure. However, JICA is not sector focused and only covers a small part of spinning in textiles and during JICA outreach it was observed that there are many interested textile units which are not covered by JICA. Hence, the GCF fund can be considered as a dedicated line for a dedicated line for textile.

GCF Fund is targeted for overall Textile & RMG Sectors in Bangladesh which will include spinning, weaving, knitting, dyeing, cutting & sewing, wet processing, cleaning & laundry, finishing, pressing and folding & packaging, which have larger energy saving potential. In this context, fund from GCF, being a dedicated line for textile sector can be considered as an attractive option to the units for speedy appraisal as well as development of loan proceeds having concessional low interest rate and loan scheme will have catalytic effect and promote energy efficiency and conservation in Bangladesh textile & RMG sectors.

GCF fund will have catalytic impact on energy efficiency market for Textile & RMG sectors so as a dedicated fund is currently catering to energy efficiency of Textile as a whole. Further, most of the

Additionally for GCF Fund
1. The engagement is relevant because it addresses the key challenge for Bangladesh - climate change mitigation.
2. The scope of the programme is in alignment with the SDG targets – SGD 7, SGD 9 and SGD 13.
3. Sustainable energy use remains of critical importance to the Bangladeshi economy. Availability and the rising price of gas is a significant cost driver and constraint for the growth of textile and RMG industries.

**of textile & RMG factories (by relaxing the major market constraints), to establish the textile as a business as usual practice so that it can be replicated in other industrial sector.**

The loan product will be directed to increase energy efficiency across complete value chain in Textiles and RMG sectors. The focus of this proposal is on financing projects which will improve only energy efficiency in complete value chain of both the industries (Different from Partnership for cleaner textile (Pact) led by UNIDO). The focus area is investment facilitation for financing resource efficiency projects in textile factories).

The loan product has the following features which make it highly attractive in the market:

- Simplified loan application process and streamlined procedures, minimizing the time for loan processing
- Availability of grace period & Long Tenure and low interest.

In addition, the broad experience of the Executing Entities (DDCOL and LFs) is in financing industrial energy efficiency projects. The executing entities have built their capacity by successfully implementing EE&C funds - Strong pipeline build by DD COLs and the LFs for Textile and RMG sector while implementing EE&C fund which uses ensured fast disbursement of GCF fund.

**Major Gaps and Barriers identified in the previous & existing EE&C credit lines and how it will be addressed in the proposed programme**

As mentioned above, although there are several loan facilities for energy efficiency and conservation projects, these have not been able to achieve their full potential due to various reasons such as delays with implementation of loan projects. In this context, both minimizing the time for loan procedure through simplification of loan application and speedy appraisal and approval of loan having concessional low interest rate will be helpful in promoting energy efficiency and conservation in Bangladesh further.

The analysis of the alternative financing indicates a number of issues required to be addressed being successful:

1. Concessional interest rate in comparison to commercial rate
2. Due higher initial investment requirement for energy efficient equipment, users may be reluctant to purchase it compared to conventional equipment. As such, sensitizing the purchase of energy efficient equipment through concessional lending would be required.
3. Flexible tenure & grace period in line with project viability
4. Some available with significant grace period (1-2 years) and longer tenures which enhances that repayment capacity but cause cash-flow problems. Easy access of funds through experienced financial intermediary of the energy efficient projects have longer grace period which can only be valid if financing is due.
5. Due diligence compatible with financing energy efficient
6. While it is important that credit worthy borrowers are selected under any programme, it is also important to ensure that the approval process is streamlined and doesn't lead to significant delays. Some of the alternative financing programme involve multiple agencies in the due diligence and approval process which could delay.
7. Proper management of currency fluctuation if repaid in foreign currency

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WINDING PROPOSAL (2.0) PAGE 31 OF 155

## Complex technical requirements for processing loans:
In order to access loans, detailed technical documents need to be prepared which provide the financial justification for undertaking the project and helps in securing the loan. The preparation of these documents involve hiring of energy auditors by the RMUs units, which further adds to the cost component and also increases the technical complexity of applying for loans for the unit owners.

In some cases, eligibility for loans includes presence of company policy documents like Environment and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) in the private sector. This reduces the financing options available to an RMU unit to fund its energy efficiency measure.

## Limited number of banks providing energy efficiency financing:
Under existing market scenario, there is limited number of banks in Bangladesh providing energy efficiency financing. Also, large scale units have limited access to finance for their energy efficiency measures as they are not eligible for RMUs units. This further limits the option available to unit owners to finance their energy efficiency measures.

## Limited capacity in bank in appraising energy efficiency projects:
Like RMU unit owners, EFIs have limited technical knowledge in assessing an energy efficiency project and are completely dependent on energy auditor for assessment. Also, there is limited market information available for the banking staff to effectively assess the viability of such projects. This has resulted in banks not being able to provide adequate financing support to these units. This limited understanding is also contributing to lack of active interest in financing energy efficiency.

Hence, it is observed that limited penetration of energy efficiency measures can be effectively overcome by providing access to concessional financing sources at the same time providing access to technical and market information. The TA programme has identified this as a critical gap and hence included the financing requirement for the sector. The TA component sought under the programme will support in reviewing all existing lending instruments, including those of RMUs units, to identify gaps in terms of availability of energy consumption in textile industries and their access to financing and identify financial gaps for realization of the planned EE programmes/activities. This will help in understanding the present requirements of the sector and also provide a framework for future financing (including the credit line), human resources need, indicative timeline for establishing the credit line, structure for raising capital or allocation of state budget to establish and maintain the credit line, structure for the utilization of funds, etc.

The TA component will also support in developing a financial gap analysis and a sector-level road map along with time-based action plan will be developed, which will be subject to annual review. The effectiveness of the financing mechanism will be monitored on an ongoing basis. The financial gap rating for reaching the EE targets and physical impact (energy and GHG savings) will be assessed annually and based on the assessment (includes stakeholder consultation) the financing strategy will be revised.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 33 OF 155

Bangladesh has been consistently witnessing high economic growth (over 6% GDP growth for the last decade). This has led to nearly 49% increase in its final energy consumption between 2008 and 2018. With the country expected to continue in its high growth trajectory, the corresponding energy consumption is also projected to rise at a rate of about 5%. The increasing demand for electricity coupled with the government's generous subsidies in energy supply. The volume has been rising steadily over the past years – national subsidy bill for power generation and energy increased from USD $10 million in 2016-17 to USD 1,983 million in 2018-19. This is expected to further increase to USD $4 billion in 2019-20. The increasing energy subsidy bill can have a crippling effect of Bangladesh’s economy. Efficient use of energy and reduction in energy consumption will not only help to check the investment for overall economic development of Bangladesh and effectively reducing GHG emissions.

Within Bangladesh, Garments and Textiles sector is the highest energy consuming sector within industries which consumes 25% of total electricity generation. The Garment industry has a huge potential for energy savings with potential of nearly 25% in the sectors, as compared to international best practices. However, there are multiple barriers to ensure benefits are accrued from implementing energy saving measures.

As gathered through stakeholder interviews, the unit owners are generally skeptical of the energy savings that can be achieved from implementing energy efficient equipment which priced significantly higher over normal inefficient variant. The lack of adequate number of demonstration projects for the sector is considered as the major barrier in this regard. The interest rate on loans provided by Bangladesh Bank is currently fixed in the range 11%-14% - under such high interest rates, financing equipment for which lower priced variants are available is unavailable for unit owners. The appreciation of cost savings arising from lower energy consumption and the associated financial benefits is also not well understood among unit owners. Limited knowledge about the unit owners on how to assess the energy saving and the financial benefits are also a major barrier in this regard. Similarly, lack of information regarding financing energy saving equipment and other information like pricing, which can help assist decision making.

Apart from unit owners, the financing institutions also have limited capacity to assess and appraise an energy efficiency project, leading to lower to no preference of banks to fund energy efficiency projects as compared or any other sector. The Bangladesh Green Climate Fund (GCF) of Bangladesh Bank is the only financial line for energy efficiency through Green Climate Fund (GCF) of Bangladesh Bank. However, the current GCF financing mechanism provides lead to disbursement of loans, delayed by months at a time.

The introduction of low-cost financing coupled with technical assistance activities can help in overcoming these barriers and will also provide an opportunity to reduce emissions from industries. Thus in the above shown diagram of Theory of Change we can assume that - • given the correct support the local financial institutions (LFIs) and the textile & RMG industries are willing to invest their own capacity to address climate change through adoption of energy efficient equipment and technology.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL (V2) PAGE 34 OF 155

## B

### Major barriers that are identified can be classified into three segments: 
- **a) Institutional capacity:** This includes complicated and lengthy loan approval process related to concessional green financing, lack of capacity of the financial institutions to appraise and monitor concessionally financed projects, and lack of awareness among policy makers regarding the benefits of energy efficient equipment.
- **b) Financing barrier:** This is lack of availability of concessional financing in the market that can widely cover the preadditional costs of energy efficient equipment. 
- **c) Policy barrier:** This includes lack of capacity to develop a comprehensive policy, lack of awareness among policy makers regarding the benefits of energy efficiency, limited appreciation for economic benefit of energy efficiency, and lack of clarity on the role of policy-level push versus EE penetration.

### In order to overview these barriers, the programme will enable access to low-cost financing for the program pilot with effective disbursement process which will help drive demand for energy efficient equipment and create demonstrative effect to develop market trust in energy efficiency. 
- **In order to facilitate effective appraisal of energy efficiency projects, monitoring and monitoring and building of banks’ disbursing capacity:** This includes training on the loan application process and capacity development in understanding the loan application process.
- **Handholding activity will include assessment of the key issues in existing loan disbursement processes and preparation of recommendations for the modification of disbursement process of the low-cost loan instrument under this programme.**

### In order to access information related to energy efficiency for unit owners, a digital platform is envisaged which will contain case studies, guides, manuals of successful energy efficient equipment, and other relevant information.
- **The platform will also provide a sectoral baseline energy consumption database which coupled with the data on the number of units in each sector will help stakeholders understand level of energy efficiency among the programme, and hope to understand sector level impact of energy consumption in Bangladesh.**

### Apart from virtual conversations, real-life interaction between unit owners and OEMs will be stimulated through technology fair wherein OEMs will exhibit their energy efficient technologies and will clarify any queries related to the same. This will also help in promoting energy efficiency among units (particularly unit owners) on the benefit of energy efficiency and the various policy levers utilized globally for approval of policy makers of Bangladesh is planned to improve awareness of benefits of energy efficiency.

### In order to stimulate policy level push, manuals and other supporting documents will be prepared to access relevant information by policy makers in developing appropriately designed policies/schemes aimed at incentivizing energy efficient equipment. The programme will also aim to bring in the paradigm shift in Bangladesh energy efficiency market and create a replicable model that can be emulated in other sectors of the country to ensure increased penetration of energy efficiency.

Since only financing will be list based, it will be easier to apprise the project. The eligibility would be supported by certificate/catalogue/ brochure report from energy auditor or declaration by the supplier. The transactional cost of the parties involved would decrease significantly.

### IDCOL as an Accredited Entity (AE)

IDCOL is a Bangladesh-based financial institution that has been working on reducing energy inefficiencies in the textile sector. The loan appraisal process for GCF credit line will take significantly less time compared to any other fund. This is because IDCOL already has a pre-approved internal structure and has undergone an internal approval process of IDCOL. In the case of credit lines where third parties are involved (like central regulatory body SREDA, in case of JICA Fund) or where intermediary financing institutes are involved (like Bangladesh Development Bank), it will take more time (3 month to 1 year) gets wasted in loan appraisal process which will be saved in this credit line.

## Figure 12: Fund flow from GCF to the end borrowers (reflecting the interest rate and tenor)

This would have a catalytic impact on energy efficiency measures in the textile sector. Component 01 & 02 envisages the creation of concessional fund with USD 133 million, including USD 100.00 million from GCF and USD 33.00 million from other sources. The project will be implemented over a period of five years. Of this amount, USD 33.00 million will be the leveraging financing. IDCOL, an Accredited Entity with GCF, will make available the concessional fund to end borrowers at interest rates ranging between 4% and 6%. In case of textile manufacturing companies, in this project, the projected EIR estimated to be achieved is 7.0 %. The implementation of these projects would result in a reduction of energy consumption by 12.5 GWh per year. This will lead to an emission reduction of 348,000 kg CO2 and 4.00 million kg CO2 equivalent over the lifetime of the project (20 years) which if included in the economic benefit would lead to an EIRR of 23%. The payback period for various equipment varies between 5-10 years. These projects will have a positive impact on the environment, economy and society by reducing social and gender impacts such as better community living standards, providing safe and better working environments, etc.

As such, the envisaged program is closely aligned to the GCF objective of catalyzing financing for this initiative and helping scale up the initiative across the country in future through increased showcasing of concessional finance. The expected impact of the project will be an increase in energy efficiency which enabled through this program will help improve the productivity & competitiveness of the local textile industry in Bangladesh thereby leading to sustainable development pathways. The investments would also create a

#### B

climate response built environment through deep penetration of energy saving technologies, equipment and appliances in the textile industries.

The proposed programme will address the energy efficiency adoption needs of textile sector with following components:

- **Component 1:** USD 133.0 million financing for Energy Saving Equipment & Technology for Textile sector
- **Component 2:** USD 5.05 million GCF Technical Assistance (TA) to develop enabling environment for EE investment in textile sector

**Component 1: USD 133.0 million financing for Energy Saving Equipment & Technology for Textile sector**

To encourage the industry and businesses to choose energy-efficient equipment which, in many of the cases, is more expensive compared to conventional ones, there is a need to reduce the cost difference by providing concessional lending for energy efficient equipment.

Although energy-efficient equipment may become economical on the capital cost basis, equipment buyers are most likely to opt for cheaper conventional equipment due to the higher cost of choosing energy-efficient equipment. Against this background, the Programme aims to provide a low interest loan to the buyers so that total payment amount for energy efficient equipment (equipment cost and financial cost) will break even with the cost of acquiring conventional equipment.

During the feasibility study, based on the present value simulations for a few types of equipment it was identified that there is a need for 25% to 36% interest rate in order to break-even the cost of EE equipment with that of cheaper traditional equipment. For example, market price of energy efficient and traditional spinning machine were USD 108,947 and USD 100,000 respectively. The interest rates for spinning and boiler machine were found to be 25% and 26% respectively higher than the traditional/conventional equipment. Subsequently, the cost impact of purchasing conventional equipment and energy efficient equipment by changing tenure and grace period have been analyzed.

The purchase of an energy efficient spinner machine is expected to be 10-25% more expensive compared with conventional type which requires a 5.53% interest rate (considering 8 year loan tenure and 1 year grace period) for the total payment to become par with that of a conventional one. Similarly, a 27% more expensive price through boiler requires a 6.28% interest rate (considering 8 year loan tenure and 1 year grace period) to be even with the conventional type in terms of total payment amount.

The above analysis clearly demonstrates that to ensure the feasibility of the project, concessional lending is required from GCF to IDCOL to enable IDCOL to extend loan at the low interest to the end borrower. For this reason, the proposed financial package seems to be appropriate and reasonable as described in section D.6.

However, one thing to be noted is that the credit provided by GCF to IDCOL will be in the form of USD while IDCOL will be lending 60% of the amount to the end users in BDT and rest the 40% in USD. As such, IDCOL will have to manage the currency risk as well in this case which is not the case for other similar credit lines.

In time series analysis done for exchange rate movement of Bangladesh Taka against USD from 1998 to 2016, it has been observed that the BDT/USD exchange rate has shown a downward trend. Hence IDCOL will be managing the currency risks by charging risk premium to the end borrowers. The feasibility study also indicates that some market exists for USD loans amongst the textile industries, so IDCOL plans to target this segment of the market. However, it is important to note that in case such loans are not exposed to currency fluctuation risk, they can be priced lower than BDT loans.

- Having Energy Efficient component of proposed equipment to be financed

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GCF TECNICAL ASSISTANCE (TA) TO DEVELOP ENABLING ENVIRONMENT FOR EE INVESTMENT IN TEXTILE SECTOR

## Component 2: USD 30.5M GCF Technical Assistance (TA) to develop enabling environment for EE investment in textile sector

### GCF non-reimbursable resources (technical assistance) will address real or perceived risks and barriers to large-scale adoption of EE investments in the textile sector. IDCOL requires the service of a consultancy/consulting firm to perform technical assistance activities.

Key output areas under TA will be:
- **Activity 2.1.1:** Develop a financing roadmap and time-based action plan
- **Activity 2.1.2:** Develop operational manual for the program.
- **Activity 2.1.3:** Develop an investment policy for the project.
- **Activity 2.1.4:** Classify the Textile use sector by scale and type of products and data collection through energy audit of sample plants
- **Activity 2.1.5:** Consolidation of data and developing sector-level baseline
- **Activity 2.1.6:** Interact with stakeholders/market players to prepare a list of equipment already implemented in the textile use sector, based on known energy savings results.
- **Activity 2.1.7:** Review of baseline by conducting energy audit and data consolidation
- **Activity 2.1.8:** Develop technically robust, bankable EE project pipeline

### Output 2.5: Summary support to the Textile Borrowers to shorten appraisal timeline

#### Activity 2.5.1: Support the end borrowers, eligible under Component 01, in fulfilling the documentation process, completing due diligence during appraisal, checking eligibility and making disbursement process faster.

This will be done through requirement standardization, capacity building of borrowers, facilitating certification from equipment suppliers and empowerment of the vendors all of which will significantly cut down on documentation and disbursement time.
- Create standardized document requirements for loan application under the credit line. The standardized document formats and simplified approval process (internal to IDCOL) will help borrowers cut down on application time.
- The TA component will hold workshops to strengthen borrowers on the application process and the documentation requirements, which will reduce levels of incomplete applications.
- Work with the equipment suppliers to facilitate certification and other documentation requirements, so the equipment impact can be clearly mapped.
- Vendors will be empowered by IDCOL. More time can be saved on their selection/realization as equipment suppliers. They will also work with borrowers to help them speed up application process
- Application process status would be available to borrowers through an MIS tool which they can access online.

### Output 2.6: Engagement with global clothing brands to increase EE adoption in textile sectors

The brands play a catalytic role in driving change in the textile and related sectors. The global brands in presence of international competition influence other stakeholders as seen through the feasibility analysis. As an outcome, the engagement with the global brands will help manufacturers can be engaged for working closely with the brands and developing customized modules to increase the reach and impact of this programme. This will be carried out by:
- Activity 2.6.1: Encouraging textile units to be more aligned to the brands’ sustainability targets and invest in energy efficiency improvement measures to reduce emissions and encouraging leading global brands by arranging round table discussions.
- Activity 2.6.2: Encouraging textile units to improve on the social and gender inclusion in workforce ratio to increase the co-benefits impact through sensitization and by undertaking consultation.

The TA component will be executed by IDCOL within the disbursement period of 06 years within project time line (Year 01-30). The main beneficiary of the component will be the textile sector borrowers for sustainable implementation of the program.

equipment, and access to commonly available commercial loans (i.e. around 1%[a]) in order for EE equipment to be widely adopted in Bangladesh. Using GCF and LFs funding, this can be achieved.

Also, there are a number of areas related to implementation where the RMIs unit requires expert guidance and external consultancy support. Banks, being a key stakeholder, also have limited capacities in assessing and financing EE projects. On one hand, both RMIs units as well as the banks have limited technical capacity and on the other hand the proposed program aims at conservatory high energy efficiency equipment which are largely available from China. Program therefore attempts to address both these issues through a structured procurement framework and a concessional line of financing. In order to access financing through the Program, the end borrowers will also be required to meet certain financial viability requirements, which is considered as equity participation and considered as part of the project amount.

Component 03 & 04 of the proposed program aims to address the key barriers of RMIs sector to increase their energy efficiency by supporting entrepreneurs and business establishments in the garment sectors through a structured procurement framework. The component will also support the installation of energy saving technology upgrades. In order to facilitate the implementation of the program, the Accredited Entity (AE) is IDLC Finance Limited Company. Limited IDLC has established LFs[1] as one of the Existing Entities for Component 03 and received financial support by means of concessional financing from Green Climate Fund (GCF).

The proposed program will comprise two components addressing the energy efficiency needs of the RMIs sector.

**Component 3: USD 200.0 million financing for Energy Saving Equipment & Technology for RMG sector**

Under this component, IDCOL will sign subsidiary agreements with the EEs to create a term loan facility which provides term loans with preferential rates directly to RMGS sector and borrowers.

### G. General criteria for lending to RMIs unit:

- **End borrowers i.e., project developer** will be a business entity of limited company
- **End borrowers should have operations in the production of RMG sector in Bangladesh**

For both Green field and Existing project related to upgradation to energy efficiency technology, IDCOL will provide term loan facility with preferential rates directly to the end borrower subject to credit (LC) for purchasing the EEAC equipment, anticipating use of the Project Loan may be opened by IDCOL. The LC will be provided on a revolving basis and will be available to borrowers having multiple expense heads only. Energy Efficient component of the proposed project to be financed.

- **All financing requests need to be supported by supplementary documentation** such as brochure or report/letter from independent/certified energy auditor acceptable to the LFs or the baseline development plan.
- **Fulfillment of other required criteria as mentioned in LFs CRM guideline, lending policy or other applicable policies/requirement**
- 100% of the eligible equipment price can be financed

During implementation of the project, LFs will undertake due diligence activities to determine eligibility of the RMG units for concessional financing. Based on the information provided by the LFs to GCF, LFs will provide assurance that the RMG units are eligible to receive concessional financing from GCF. If so, they will receive funds from GCF as per the disbursement schedule shared under the term sheet. The repayment of the loan from the EEs will also be supervised by DCC.

As per the preliminary analysis in the program concept stage, it has been estimated that the program would require a total investment of approximately USD 150 million coming from GCF and USD 100 million from co-financing sources. The disbursement period has been considered to be five years and the repayment period has been considered to be maximum 20 years at this stage.

### Component 4: USD 230 Million GCF Technical Assistance (TA) to develop enabling environment for EE investment in RMG sector
Based on the discussion with key stakeholders it has observed that there is limited market awareness and overall scepticism amongst stakeholders regarding implementation of energy efficiency measures, monitoring and verification of benefits accruing from such measures apart from need for concessional

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 91 OF 155

## Financing:

The TA feels that the institutional capacity in appraising an energy efficiency project and determining bankability of such projects is low as well as the capacity to ensure effective monitoring and verification of these projects to ensure benefits accrual is taking place and hence, repayment is also on track. RMG units, despite being aware about the importance of energy efficiency measures, are not fully aware that there exists limited awareness regarding implementation, maintenance and operational requirement of such projects. Also, the lack of demonstration projects within the sector and country, in general, is preventing otherwise promising projects from moving forward.

Given this context, along with concessional line of financing, there is a definite need to improve market perception about energy efficiency and provide support to the relevant stakeholders at all stages of implementation of energy efficiency measures. Also, creating an interface between energy efficiency equipment manufacturers and RMG units will help in disseminating information on energy efficient products to RMG unit owners, can help disseminate the information related to energy efficiency and resolve any queries that they may have regarding same. Policy makers are also required to be apprised of the barriers to implementing energy efficiency measures within the RMG sector so as to take appropriate policy reforms in order to help overcome these barriers.

It is envisaged that without such enabling activities, concessional line of financing could not help in generating sufficient interest among the stakeholders in undertaking energy efficiency measures. Also, given that the energy efficiency market in Bangladesh is in its infancy, such support activities would be critical to enable market transformation and galvanize the equipment procurement decisions of RMG units. Hence, the TA is very much required to ensure greater uptake of energy efficient measures during the programme duration as well as ensure sustainability of energy efficiency market for RMG sector beyond the programme duration.
