### Component 5: USD 1.15 million GCF Technical Assistance (TA) to strengthen regulatory & institutional capacity of national institutions at policy level to overcome the operational constraints related to implementing EE&C in the country

The component has been incorporated to address the policy/regulatory support issue that is preventing faster adoption of EE&C in the textile & RMJ sector of Bangladesh. Sustainable Renewable Energy Development Authority (SREDA) and National Environment Services Limited (NESL) are the nodal executing Entity for the proposed activities under this component.

The component includes several activities under two broad outputs (5.1) Development of relevant policies, strategies & plans and (5.2) Capacity building to strengthen capacity of relevant national institutions to overcome their existing operational barriers related to implementing EE&C projects.

The activities under output 5.1 are as follows:

- **5.1.1:** Review of existing national policies, strategies & plans regarding Energy Efficiency
- **5.1.2:** Identify any policy gap & develop mechanism to address such gaps & lack thereof baseline
- **5.1.3:** Development of relevant policies, strategies & plans

The activities under output 5.2 are as follows:

- **Activity 5.2.1:** Assessment of detail capacity development requirement at policy level
- **Activity 5.2.2:** Develop training manuals based on the world best practices focusing on performance improvement in textile and RMJ sector for implementation of energy efficiency projects; (prospective business model with feasible payback period)
- **Activity 5.2.3:** Organize 03 training of the trainers (ToT) for Government officials for institutionalization of the training manual
- **Activity 5.2.4:** Conduct a national consultation for the policy makers and relevant government official by the trained trainers. National relevant officials mean officials from different government departments who are directly involved in EE&C related activities.
- **Activity 5.2.5:** Organize 02 workshops to disseminate outcome of the programme to policy makers and EE practitioners and also the learning from implementing energy efficiency in textile & RMJ sector, including the role of women in energy efficiency projects, women involvement and role of government for long-term sustainability) to ensure institutional capacity building.
- **Activity 5.2.6:** Mass awareness for energy efficiency through other disseminating tools like media coverage, bill, etc.

The proposed activities under component 05 will facilitate institutionalization of the EE&C measures in the country and will be the key strategy to ensure additional concessional funding continues after programme completion.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND
## GREEN CLIMATE FUND
### GREEN CLIMATE FUND
#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND

Sustainable and Renewable Energy Development Authority (SREDA) has been formed under the provision of Sustainable and Renewable Energy Development Authority Act, 2012* on 22nd May, 2014 as a nodal agency to ensure energy security and promote, facilitate and disseminate sustainable energy (SE) in both urban and rural areas.

Earlier, different ministries and departments, including the Bangladesh Power Development Board (BPDB), the Rural Electrification Board (REB), the Local Government Engineering Department (LGED), autonomous bodies such as the Bangladesh Water Supply and Sanitation Mission (BWSSM), etc., were responsible for various aspects of energy sector. The formation of SREDA was a result of the decision of the Ministry of Power, Energy and Mineral Resources (MPEMR) of the Government of the People's Republic of Bangladesh as a coordination body for the development of the renewable energy in the country.

The below diagram represents the implementation arrangement proposed for Component 05:

| --- | --- |
| AMA | FAA |
| IDCOL | SREDA: Implementing Partner (Co-financing US$1.02 million) |

IDCOL as the accredited entity to GCF will have to sign the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) with GCF to ensure proposer implementation of Component 05. IDCOL, as the EE will be liable to supervise & monitor proper implementation during the implementation time. Since, SREDA will be the Implementing Partner for this component, IDCOL will provide necessary technical and financial support to SREDA in implementing the required consultant/consultant firm under its own procurement policy in consultation with and engagement of GCF. SREDA will be responsible for the overall management of the project including budget calculation. SREDA as the Implementing Partner will carry out specific activities mentioned above and IDCOL will facilitate SREDA for the management and operation for component 05.

In this regard, SREDA will prepare a report on energy savings achieved under this entire program to SREDA, in line with reporting to GCF (with the same frequency). This information will be incorporated in national data base for energy savings.

## Governance arrangements under the programme for Component 01 and GE2

ICDOL will play the significant role as Executing Entity to assess the eligibility of the sub-projects of the Textile sector for financing under the program as per the set criteria and accordingly will place disbursement request to GCF. ICDOL will be the borrower & Executing Entity (EE) where the Textile owners will be the end borrowers and the ultimate beneficiaries, textile borrowers. This business unit(s) of ICDOL will be in primary contact with the end borrowers. ICDOL has two separate roles as Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entity (EE).

ICDOL's Organogram (Figure 16 above) shows all the functional departments and their reporting structure under the Head of Corporate Operations including Financial Control, Audit, Procurement and other key functions. ICDOL Board has the following three special committees comprising board members: Audit Committee, Credit Committee and Organization Committee (Recruitment and Promotion Committee). Composition and roles of responsibilities of Board Committees is annexed.

Among list of internal oversight bodies/entities, ICDOL has its own standing committees for managing internal audit different areas of corporate operations: Management Committee, Credit Risk Management Committee, Asset Liability Management Committee. Internal Control and Compliance Unit, Risk Management and Risk Analysis Unit and Audit Committee. The composition of members of the aforesaid committees are enclosed in Annex 1. All these committees are assigned to ensure dedicated internal oversight on vital corporate governance issues. The committees comprise senior management officials of ICDOL with appropriate functional designation and responsibilities that best fit to serve the purpose of the committees.

**IDCOL experience in EECF:**

The Energy Efficiency and Conservation Promotion Financing Project (EECP) is a 100 Mn USD JICA credit administered by SREDA, and IDCOL is one of the participating financial institutions for further on-lending this fund at preferential rates to eligible projects based on a predefined criteria. Under the JICA funding, IDCOL has already approved 25 projects with an amount of approximately BDT 17 million. The total USD 42 million in the RMG and Cement sectors, out of which USD 25.15 million has been already disbursed. The financed project would help the end consumer in saving 25 percent and 34 percent of primary energy consumption for cement and RMG respectively. This will result in a reduction of electricity consumption of approximately 17 MWh over the life of the equipment. This would also result in a cost saving of approximately BDT 14 Crore per year for these installations for the end consumers.

IDCOL has already started approving projects under the second tranche of JICA's EECP, which approved 20 projects with an amount of approximately BDT 7 million. IDCOL targets to fully disburse the first and the second tranche of EECP by the end of 2021.

# Need for GCF funding for proposed mitigation intervention for Textile Sector (Component 01 &02):

- **Addressing high energy savings potential:** The textile is one of the most energy intensive industry in Bangladesh and a transition into an energy efficient, climate resilient, internationally competitive industry is critical. However, despite the availability of various financial instruments from both public sector, private commercial banks and NBIFs are the dominant players in financing energy efficiency, however, most of these instruments have not been able to meet their full potential due to several challenges like higher lending rate for end-user; difficulties in approval and disbursement process; lack of technical assistance either financial or technical; delay realization of the investment pipeline etc. Further, due to high interest rates and limited availability of financing, energy efficiency is not getting adequate funding from the commercial banks to the textile sector.

- **Key reasons why this funding from GCF will be very important for the sector as follows:**

  - The scope of the project aligns with the Bangladesh National Climate Change Strategy (BNCCS) and SDG 13.
  - Sustainable energy use remains of critical importance to the Bangladesh economy. Availability and the rising price of gas is a significant and growing constraint on the growth of industrial activity.
  - IDCOL will target to offer 50% of the loan amount to medium and small textile factories in Bangladesh, which are currently not able to access affordable financing from commercial perspectives. This will increase their access to debt-based instruments at affordable terms, and ultimately build


---
**Page 69**
---

their competitiveness. Without GCF funding, these smaller segment factories would not be able to access available loans from mainstream FIs.

* The loan product will be directed towards improving energy efficiency across complete value chain in Textile industry (different from Partnership for cleaner textile LED) by which it is a unique product catering to energy efficiency in complex value chain of Textile industry (Different from Partnership for cleaner textile LED (Pact) led by IFC whose focus and scope are limited to the end use)
* The loan facility will have following features which will make it highly attractive in the market:
    * Simplified of loan application procedure and speedy appraisal minimizing the time for loan processing
    * Availability of Grace period & Long Tenure and low interest.

In addition, since the programme will be implemented by ICDCL, it will have following advantage:
* Experienced implementing partner: ICDCL has built its capacity by successfully implementing EEAC in Bangladesh.
* Strong pipeline built by ICDCL for Textile and RMG sector while implementing EEAC under ICA/GEF.

### Ensuring suitable financing options: As a result of all the above-mentioned factors, there is a high need for a programme that can support the transition to energy efficiency in Bangladesh. The mixed finance model will be used as it has been found to be more effective and efficient than any other option for financing any new financial instrument. The proposed programme has been designed after a detailed feasibility study which has been conducted by ICDCL. The lessons learned from the feasibility studies learned for long term sustainability of the proposed programme. Since the feasibility studies suggest that the requirement for concessional, low interest rate loan scheme is quite high, the proposed programme will be co-financed by GCF and ICDCL’s own funds as it has been found to be more effective in attracting more beneficiaries especially the small and medium textile units for a greater impact. Moreover, it will minimize the time for loan procedure through simplification of loan application and speedy appraisal.

Relevance of the proposed financial instruments and Concessionality:

Existing or past EE financing products available in Bangladesh: provided capital at a very high interest rate to the end users. This restricted the clientele base to only large borrowers who can afford high interest rates.
Table 6-9, indicated that due to significant price difference of EE equipment a concessional rate is required which is not feasible for the existing financial institutions. The cost analysis of energy efficient equipment and capital equipment for textile industry indicate that 4% to 5% interest rate for local currency loan will be appropriate to compensate the extra capital cost of EE equipment. However, it has been learned from JICA- Energy Efficiency Financing Project (EEFP) that concessional rate is not feasible in Bangladesh.

In addition to GCF funds, the programme is proposed to be co-financed equally by ICDCL’s own funds as well as the end borrower’s equity thus ensuring all the stakeholders have a significant stake in the programme's success. The co-financing amounts and prices were determined based on the institutional and financial capacities of the beneficiaries and the trends in market. The associated financial assessment is discussed in detail in the feasibility report annex 2.

### Risk sharing

The program has been designed to address the anticipated risks and ensure that risks are shared and mitigated by the stakeholders best suited for a share. A detailed risk assessment has been carried out & mitigation measures have been identified during the program design (Anx: Risk Report). While ICDOL will be bearing the exchange rate risk, the currency fluctuation risk due to USD/BDT exchange rate will be mitigated at the end user level. One of the key risks to ICDOL would be on account of currency fluctuation risk since GCF will be lending in USD while ICDOL can be lending to the end users in BDT.

In time series analyses done for exchange rate movement of Bangladesh Taka against USD from 1998 to 2016, it is observed that the exchange rate has been highly volatile. Need for GCF funding for proposed mitigation intervention for RMG Sector (Component 3S 64A):

While adoption of energy efficiency measures can help in reduction of GHG emissions from the RMG sector and increasing in operating margins, the units must have finances available for their implementation. A comparison of the interest rates across major RMG producing economies is given in Figure 15 above for reference.

Even though the interest rates have exhibited a sharp fall in recent years, RMG units in the country are reported to be receiving funding at 10-12% interest rates from the banks and at 13%-14% from other financial institutions. The high interest rates are attributed to the following factors: (i) higher lending rate by the agency’s risk appetite, loan appraisal process and the creditworthiness of the particular RMG unit. In case a higher risk hierarchy is assessed, the interest rates may go beyond this range as well. As per discussions with stakeholders, RMG units in Bangladesh are facing difficulties to obtain financing from banks due to the higher risk perception about energy efficiency loan. It is also contributed by a combination of insufficient quality of collateral and lack of awareness among RMG units on the benefits of energy efficiency loans. In case of RMG units in Europe, USA have very low inflation rates, leading to near stagnant selling price. On the other hand, the input markets of Bangladesh experience higher inflation rates, leading to increasing input cost for RMG units. The high interest rates and rising input costs are further exacerbated by the presence of 'Alliance' in Bangladesh’s RMG sector. The increasing input cost and stagnated sales prices are reducing the profitability of RMG units. This tightened economic environment negatively affects RMG units’ ability to invest in energy efficiency.

Even future outlook for financing in the country does not provide any indication of lowered interest rates. It is observed that State-owned Commercial Banks (SCBs), which have a 48% share of loans in the country, have been facing liquidity constraints due to non-performing assets and are also reported to be facing SCBs from collapsing. Government of Bangladesh has taken the recourse of recurrent recapitalization under which it has spent USD 1.8 billion during the period FY2009-FY2017[6]. In order to stimulate growth in the country, the government has been implementing various fiscal measures and reforms.

Continued bank recapitalization by the Government is expected to constrain the government exchequer to invest in social and physical infrastructure.


The schemes under discussion are all related to energy efficiency and green financing in general. While all of the schemes include energy efficiency as one area of financing and/or RMG sector as one of the participating sectors, none of them are focused exclusively on energy efficiency improvement in the RMG sector. The focus is more on the overall sustainability of the RMG sector. It has been observed that some of these schemes have been able to achieve their intended targets and understand the gaps, if any, which limits the success of these schemes. Based on the discussion the following key gaps have been identified:

### Lower interest rates required

In order to ensure competitive advantage, the unit owners are operating with continually decreasing margins. In such a scenario, interest rates much lower than the existing market rate (in the range of 5-6%) would be required to encourage unit owners in taking loans exclusively for energy efficiency projects.

### Complicated loan application process

In order to get access to energy efficiency financing, unit owners need to conduct an energy audit. After the audit, the usual loan appraisal process is undertaken by the banks, making the entire process a lengthy and complicated one. Also, unit owners have to pay upfront the cost of energy audit, further adding to the cost of undertaking energy efficiency.

### Limited capacity of banks in appraisal process

Officials from the banks have limited understanding regarding energy efficiency projects. The bank officials have limited access to the standard tools, techniques and framework for appraisal of energy efficiency projects. This leads to lack of focus in the banks to neither develop financial products targeting energy efficiency investment nor conducts processing of loans for such project in a faster and efficient manner.

### Non-availability of loan with banking partners

The willingness of RMG partners is lower than expected due to RMG partners prefer taking loans from existing banks with existing bankers rather than engaging new relationships and in case of prolonged relationships, RMG owners secure loans at lower interest rates than prevailing market rates from their existing bank. In case the existing banks do not provide energy efficiency loans, the RMG owners are forced to take out new banks for energy efficiency loan - a deterrent which adds to the issues of energy efficiency financing.

Given this context of Bangladesh and the key concerns highlighted above, concessional finance can help break this vicious cycle by stimulating the willingness to lend to the banks as well as the willingness to invest in RMG owners end-use equipment.

### Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The programme has been designed to address the anticipated risks and ensure that risks are shared and managed by the stakeholders best suited for the same. A detailed risk assessment has been carried out & mitigation measures have been identified during the program design (Annex: Risk Report). The credit risk in this case, will be borne by the Executing Entities.

### Programme sustainability

The programme activities are designed to impact the sector in a way that the growth is inevitable. A catalytic change in financial, institutional, social, gender equality and environment aspect which will have longer influences far beyond the scope and timeline of this programme. (refer performance indicators annex 1)


---
**Page 72**
---

Based on consultations with RMG sector stakeholders, it has been established that the environmental, economic and resource conservation benefits of energy efficiency are recognized by over 60% of the top management of the garment manufacturing firms. However, lower prevalence of energy efficiency is due to constraints in the following areas:

1. **Focus excessive on top-line over bottom-line by the factory owners:** In a competitive market scenario, the factory owners are more inclined to invest in adding production capacity to increase revenue i.e., top-line rather than investing in energy efficiency to increase profitability i.e. bottom-line. Hence, they are not willing to spend money on energy audit and other related activities.

2. **Capital investment requirement:** The investment payback in energy efficiency is not immediate and depends on the specific measure being undertaken. In most cases, units do not have adequate capital availability to undertake additional capital investment in increasing production efficiency and hence, are sceptical about investing in energy efficiency measures. As a result, they have no choice but to spend money on hiring energy audit firms. This adds to the cost component for undertaking energy efficiency measures.

3. **Insufficient demonstration examples:** Examples of actual savings achieved from energy efficient projects are very few and far between in Bangladesh. Owners are normally not hesitant on making additional investment when they have case studies which make them help be fully aware of the on-ground requirements for getting benefits from implementing energy efficiency measure.

4. **Lack of standardization of energy efficient technologies and processes:** There is limited market information available regarding the availability of specific types of the energy efficient technologies and are completely dependent on the opinion of energy auditors to implement a specific measure. A list of standardized technologies along with normally used technical specifications that can be used in the RMG sector would be a useful tool for appraise owners as well as the on-ground staff about the energy efficient equipment.

5. **Limited market information:** Also, there is limited information available in the market regarding the processes and procedures to be undertaken to identify energy savings opportunities and for assessing the benefits of energy efficiency measures. The lack of such information makes the DPOs unaware of this knowledge gap and instil a sense of confidence in the owners regarding the entire energy savings assessment process.

The proposed programme aims to address these issues by providing concessional loans and technical assistance to stimulate the energy efficiency market in Bangladesh. Since the programme has been designed as loan to the beneficiaries – only the projects which are bankable in nature i.e. projects which, on an economic basis, can be financed through commercial banks or other financial institutions – will be a suitable solution set for the programme. Thus, the programme will help kick starting energy efficiency projects in the RMG sector at an accelerated pace. This will generate a strong sector-wide demonstration effect. Hence, it is to be expected that (a) the RMG sector will be more willing and able to make investments to invest in energy efficiency projects; and (b) banks and FIs will also be ready to offer a greater number of financial products and instruments designed to cater to the appropriate need of the factory owners for investing in energy efficiency projects.

As mentioned above, the programme is expected to improve the risk perception of investment in energy efficiency in the sector by creating strong demonstration effects and increasing the availability of

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.0 | PAGE 79 OF 155

## Financial
Financial support to the industries to procure EE equipment which would otherwise be filtered out of their options due to capital constraints. The EE equipment procured under the programme, if costly, will not burden the textile and RMG factory owners, since the cost saving on energy will, in some cases, reach the break-even and the loan is hopefully paid off through the savings.

## Institutional
Institutional capabilities will be built financially, technologically and in terms of the work force. Internationally competitive EE equipment will facilitate growth in terms of production, productivity and quality. The industries will be able to compete with other textile and RMG industries will timely be reporting the social impact on their workforce especially gender opportunity and the initiatives towards environmental cleaning of their factories.

## Social
Institutional and financial growth of the industries will bring about growth of the communities associated with them. The work force will experience growth in wages as an indirect benefit of industrial growth/profit-making; with expansion in production, order entry and sales, etc., the work force will be able to earn more money. The industries will have to provide for basic needs like medical facilities, savings accounts, provision of safer working environments, etc.

## Gender equality
Special attention should be given to ensure that neither of men or women experience discrimination. There has to be equity in opportunities for financial opportunities in loan disbursement and job opportunities; skill training; provision of special toilets, separate washrooms and maternity leaves, improving work environment, etc.

## Environmental
The programme scope is designed to continually prevent a great amount of greenhouse gases from textile and RMG sectors of Bangladesh. The endorsement and installation of EE equipment will prevent emissions during the programme life cycle and beyond.

## Sustainability as a goal
The programme aims to achieve catalytic impact in the textile and RMG sector on various parameters. These, if successful, would be replicated across other sectors and ensure significant momentum for the objectives. Post 20 years, the impacts of the programme will continue to benefit the environment, society

# GCF'S EXIT STRATEGY

## For Component 01 & 02:

The long-term sustainability of the programme and responsible stakeholder behavior will ensure smooth exit from the programme through pre-screening of the potential test end term borrowers to disburse fund and/or their progress through the respective deliverables as mention in MRE. Evaluation of progress reports and timely repayment of loan will be done to ensure continued sustainability of the sub-projects. These interventions will be carried out by GCF, while the responsibility for the exit strategy is SDCN’s responsibility to facilitate GCF to exit the programme once the tenure has been completed.

## For Component 03 & 04:

The programme activities under Component 03 & 04, have been designed with a view of developing market for energy efficiency equipment in RMG sector through capacity building and awareness generation activities. It is expected that the RMG sector will be able to overcome the immediate financial barrier that prevents RMG units considering energy efficient equipment, a concessional loan is envisaged under Component 3.

A key element of this component is that there will be real-life examples of implementation which will demonstrate the actual benefits that can be achieved and hence establish business case behind adopting energy efficient equipment. This will improve risk perception of energy efficiency measures and create a demand for energy efficient equipment in RMG sector. The increased demand will lead to an increase in the demand of the equipment, thereby increasing availability of financing options in response. The market development activities under Component 03 & 04 are expected to result in a significant increase in demand for energy efficient equipment. The increased demand is also expected to bring down the prices of equipment, making it more affordable and hence creating a positive product market transformation, which will be self-sustaining and continue after GCF closes the program.

The program design also includes screening of loan application based on established due diligence parameters. These parameters include energy efficiency criteria. Appropriate arrangements are made for loan disbursement, thereby insulating GCF from any default risk and facilitate safe exit from programs.


## For Component 05:

Under this component certain policy will be developed upon proper assessment of policy gap, if any. Sustainable capacity building by key regulatory stakeholders will be ensured through capacity building initiatives. Energy savings data from this program will be reported to SREDA & will be incorporated into national database.

### IMPACT POTENTIAL IN THE TEXTILE SECTOR:

#### Energy Saving Potential:
The textile industry is one of the major energy intensive industries in Bangladesh with total industrial demand of 1,701 TOE/year which amount to 17% of total energy demand. As per the feasibility analysis, the replacement of old inefficient machinery with new efficient machines can save up to 54% of electricity and other machineries to save costs and thus has lot of potential for implementing new energy efficient equipment or rettolfitting the existing machineries with energy efficient technologies. Because of energy efficiency improvement under the programme in the textile sector, the total energy saving in the sector is estimated to be 5.47 T GWh.

#### Emission Reduction Potential:
The programme is expected to result in direct emission reductions of 4.0 million tCO2e by facilitating and incentivizing energy efficiency improvements for a number of textile facilities. Energy efficiency improvement projects include both equipment and technology measures in the selected textile factories. The estimated emission reduction potential from the project activities is calculated based on avoided quantity of fuel consumption by multiplying baseline energy use by relevant GCF-approved carbon intensity factors (tCO2e/tonne). The total emission reductions are expected to be 4.0 million tCO2e cumulatively over the 20-year investment life-cycle. Emission reductions are calculated based on avoided quantity of fuel consumption by multiplying baseline energy use by relevant GCF-approved carbon intensity factors (tCO2e/tonne).

The expected programme cost for Textile sector is USD 13 million of which, USD 100 million will be funded through GCF, USD 33.0 million from IDCOL in the form of loan. There will be leverage financing of $33.0 million in the form of equity investment from end borrowers. Besides, there is a GCF grant for Technical Assistance (TA) to support project implementation and monitoring and evaluation activities. The TA is not enough to resist national market reflexes. # IMPACT POTENTIAL IN THE RME SECTOR:

The programme aims to achieve the fund’s objective and result areas by contributing shall to low-emission sustainable development pathways. The programme is anchored by providing targeted incentives (i.e. RME sector of Bangladesh) with a clear line of financing for underpinning energy efficiency measures which will result emissions savings as well as provide market participants (RME units, banks, policy makers) with appropriate tools and instruments to support implementation of energy efficiency initiatives. Implementation of the programme will also help Bangladesh meet national climate commitments*, energy targets**, contributing to nation’s overall efforts reducing GHG emissions. The RME sector contributes a major proportion to Bangladesh economy (sector contributes to 11% of nation's GDP and employs nearly 4 million people)***, it will help streamline industrial development with human development.

## Energy Saving Potential:

The textile industry is the most energy intensive industrial subsector of Bangladesh with total demand of 2.9 TCF/year or about 50% of total demand of industrial sector. As per situation analysis conducted in feasibility studies, energy consuming processes in the sector employ old equipment and exhibit potential to be replaced with equipment which lead to efficient use of energy through incorporation of new technologies. The textile industry consumes a large amount of electricity (12 TCF/year) and heat (30 TCF/year), which can be reduced by undertaking low-cost or no-cost measures. Based on the assessment, an average efficiency improvement potential is estimated at 45% for existing equipment and considering the potential number of units that can be targeted by the project cost (i.e. USD 250 million), the total energy saving is estimated to be 17.486 TCF/year throughout programme lifetime (21 years).

## Emission Reduction Potential:

The textile industry has the potential to result in direct emission reductions of 0.327 TCD/Year by facilitating implementation of energy efficiency measures in RME units. Energy efficiency improvement projects include both equipment and technology measures in the selected textile factories. Considering the programme lifetime (i.e., 21 years), the estimated total emission reduction potential is 6.87 TCO2e/year. The emission reduction potential per unit of investment (cost per USD) is USD 0.35.

## Project Cost Breakdown:

The expected project cost is USD 200 million, out of which USD 150 million will be funded by GCF through co-financing loans and USD 50 million through co-financing. With the co-financing component, USD 50 million will be contributed by LFBs by way of loans and leverage financing of USD 50 million will be contributed by Bangladesh Technical Assistance (TA) for implementation support and development of energy efficiency market in Bangladesh.

The investment from the programme perspective as well as GCF contribution is evaluated per term cost per TCO2e. The estimated average term cost per TCO2e for programme value and GCF’s contribution are USD 19/20TCO2e and USD 14.5/14.5TCO2e respectively.

## D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the degree to which the programme and associated impact extend beyond a one-off project or programme investment. Describe the following, if applicable:

- **Potential for knowledge sharing and learning**
- **Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment**

The programme will enable market-based energy efficiency investment and shifting the established paradigm and pathways the investments must be made. The paradigm shift potential for the proposed programme lies in the programme's focus on the sector as the driving force for investment and implementation of EE retrofits, as opposed to current models which only focus on large industries.

## Paradigm Shift Potential for Textile Sector

### Potential for scaling up and replication

The potential to scale-up is great and has been well-incorporated into the project design through the programme's focus on the textile sector. The programme will enable market-based energy efficiency investments and ease access to finance for textile factories besides enabling further investment decisions.

Its replicability within Bangladesh and abroad is high. A transformational programme as this will invite more textile factories to participate in EE adoption and upgradation, and energy efficiency itself and enable climate mitigation. It can be replicated with similar success in other developing countries such as neighbouring countries like Indonesia, Vietnam, and Cambodia with strong textile industrial sector and similar barriers and risks involved, and may benefit by learning from successful projects in Bangladesh which is a high-volume supply market.

### Potential for knowledge and learning

The programme will contribute to knowledge creation and sharing by all market players. The two workshops have been designed to ensure availability of fund and knowledge sharing.

The programme has a strong component of communication and knowledge sharing through consultations, capacity building process of key implementing partners of the programme (i.e., IDCOL and the textile industries) and dissemination of information on EE technologies, equipment and associated financing options. The monitoring activities during implementation of the programme will generate information and early lessons from good practices to be further replicated in later implementation stages of the programme allowing exchange of knowledge.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The programme will enable market-based energy through policy, finance, technical / capacity development and barrier removal. There will be deep penetration of energy-saving technologies, equipment and associated financing options into the textile sector, creating a strong enabling environment for both end users and suppliers of energy saving technologies. Helping to build scale and volumes for energy saving technology suppliers, the program will bring down the costs of these technologies and make them attractive even at commercial financing rates.

## Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change mitigation/adaptation strategies and plans

As per the commitments of GCF+, programme will mainstream climate-resilient development pathways into project design, implementation and monitoring. This will be achieved at national, regional and local levels including private-sector decision-making through this programme will contribute in emission reduction by promoting energy efficiency measures for low-carbon and climate-resilient markets. The investments enabled through this program will lead climate resilient development.

## PARADIGM SHIFT POTENTIAL FOR RMG SECTOR

The programme exhibits substantial potential to affect a paradigm shift in the application of energy efficiency measure for RMG sector in Bangladesh and catalyse impact beyond a one-off project or programme investment:

- **Potential for scaling up and replication:** The model adopted by the programme will help establish the replicable framework for financing, implementation and monitoring of energy efficiency measures that will help overcome the market apprehensions regarding energy efficiency which is leading lower uptake of such technologies. The two-pronged approach of providing concessionary financing and providing technical assistance for RMG units will create a conducive environment to develop sufficient markets for RMG sector. With industries sector in Bangladesh having substantial savings potential, this approach can be scaled up across the country.

- **Potential for knowledge sharing and learning:** As per the programme design, TA consultants will provide implementation support to RMG units and MRR support to Executing Entities (EEs)/LFs for the project execution. The TA component will also help in addressing the key issues faced by both the stakeholder groups along with the implemented solutions. This will provide future project proponents with access to lessons from previous projects and accordingly, these learnings can be used to improve the design of future projects leading to better project realization.

- **Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment:** The TA component will help develop the energy efficiency market in Bangladesh for RMG sector. Overall, the programme through its TA and concessionary financing component will address the technical and financial barriers that are preventing uptake of energy efficiency measures. Technology fairs are planned in the program design to create an interface with the end users i.e., RMG units and the energy efficient equipment manufacturers. The technology fairs will provide a platform for showcasing the technologies required for improved access to energy efficient equipment, in terms of availability and lower costs.

- **Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies:** Under the TA component, capacity building of policy makers of Bangladesh in fields related to energy efficiency in industrial sphere is planned. This is expected to give policy makers with insights on the potential contribution energy efficiency can have

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE OF 155

## Bangladesh is one of the most adversely affected countries in terms of climate change.

The topography is flat, and majority of the landmass lies within 10 meters above mean sea level. The geographic location at the confluence of two major river systems (the Brahmaputra and Ganges) makes it highly vulnerable to flooding from the Himalayas. All land types except highlands are exposed to monsoon flooding for part or whole of the year; Flooding is further exacerbated by climate change, which has led to an increase in frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as cyclones, storm surges, salinity intrusion and coastal inundation. Permanently terrace land is characterized by moisture stress when rainfall is below normal levels. The country’s vulnerability to climate change is further compounded by the fact that river flow and river flood by cycloptically caused by GHG emissions from using fossil fuel consumption.

This is despite the total GHG emissions in the country being less than 0.35% of the global emissions. The projected increase in temperature, sea level rise, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and net GHG emissions internationally, the impact of climate change to Bangladesh could be catastrophic resulting in significant economic losses and human suffering. Bangladesh is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change and is among the least affected countries of world in the Climate Risk Index for 2017.

Bangladesh has been a strong proponent of global action under UN Paris Agreement framework to help limit GHG emissions and mitigate climate change impacts in the country. Being a developing country, Bangladesh is also in the list of LDCs by GCF and hence a focus area for the fund.

Also in the current energy supply scenario, the public sector investments in Bangladesh is focused more on supporting the existing power plants rather than investing in new generation capacity. This is due to limited budgetary resources available with the government to drive public sector investments for demand side interventions, especially towards energy saving technologies. In this scenario, boosting private sector investment into energy efficiency and renewable energy projects will be critical to achieve the country’s targets such as such as high capital market, high cost of lending, high investment costs as well as off-taker credit quality. Efforts are being made by the government to create enabling environment for private sector investments in this scenario, supporting businesses with attractive financing products and mechanisms for energy saving technology upgrades could go a long way in getting the attention of corporate top management and encouraging them to invest.

For Textile and RMG manufacturers, available means of financing is through bank loans or equity contribution. Under the current market scenario Textile and RMG units have to lend at a rate of 9-15% based on the credit worthiness and credit history of unit. While the bank-lending has grown over since 2016th, credit risk in the textile sector is still high due to several factors such as: low credit rating of borrowers, weak credit culture elevated owing to non-banking practices, historically weak credit-risk management practices, concentrated loan books, exposure to single large borrowers and government-directed pressure on state-owned bank lending decisions. The inherent high level of risk of lending explains the high lending rates, as

compared to other competitors in RMG export market. Current efforts to stabilize banks by recapitalization by Government of Bangladesh is expected to continue leading to expectations that high interest rates will continue to prevail. Such high interest rates negatively affect credit influx into the sector at a time when production capacity is already underutilized, due to increased and increased labour wages and due stagnant market prices in customer markets in USA and EU.

Besides the financing barriers, energy efficiency uptake also technical challenges such as lack of awareness and understanding of the long term benefits by consumers, perceived underperformance risks in energy savings, and limited availability of skilled manpower for implementation of energy efficiency projects. These can be mitigated to some extent by demand side awareness and capacity building. Based on discussion with stakeholders, it was established that there is limited capacity in banks to assess and finance energy efficiency projects. This is also due to the fact that most of the energy project objectives are realized. There are also technical knowledge gaps in RMG units regarding implementation of energy efficient technologies, policies and practices. Therefore, a conducive energy efficiency industries sector should be enhanced to ensure market barriers are removed. Accordingly capacity building of policy makers on global best practices on energy efficiency policy development is essential to provide guidance on developing similar policies/regulations for Bangladesh.

The programme is consistent with the country's ambitions and goals under the UN Paris Climate framework. Moreover, the program is consistent with the country's national strategy (Energy Efficiency Master Plan) to reduce energy consumption in the industry sector by 2030 through energy efficiency products with low interest loan as one of the key financial incentives. Furthermore, the project also contributes to the GCF target to achieve 10% of energy consumption reduction in the industry sector by 2030 and 45% of total emission reduction.

Bangladesh's NDC identifies three key emitting sectors, among which power sector accounts for 33% of total emission, transportation 26% and industrial sector 41%. Taking this into consideration, improving industrial energy efficiency is one of the key mitigation interventions. The Bangladesh government has been identified as the three key mitigation interventions. This has been embedded in our Five-Year Planning process (2015-2020) and the country's NDC. The target was set to reduce 15% GHG emission below Business As Usual (BAU) emission level by 2030.

To arrange necessary funding for low emission development, the Economic Relations Division of the Ministry of Finance, also the NDA of GCF in Bangladesh developed a Country Programme in 2018 with support from GCF. The country programme is a strategic document that outlines the overall strategy and objectives projects/programme that should be funded under GCF. The Country Program includes a robust pipeline of projects and programs which will be submitted to GCF for its financial support (SOCL as a Direct Access).

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe how the financial structure is adequate and reasonable in order to achieve the proposal’s objectives, including addressing existing bottlenecks and barriers, and providing the minimum concessionary interest rate to ensure the project is viable for both public and private and other public investments. Refer to section B.5 on the justification of GCF funding requested as necessary.

#### FOR TEXTILE SECTOR

The programme's results chain is an approach to market transformation for energy efficiency. This approach is based on the fact that, due to the high upfront capital intensity of energy efficient investments in the textile sector and large quantities of low-cost financing is critical to cost-effectively drive energy efficiency in the textile sector.

By conducting payment simulations for four types of equipment in B.3, the proposed financial structure was deemed to be adequate and reasonable to achieve programme’s objective:

- The purchase of an energy efficient spinning machine which is ~25% more expensive compared with a conventional type will require a 6.53% interest rate loan (considering 8 year loan tenure and 1 year grace period) to be even with the conventional type in terms of total payment amount.
- On the other hand, in order to overcome the barrier of longer payback period, the loan tenor was increased to 10 years, with 2 years grace period. With such, the purchase of an energy efficient spinning machine will require a 6.67% interest rate loan (considering 10 year loan tenure and 2 year grace period) to be even with the conventional type in terms of total payment amount.
- Similarly, for the purchase of an energy efficient boiler which is ~27% more expensive once-through boiler requires 5.28% interest rate (considering 8 year loan tenure and 1 year grace period) to be even with the conventional type in terms of the total payment amount.

On the other hand, in order to overcome the barrier of longer payback period, the loan tenor was increased to 10 years, with 2 years grace period. With such, the purchase of an energy efficient spinning machine will require a 6.67% interest rate loan (considering 10 year loan tenure and 2 year grace period) to be even with the conventional type in terms of total payment amount.

The above analysis clearly demonstrates that to ensure the feasibility of the subproject, concessionary lending is required from GCF to IDCOL to enable IDCOL to extend loan at the low interest to the end borrowers. The proposed programme will use the Energy Efficiency Component Project (EECP) which has been so far for the most successful EE credit line in Bangladesh. Thus, for the proposed programme IDCOL is using EECP project as the benchmark for the end user financing package.


---
**Page 88**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 85 OF 155

Besides just comparing the market value of the traditional and EE equipment, further analysis in terms of assessing the NPV or operational cost of both types of machines were done to determine the right interest rate that will provide appropriate RR for the end users. Please refer to Table 12.

The table below compares the NPV of additional investments required for installing the energy efficient spinning and boiler machines compared with the proposed GCF credit line vis-à-vis regular equipment under the standard commercial loan.

| Machine Type | Δ Investment NPV if GCF loan is availed (in% @tenor-10yrs, moratorium-1 yr) | Δ Investment NPV if loan to commercial loan is availed (in% @tenor 8 yrs, moratorium -1 yr) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spinning machine | BOT 4.81,998 | (BOT 29,966) |
| Boiler | BOT 137,760 | (BOT 17,823) |

The above table clearly indicates that only an interest rate in the range of 5%-6% for the GCF credit line would make the additional investment in EE equipment worthwhile to the borrowers. Thus, crowding out or pushing out will not happen at this time and the same time the borrower will be able to provide energy efficient end users.

The Δ investment in the above table signifies the overall savings that a borrower will achieve with the proposed GCF credit line vis-à-vis regular commercial loan. The total cost of ownership of EE equipment are on higher side than that of a conventional machine, the savings in terms of operating cost over a lifetime and a tenure of 8-10 years, outweigh the higher cost of EE equipment. The life of machine is so long that the borrower will be able to recover the additional investment within the first year of operation and an incremental electricity tariff of 2% is considered for calculating for the operating. The EE equipment for each machine type shall have a minimum efficiency of 10% over conventional machine.

With the current JICA-EECAPP credit line, there are some challenges faced such as (a) it focused on varied sectors and industries and not specific to textile sector; (b) the interest rate is high, thus, aproriation proposed in this programme will help to cater the huge demand and potential of energy efficiency in the textile sector and will percolate deep inside the sector. The second issue with JICA-EECAPP involves the project ceiling amount which is limited to USD 10 million. Thus, the project ceiling has been extended to BY 25 million for this programme. This will help to accommodate all kind of EE equipment that can be installed under this project. These limitations do not help the project to penetrate deep inside any sector with an approach to bring the entire sector to world standards norms and energy intensity standards. Hence keeping this in mind the proposed programme is designed to accomodate all kind of textile equipment having a minimum efficiency level of 10%.

## FOR RMG SECTOR

High upfront capital investment and high interest rates of borrowing has been informed by stakeholders as a key barrier from implementing energy efficient technologies, even if the technologies exhibit a high


---
**Page 89**
---

expected cost of return. The program intends to address this barrier through concessional financing and supplement it with market development activities which would help stimulate market demand, increase availability of energy equipment and develop capacities of market players.

The level of concessionality which will stimulate market for energy efficiency has been determined by simulation loan payment scenarios (considering 8-year loan tenure and 2-year grace period) for 2 sample equipment:

| Conventional vs Energy Efficient boiler: The energy efficient gas boiler is around 25% more expensive than a conventional gas boiler. If the interest rate on the loan remains at market rate i.e. 11%, considering an amount as that of a conventional gas boiler being financed by loan at market rate i.e. 11%. Considering a 10% premium on repayment, the interest rate becomes 7.55%. |
| Conventional vs Energy Efficient Stirling: The energy efficient Stirling is around 25% more expensive than an energy efficient boiler. If the interest rate on the loan remains at market rate i.e. 11%, considering a 5.35% interest rate to have the same repayment amount as a conventional Stirling with financial assistance, the interest rate becomes 7.55%. |

Accordingly, a loan payment simulation has been conducted to compare the rate of interest at which an energy efficient equipment will have the same repayment as a loan taken at market rate for a comparable conventional equipment in terms of the loan remaining the same (viz. loan tenure and moratorium/grace period). The simulation has been conducted for two sample equipment viz., gas fired boiler and Stirling machines as given below.

It is assumed that since energy efficient equipment gives an additional benefit of monetary savings through energy efficiency, the amount to be owned can be financed by paying a lower interest rate than any equivalent variant. This would also help financial institutions give some margin to accommodate any operational costs. The results from the simulation are given below:

Table 13: Price simulation for Energy Efficient Gas Boiler

| Parameter | Unit | Commercial finance for energy efficient equipment | Concessional finance for energy efficient equipment |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Price | USD | 57,600 | 72,000 |
| Interest Rate % | 11% | 11% | 5.15% / 7.55% |
| Tenure Period yrs. | 10 | 10 | 10 |
| Grace Period yr. | 2 | 2 | 2 |
| Total Payment USD | 89,543 | 111,929 | 89,661 / 98,527 |

**Page 90**
---

# Table 14: Price simulation for energy-efficient device

| Parameter | Commercial Finance for conventional equipment | Commercial finance for energy efficient equipment | Concessional finance for energy efficient equipment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Price | USD 68,000 | - | At par |
| Interest Rate % | 11% | 11% | 5.35% | 7.85% |
| Tenure Period yrs. | 10 | 10 | 10 | 10 |
| Grace Period yr. | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
| Total Payment USD | 100,736 | 124,385 | 100,428 | 110,736 |

Considering a wider range of equipment, a range of interest between between 5.0%-7.5% should be ideal market prices of loan instruments to generate interest in unit owners for energy efficient equipment.

Hence, a minimum concessional financing at -5.0-7.5% would be required to make energy efficient equipment more attractive than conventional equipment. Given the expected increase in electricity tariffs and monetary savings (in the form of energy savings), it is expected that unit owners will be more inclined to buy an energy efficient over a non-energy efficient one, even with 10% premium on repayment, thereby creating a market for energy efficient equipment.

The program will also create a competitive environment among unit owners on the business case for energy efficiency as well as increasing access of energy efficient equipment. The financial structure for the program includes 04 local financial institutions, which will act as EE for the program. However, during programme implementation new LFs may be selected based on the selection criteria described in B4, notably iDCOL may also be an EE if required. For now the shortlisted 04 LFs these are: Bangladesh Bank, IDCOL, BRAC and Grameen Bank. These banks have already been identified as instruments. They have established project due diligence procedures aligned with the statutory requirement of Bangladesh. The presence of these institutions in the financial structure will help in taking the concept forward and also in ensuring that the funds are used for the intended purpose. This will increase efficiency in disbursing and monitoring given the alignment of the bank in operating green financing instruments.

The role played by the banks will help in inviting private sector financing in the sector and with success of the program, it is expected more financial institutions (both public and private sector) will increase participation in the sector. Apart from the issues discussed above, a number of stakeholders were consulted during program design, which helped to identify gaps in capacity building in appraising, implementing, monitoring and evaluating energy efficiency projects as well as improve market interface between equipment suppliers and RMUs units. These non-financial barriers, which are also critical to the success of the program, have also been addressed through activities proposed in Component 2.

## FOR TEXTILE SECTOR

As mentioned above, this programme envisages the creation of concessional fund with USD 133 million, an Accredited Entity with GCF, will make available the GCF funds in the form of loans to the end borrowers, who are in this case the private textile manufacturing companies. Using this fund, the projected IRR estimated to be achieved is 5.0%. The project envisages that the total energy savings from the implementation of the proposed GHG reductions estimated at 0.2 million tCO2 and 4.0 million tCO2 equivalent over the lifetime of the project (13 years). This will lead to a reduction in electricity consumption by 65% for various equipment and by 78% for various equipment varies between 4-13 years. In addition to these economic and financial benefits, the project will also lead to other tangible social and gender impacts such as better community living standards, provision of safe working environment, and skill up gradation for workers.

## FOR RMG SECTOR

The proposed programme envisages to infuse USD 200 million in financing energy equipment in tSCF units. The loans will be provided by concessional loans from GCF and USD 50 million from EIB as LFs/GCs, in the form of loans. The loans will be provided by LFSDOL/ICCDL to the end borrowers at a rate substantially lower than prevailing market rates (+1%). Using this fund, the projected IRR is 5.0%. The project envisages that the total energy savings from the implementation of the proposed GHG reductions estimated over the lifetime.

Considering the economic benefit of the emission savings, the program returns an IRR of 44.5%.

In addition to these economic and financial benefits, the project will also lead to other tangible social and gender impacts such as better community living standards, providing safe and better working environment leading to higher gender balance in workforce and skill up gradation for workers. It will also lead to higher cost effectiveness in the RMG sector through energy efficiency and creation of green jobs.

The increased demand for energy efficient equipment will also lead to creation of green jobs.


## FOR TEXTILE SECTOR

Sector wide best practices can be broken down into the process improvement, metering water conservation, fuel efficiency and reduction in electricity consumption. These measures represent the best practices for resource efficiency and a cost effectiveness perspective in the textile processes.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 89 OF 155

## Process Improvement
- Development of skills and knowledge management for awareness
- Information systems for monitoring and reporting
- Procurement and investment
- Marketing and communications for encouraging other units/firms
- Process automation to control drying and printing processes
- Replacement of energy-intensive machines with less energy-intensive machines
- Benchmark water and energy use and set concrete reduction targets

## Metering
To enable facility management to closely track resource and energy consumption in specific processes and to identify those resource-intensive processes where efforts to improve efficiency should be focused.

## Water Conservation is Energy Conservation
- Water recycling, preventive maintenance, improved cleaning
- Improve boiler efficiency
- Improve efficiency of steam systems and maintain steam traps
- Insulation of equipment and tanks
- Reuse cooling water. Reuse condensate. Reuse process water

## Efficient Use of Fuels
Fuel savings from above mentioned water conservation and reuse by:
(a) Improving the operation and efficiency of the boiler
(b) Reducing the quantity of steam in the production process to reduce the quantity of steam the boiler must produce

## Reduction in Electricity Consumption
- Optimization of compressed air systems: fixing leaks in the air system and checking and optimizing pressure
- Electricity savings from heat recovery

The above mentioned practices are easy-to-implement and low-cost that come with multiple co-benefits. Based on best practices, these measures can be adopted to improve energy effectiveness and increase in productivity of textile units. These can be taken up as capacity building part of the TA programs.

### RMG Sector
Garment production is an organized activity consisting of sequential processes such as laying, marking, cutting, stitching, checking, finishing, pressing and packaging. Based on best practices in each process interventions were suggested to the stakeholders. These measures represent the best practices for resource efficiency and a cost effectiveness perspective in the garment manufacturing processes.

Energy Management and Conservation
- Optimization of compressed air system
- Installation of auto oxygen control in boilers

Fuel Reduction
- Improving boiler efficiency
- Regular cleaning of condensate systems and maintain steam traps
- Insulation of equipment and tanks
- Heat recovery and reuse: Reuse cooling water, Reuse condensate.
- Increasing efficiency in the use of steam in the production process to reduce fuel consumption.

Reduction in Electricity Consumption

- Installation of LED lighting system

- Installation of energy-efficient screw compressors

The above-mentioned energy conservation measures are easy-to-implement and low-cost that come with multiple co-benefits. Besides energy efficiency, these would bring in resource efficiency, environmental benefits, cost effectiveness and increase in productivity of RMG units. These can be taken up as capacity building part of the TA programme.

- (ii) Baseline numbers reflect market conditions and are not influenced by owners or decision makers for loan applications
- (iii) Cost burden does not fall on borrower completely.

# E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Arrangements for monitoring, reporting and evaluation for component 01 & 02 (E&E in Textile sector)

### Monitoring

- **ICCOL** has a proven track record of highly efficient, effective monitoring, and supervision of the previously led and currently internationally funded country-wide program. This has been made possible because of the programme specific MRE plans devised and implemented by ICCOL suited to the needs of the project.
- As ICCOL will act both as AE & EE for the program, separate units of ICCOL will play different roles:
  - **Business Unit of ICCOL**: Will be responsible for both the execution of TA support & concessional funding to projects, including disbursement of funds and monitoring of the projects. The unit will also manage the fund as well as supervision, evaluation and reporting.
  - Monitoring will be ensured for each sub-project, to be financed under this program, in different project phases through multiple engagements.

### Stage | Responsibility | Monitoring action

| Sub-project approval stage |
| --- |
| Business unit of ICCOL | Select eligible borrowers suitable for GCF financing & assess projects viability under regular process |
| Credit Risk Management (CRM) | Ensure due diligence of borrowers and compliance with existing lending policy of ICCOL & other GCF facility criteria |

### Stage | Responsibility | Monitoring action

| Sub-project implementation stage |
| --- |
| Business unit, Legal Unit, Department (CAD) | Approve funding proposals as per ICCOL’s approval ensuring viability, integrity, governance, sustainability and other related aspects. |
| Business unit | Ensure disbursement of GCF fund to line with F&A |

### Reporting & evaluation mechanism:

- As per GFCF mandate for a participatory AE, there will be frequent reporting from the EE to the AE and the AE to the Fund at pre-determined intervals. As the EE, while the business unit of ICCOL will continue to report as usual to the EE, the GFCF unit of ICCOL which is a dedicated wing other than the business unit will supervise & monitor generation of supporting/documents for reporting & verification of different components under the log frame.

IDCOL will supervise & monitor generation of supporting/documents for reporting & verification of different components under the log frame.

Periodic energy savings reporting will contain actual savings from ongoing sub-projects. The estimated potential of energy efficient equipment & technology will be ascertained by the borrower with the help of consultants and will be reported to IDCOL. This information will be used in the preparation of the TA. End Borrowers will install energy measurement equipment at the factory premise to identify energy usagesavings for the equipment/technology financed under this specific GCF program. The expected impact of the project (i.e., whether the project’s overall outcome will be verified through 1) Energy savings and 2) Increased production output by using EE equipment & technology

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk Assessment and Mitigations (max 3 pages)

### FOR COMPONENT 01 & 02 (EEAC IN TEXTILE SECTOR):

The main type of risk is technical & operation risks associated with project & less social & environment risks.

Based on the above assessment, the maximum risk category is Low-Moderate and therefore, the risk category for the overall project is moderate. A summary of the risk as follows:

1. Demand risk and market risk in the textile sector which may slow down investment
2. Fulfillment of demand with changing attitude towards support & priority for EE in textiles
3. Macroeconomic risks like exchange rate fluctuations
4. Social unrest affecting BBLU scenario
5. Delays in project implementation due to time consuming process of review and clearance as well on ground operational issues
6. Potential technological risks like equipment failure & supply chain failure
7. Safety risk to equipment and employees.
8. Lack of awareness and knowledge about the benefits of energy conservation
9. Social risks like exclusion of social groups and indigenous communities from project benefits
10. Gender risks regarding non-access to project benefits
11. Environment risks caused due to inadequate disposal of waste generated in textile units
12. Health Risks occurring from the processes in textile units

### Selected Risk Factor 1:

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit/Credit | Low/Low | Medium/Medium |

Demand risk and market risk in the textile sector which may slow down manufacturing, marketing and profit making and fail to attract investment

Mitigation Measure(s):

While chances of demand and market risks are low given the potential role of Bangladesh's textile sector on National and International clothing sector.

IDCOL will take mitigation measures such as collateral loans for distributing loans to end consumers and also providing access of energy efficient equipment finance if required.


### Selected Risk Factor 2:

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit/Credit | Low/Low | Low/Low |

Description

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 131 OF 155

## Mitigation Measure(s)

### Selected Risk Factor 3
**Category:** Medium/Medium  
**Probability:** Select  
**Impact:** Select  

#### Description
Significant foreign exchange risk at the time of release of GCF fund in future as borrowing from GCF will be in foreign currency (USD) and so lending to the end user in local currency (BDT).

### Mitigation Measure(s)
GCF will provide USD loans to BDT borrowers, thereby enabling borrowers in local currency i.e. BDT.

Considering the trend of 2.94% yearly devaluation rate of BDT against USD for the last few years due to a number of factors including global and domestic economic conditions, exchange rate risk is very high and may wipe out the concessional benefits of this program if end borrowers have to bear the currency fluctuation cost then sub-projects financed under this program will not be viable anymore. The absence of long-term hedging instruments in BDT market makes it difficult for the borrower to hedge against such risks.

This risk can be addressed by partly lending in USD to the borrowers thereby reducing their BDT exposure and also, having currency risk fluctuation premium as part of BDT interest rate. As Executing Entity for this project, we will bear the FX risk.

### Selected Risk Factor 4
**Category:** Technical and operational/Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Low/Low  
**Impact:** Low/Low  

#### Description
Social unrest caused due to external influences leading to disruption in the BAU scenario for textile sector

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Social instability caused from external influences can be mitigated through involvement and healthy relations with the NGOs, social welfare groups and associations working together for worker's development and welfare.

Compared with the programs, GCF will foster a healthy social relationship, ensure social participation and empowerment and reduce the likelihood of unrest in the associated community. The end borrowers of this fund will have the sole responsibility of mitigating this risk.

### Selected Risk Factor 5
**Category:** Technical and operational/Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Low/Low  
**Impact:** Low/Low  

#### Description

Companies who have past experience of successful operations in Bangladesh as service provider through the project will be considered. The supplier of the EE technology and spare parts required to maintain it can be procured from any of these reputed manufacturer / suppliers at discretion of end user customer. A qualified service technician for the operation & maintenance of the EE technology will be ensured through trainings and other initiatives.

It's mandated to ensure compliance with the following laws and regulations for requisite avoidance, mitigation and compensation measures: National Labor Laws and Policies 72) register with Accord on Fire and Building Safety in Bangladesh (Accord); the Alliance for Bangladesh Worker Safety (Alliance) The National Action Plan on Fire Safety and Workplace Integrity (NAPW) or any other regulatory requirement set by the Government as and when required.

Supervision of these will significantly reduce risks of accidents, injuries and incidents of fire and other occupational damages. Compliance with Regulatory Standards of the Ministry of Labour and Employment & ICDCL EHS policies and guidelines & clear contractual rules covering the need for occupational health and safety technical activities will be monitored.

## Description

**Lack of capacity in equipment operation and maintenance (O&M):** If penetration of advanced EE equipment is low, adequately trained personnel for O&M of machine would be low. Also, availability of spares may be low and cost of spares high. Hence, overall cost of O&M would be high increasing skepticism of potential borrower and reducing uptake of EE equipment.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

- Technical Assistance (TA) activity designed to develop capacity within set of RMU units in O&M of energy efficient equipment and continued use of energy efficient equipment in targeted units under the programme will help create real-life demonstration of the benefits as well as prompt other parties to take up similar measures.

## Selected Bank
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit/Credit | Medium/Medium | High/High |

### Description
Default in re-payment: Even though RMG sector has been witnessing three-year average credit growth of over 15% since 2016*, credit risk in the sector continues to be high. Furthermore, sector is plagued by long-term debt and liquidity constraints. The prolonged and continued case of defaults may lead to reduced access of banks in lending to RMG sector units.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
As part of TA activity, templates for verifying energy and cost savings project in the loan application will be prepared. This could help in establishing financial health of a borrowing entity, will help in assessing repayment capacity and continued case of defaults by ensuring energy audit and other entities to monitor project progress; templates for which have been prepared as part of prior preparatory activities.

## Selected Risk Factor 9
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Foreign/Forex | Low/Low | High/High |

### Description
Foreign exchange fluctuation: LFs will be receiving funds from GCF in USD. In case it chooses to extend loans to RMG units in BDT, it would be exposed to forex rate fluctuation risk as repayment from the borrowing unit to the LF would be in BDT and bank would be repaying the amount to GCF in USD; in case of fluctuation in forex rates, banks would have to repay more or less than original amount received.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Since program design leads to lower risk rating, it is recommended to ensure that structure of loan documentation is reviewed during program implementation

## Selected Risk Factor 9
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Government/Governance | Medium/Medium | High/High |

### Description
Limited capacity in LFs due diligence of energy efficiency project: Under current scenario, bank officials have limited technical knowledge about energy efficiency; the requirements for energy efficient equipment, assessment of cost savings realized by installing such equipment and how the savings affects loan repayment capacity. Hence, limited capacity can lead to increased assessment of borrowers and hence increase risk of loan repayment.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
A training module on necessary information gap among the LFs and accordingly develop training material to bridge the knowledge gap. The knowledge material can be disseminated through workshops and seminars. In addition, a set of standard operating procedures and technical standards documents will be prepared which will help establish the rules for engagement of the borrower and the LFs. This will help create the necessary framework which can be adopted by the LFs.

Long time in loan disbursement: To design an energy efficiency project, an independent energy audit required where entire process may take considerable time, spanning into 7-8 months in worst case scenario. Under such circumstances, the premise under which the loan proposal was prepared might change and the project might not be feasible anymore. Energy savings through this project would be discouraged from pursuing such projects.

In order to ensure a smooth turn-around of the loan proposal, evaluation of the proposal will be conducted by the EEs.
