# Environmental Audit

An environmental audit was conducted on one of the textile factories to establish the past performance, environmental issues or non-compliances if any. It assessed the entire processing area and the area under which the factory is located. The audit covered all aspects including water use, air emissions, solid waste and emissions, as well as light, sound and ventilation were covered under this audit. Furthermore, the audit included assessment of energy conservation and energy efficiency opportunities at the facility.

# Documents to be developed during programme implementation

Site-specific screening check-lists and Environmental and Social Management/Intervention Plans (ESMP) based on the findings from the environmental impact assessment works will be implemented, publicly consulted and disclosed locally before procurement commences for the civil works.

## Key Environmental and Social Risks have been presented in detail with respective and highly specific mitigation measures in Section F. To summarise, the relevant risks and mitigation measures are:

### Discrimination/exclusion

Social exclusion may happen due to multiple reasons like structural/economic (iniquitous economic conditions leading to poverty), social (discrimination, lack of access to education and employment), discrimination, absence of legal recognition, institutional/non-acceptance, self-exclusion, etc. These can be managed by complying with social regulatory frameworks in the country, sectoral mandates, DCGLs, ESFs, ESS, GEF’s ESF and programme ESMP.

### Gender-based discrimination/exclusion

Gender-disaggregated data will be used for monitoring outputs, outcomes and impacts during project implementation. This will ensure equal participation in employment opportunities in the textile sector. Hence the gender issues and mitigation is addressed in almost all the programme design documents namely: ESMP, Gender assessment and action plan, Logical Framework (Performance indicators) and the MRE Report.

### Occupational safety

Each factory was created to enable working in a fearless environment for concerns like fires, building collapses, or other accidents that could be prevented with reasonable health and safety measures. Besides, DCGLs, ESS, GEF’s ESF and the programme ESMP developed specifically for this case, identify potential hazards and recommend appropriate control measures and thereby avoid the financial costs of accidents & occupational ill-health.

### Waste management

Under Section 12 of the Bangladesh Environment Conservation Act 1995 (ECA 1995) no industrial unit or project can be established or undertaken without obtaining an Environmental Clearance Certificate (ECC). Hence, each factory will have to obtain an ECC. ESS should be made to ensure compliance with the regulations associated to solid waste management and Bangladesh Standards and Guidelines for Sludge Management and other related guidelines.

### Health

Health-related risks will be minimal as the certified, user-friendly energy-efficient equipment will reduce exposure of workers to health hazards as they minimise handling of chemicals, heat, dust with automated machines. This will be a low-level risk with least chances of occurrence.

# Institutional arrangement

The textile factories (EEIs) with the assistance of the Project Management team from IDCOL (AE), will be responsible for ensuring sound environmental performance of the contractor in charge of construction

# Environmental Audit

In order to understand environmental and social risks at program level, survey was conducted for 20 RMG units and further supported by survey of 150 households located within the selected REE units. Under the guidance of working group, health and safety, socio-economic factors were assessed along with interviews with unit workers and facility managers. This formed the basis for prioritization.

## Documents to be developed during programme implementation

### Site-specific screening check-lists and Environmental and Social Management/ Mitigation Plans (ESMPs), based on the ESMP, will be prepared for each project site, publicly consulted and disclosed locally before project implementation.

## Management of key environmental and social risks

Key Environmental and Social Risks have been presented in detail along with their respective mitigation measures in Section 2.1 to turn around identified environmental and social risks and mitigation measures are:

### 1. Discrimination/exclusion of social groups

Introduction of energy efficient equipment is independent of which social group is involved in GAO. Also, impact is medium since the equipment is not a new technology and hence, the training required for operating the equipment, is readily available in Bangladesh. Hence, overall risk arising from this is low.

### 2. Gender based discrimination/exclusion

Introduction of energy efficient equipment may require updating for female workers in the Garments Sector as they are less likely to be literate. Given this risk, capacity building activities are planned specifically for women workers which will disseminate relevant information for operating new energy efficient equipment and use of reduced power.

### 3. Occupational safety

Introduction of new machinery can lead to deviation from existing operations of workers and organizations may not have the ability to lead to related safety risks. This can not only lead to injury of concerned worker, but also unrest among worker groups or loss of life in the extreme case, causing high to medium levels of risk. Hence, it is recommended that GAO should provide training for new energy efficient equipment workers group to ensure they get acquainted with new equipment and gain knowledge on implications of implementation.

### 4. Waste Disposal

Lack of regulatory bindings on disposal of inefficient equipment would mean unit owners will adopt the least cost method of disposal, which may not be environment friendly. Accordingly, ESMP provides guidance on safe disposal of all waste arising from project activities, thereby minimizing environmental damage.

### 5. Health Risks

Impact of energy efficient RMG equipment do not contain harmful or hazardous material that can potentially harm workers. Difference in generation of noise between efficient and inefficient equipment is also low. Hence, it is perceived that no health risks will occur as a result of the program design.

## Institutional arrangement

A dedicated ESMP management is provided where specialist in procurement, social development, financial management, environment etc., will support the project director and the project management unit to ensure implementation of ESMP. In addition to this, ESMP management will be responsible for ESMP monitoring and evaluation, specifically capacity building programmes have been proposed as part of the program design. Also, disclosure of ESMP documents as well as periodic compliance monitoring reports is

## FORMULATION OF A GENDER BALANCED PROJECT COMMITTEE WITH WOMEN PARTICIPATING IN DECISION-MAKING THROUGH TBOCL, A PROGRAMME MANAGEMENT TEAM AND TEXTILE FACTORIES

The programme's M&E scheme will measure outputs and track progress of gender-specific activities through monitoring and reporting the achievements towards mid-term and final targets for each activity with a specific performance indicator and means of verification. Timely reporting will facilitate evaluation of the performance of gender-based programme activities.

### FOR COMPONENT 03 & 04 (EEAC IN RMG SECTOR):

The gender assessment was conducted by examining the engagement of men and women in the RMG sector in Bangladesh, labor force participation, access and control to technology and financial resources, as well as employment conditions. Such analyses were explored into a plethora of factors namely Socio-Economic; Health & Safety; sanitation; working hours; wages; training and skill development; etc., to assess the magnitude of adversities based on gender and the precise requirements necessary to mitigate them.

Primary data was collected through industrial surveys, household surveys, Key Informant Interviews (KII), ten focus group discussions (FGD) with women workers in RMG factories, and secondary data through relevant reports, about national policies, guidelines and statistics (environmental, social, gender). Documents published by the government which focused upon potential risks identified in the RMG sector; historical studies on gender equality in Bangladesh; and international best practices were also reviewed to identify the international best practices, considering gender equity to identify and scrutinize into the gap prevalent in terms of gender considerations in the sector.

### FINDINGS:

The findings derived from the gender assessment emphasized upon the discrepancies prevailing within the RMG sector which essentially includes male-female employment ratio, wage discrimination, employment conditions, access to technology and financial resources, working hours, training and skill development. A trainings and basic facilities, welfare provision, social protection and freedom of association, collective bargaining and social dialogue as well as playing a decision making role at the managerial level.

The women labor force have been in the forefront of Bangladesh's rapid economic and industrial growth. They make up 70% of the workforce in the textile made-up goods (TMG) sector; the country's flagship export-oriented industry. However, even though the RMG sector has played a tremendous role for women employment, it is still lagging behind.

From data collected within factories, 4 out of every 5 production line workers are female, whilst just over 1 in 20 supervisors is a woman. This is mainly due to lack of education, trainings, technical knowledge and social responsibilities of women towards their families. Also, women are engaged more in sewing or as helpers rather than in supervisory roles. A large number of women are seen in RMG units working in cutting, quality control or supervising compared to men.

Numerous surveys (both industrial and household surveys) under the PPI project reveal that within export-based factories, although there is no formal discrimination in theory on equal treatment – women are forced to do overtime more than the men and the cut from the overtime allowance is more for women workers than men. Women workers face severe discrimination with regard to the scope of promotion and their career prospects is limited.


---
**Page 150**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.03 | PAGE 147 OF 155

## Participation of women is increasing in the Bangladesh labor market, but socio-cultural norms envisage that women and men's position is not equal. Women tend to be concentrated in lower-paying, low-skilled sectors in the garment industry, and within it, are often stuck in lower paying occupations such as sewing machine operators and helpers.

Do women have access to training? Do men’s aspirations, preferences and capabilities, employers’ perceptions of women’s and men’s skills and attitudes are affected. Thus, majority of women are not privileged enough to avail accessible credit to enhancing their skilled and technical knowledge through trainings and workshops. Lack of awareness about the importance of education for girls, lack of access to social and work place safety, maternity and pregnancy issues, unwillingness to work long hours/overtime, lack of transport facilities and the care burden all contribute to the fact of significantly limited presence of women engaged in more sophisticated employment positions such as managers/supervisors in the RMG sector.

The energy efficiency component procured under this program will be the subject matter of the loan financing. Thus the procurement will be carried out by the end borrower for their respective projects as per the business as usual practice. Along with the disbursement request/procurement application, the end borrower will also submit a list of all the procurements that have been made under GCF funding procurement under the programme. These will be screened by iDCOLFLFs to approve the loan application.

After the disbursement of GCF fund as per the implementation arrangement in section B4, iDCOLFLF is required to ensure that the proceeds of the funding are efficiently used for intended purposes only and in accordance with GCF and Gold Standard rules and procedures (Annex 14).

The internal audit division, reporting directly to the Board, will add value through an independent appraisal of all iDCOL's financial operations and activities. The results of such appraisals will be reported to the Board on a regular basis. This will ensure that all financial transactions that will ensure achieving GCF’s corporate objectives. Moreover, iDCOL’s financial statements are audited externally by a qualified, internationally recognized auditing firm, in accordance with international standards.

As per the AMA, the procurement of goods and services for funded activities, whether by the Accredited Entity or by the end borrower, shall be carried out in accordance with the applicable rules, policies and procedures of the Accredited Entity i.e. iDCOL to the extent and scope of its Accreditation. Thus iDCOL will follow its own “Procurement Policy” for the operations of this program. Procurements made under this program are subject to the same requirements as those set out in accordance with the policy.

### G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme documents are subject to confidentiality unless otherwise specified by the Board.

Subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP, information provided as part of a funding proposal will be made public on the GCF website. The Board may specify in this document if the document contains specific segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full to the GCF as no information is being provided in confidence.
With confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
- Full version for use within the GCF
- Redacted version with a cover letter together with an explanatory note regarding the said portion and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accreditation process.
redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

**Secretary**

Economic Affairs Division

Ministry of Finance

Government of Bangladesh

D.O.:785/03-14/0000/105.05.021.17-P1

14 October 2020

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by ICDCL regarding the programme "Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sectors of Bangladesh"

Dear Sir,

We refer to the programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sections of Bangladesh” in included in the funding proposal submitted by ICDCL as on 30 October 2020.

The undersigned Fatima Yasmin, the duly authorized representative of the National Designated Authority of Bangladesh.

Pursuant to the terms of BIDCL’s letter dated 14 September 2020, the copy of which we acknowledge to have received, we hereby communicate our objection to the programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sections of Bangladesh” as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our objection, it is intended:

(a) The government of Bangladesh has acquiescence to the programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sections of Bangladesh” as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The government of Bangladesh has acquiescence to the programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sections of Bangladesh” as included in the funding proposal, subject to the following conditions, priorities, strategies, and plans;

(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sections of Bangladesh” as included in the funding proposal is not consistent with the GCF’s policies.

We also confirm that our national process for acquisicing no-objection to the programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sections of Bangladesh” as included in the funding proposal has not been followed.

We are certain that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme, including the textile sector project and activities and garment sector projects and activities.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

Fatima Yasmin

Executive Director

Green Climate Fund

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP150

## Proposal name: Promoting private sector investment through large-scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Transport in Bangladesh

Accredited entity: Infrastructure Development Company Limited (IDCOL)

Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium/High

1. **Bangladesh**, in South Asia, forms the larger and eastern part of the Bengal region. It is situated in one of the largest deltas in the world, formed by a dense network of the distributaries of the rivers Ganges, Brahmaputra and Meghna, and more than 230 major rivers and their tributaries. The country has a total land area of about 147,570 km², with over half being low-lying flat land. With a population exceeding 166.5 million, Bangladesh is ranked as one of the most densely populated countries in the world.
2. Although its status is being reviewed, Bangladesh is currently regarded as a least developed country. The country's economy recorded remarkable growth since 2010 as a result of strong export performance and increased foreign direct investment (FDI). Real gross domestic product (GDP) grew from 7.3 per cent in 2010 to 8.2 per cent in 2019.
3. Bangladesh is frequently cited as one of the countries most vulnerable to climate change because of its low-lying coastal location, high population density, and low elevation from the sea, among other things. The country experiences severe climate change impacts such as higher summer temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, floods, droughts, coastal and riverbank erosion, and salinity intrusion along the coast.
4. Although the total greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions of Bangladesh amount to less than 0.35 per cent of global GHG emissions in 2018, its annual growth rate of carbon dioxide equivalent emissions have grown by 6.8 per cent since 2000. GHG emissions per capita increased from 9 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) to 14 CO2e between 2005 and 2018, with an annual growth rate of 6 per cent. The increase was driven by rapid industrial expansion, and growth of energy consumption.
5. Bangladesh is the world's second-largest apparel producer after China. Textile industries are one of the fastest growing and among the most important industries of the country, employing over four million people or six per cent of the country's industrial employment.
6. The industrial sector in Bangladesh accounts for 47.8 per cent of commercial energy consumption. However, the overall efficiency of this sector is not efficient as it uses outdated or poorly maintained machines and technology. The inefficiency in energy use is assessed to be due to inadequate financial incentives, and lack of


---
**Page 163**
---

technical expertise. If the economic growth persists with the current industrial energy intensity, Bangladesh could face difficulties in managing the rising energy demand and consequently is achieving its GHG emission reduction targets under the Paris Agreement.

Although energy-saving opportunities are present across the textile industry sectors, the textile subsector (cutting, sewing, dyeing, ironing, and finishing) in particular are considered to have substantial potential for energy savings. The textile subsector accounts for 15 per cent of total industrial energy demand in Bangladesh. The textile sector accounts for 60 per cent with its demand representing 32.4 per cent of the industry sector.

Investment in energy efficiency is seen as a key opportunity for EE and RME sectors as an additional financial burden, which is currently underdeveloped especially in EE and RME sectors. The feasibility study conducted for the textile sector suggests that the larger textile facilities are more likely to adopt EE technologies than smaller ones due to their economies of scale advantages, with efficiency differences being as high as 50 per cent in some cases. In the RME sector, small factories are more likely to adopt EE technologies and are expected to demonstrate increasing short-term profits than increasing productivity and profits in the long run.

The proposed programme “Promoting private sector investment through large-scale adoption of energy-efficient technologies and renewable energy sources (EE and Renewable Energy (RME) sectors of Bangladesh)” offers concessional financing to local financial institutions to on-lend private sector EE and RME projects. The project will provide technical assistance for technology for replacement or expansion with the aim of reducing GHG emissions from the subsectors, and ultimately from Bangladesh. The programme period is 20 years including an implementation period of five years.

IDCOL of Bangladesh is the accredited entity (AE). In addition to IDCOL, four local financial institutions have been identified as potential lenders: Dhaka Bank Limited, United Bank Limited, BDB Finance Limited, and South East Bank Limited. The Sustainable and Renewable Energy Development Authority (SREDA) under Ministry of Power, Energy & Mineral Resources will be the implementing agency.

The programme is comprised of five components: two funding components and three technical assistance (TA) components:

Component 1 amounts to USD 133.8 million for energy-saving equipment and technologies for the textile sector. Under component 1, IDCOL, as the EE, will make GCF funds available to local financial institutions on a non-reimbursable basis under concessional terms together with its own contribution of USD 30.0 million, which IDCOL would provide from its own account or mobilize from other sources. Reimbursements will be made from IDCOL in USD for the repayment of principal and interest to local financial institutions and technologies. Technical eligibility criteria require a 15 per cent energy-saving potential for the equipment and technologies proposed under component 1, as well as inclusion on manufacturers’ catalogues or certifications, or energy auditors’ reports. Component 1 will finance up to five EE projects with an estimated cost of USD 20 million. The maximum amount of a single loan is set at USD 20 million.

In component 2, TA amounts to USD 35.9 million to be provided from GCF to support IDCOL in the implementation of component 1. This will help IDCOL to establish an implementation strategy and build the capacity of IDCOL staff for efficient adoption of energy-efficient technologies as well as effective monitoring, reporting and verification of energy


---
**Page 164**
---

## Component 3 provides USD 200 million of financing for energy-saving equipment and technology upgrades in the RMG sector.

As EEs, the four LFs will use the GCF concessional funding in both USD to private sector RMG businesses for investment in energy-saving equipment and technology upgrades. The private sector RMG businesses are required to co-fund this loan by providing an additional USD 50 million on their own account in both USD and local currency in total. Technical eligibility will be confirmed by the LFs based on manufacturer's catalogues or certifications, or energy audits reports.

## Component 4 provides USD 23.8 million to support the lending activities of the four LFs in component 1, and to promote energy efficiency investments in the RMG sector.

This component includes support for the EEs to undertake preparatory work for implementing energy efficiency measures and conservation in both the EE and private sector RMG sectors. Technical support for the EEs and private sector entities is also provided for monitoring, reporting, verification, capacity building, and dissemination of best practices and lessons learned. In addition, this component envisages a technology fair to be held within three years of the start of implementation of energy efficiency measures and conservation technology.

## Component 5 in TA amounts to USD 1.15 million to strengthen the regulatory and institutional framework for promoting energy efficiency and conservation in both EE and private sector RMG sectors by supporting the development of policies, strategies, and capacity building activities conducive to the promotion of energy efficiency in Bangladesh. It further includes capacity building on the use of international best practices and methodologies for energy efficiency measures and financing.

## The programme requires total financing of USD 340.5 million, of which GCF is required to provide a USD 250.6 million loan and USD 6.48 million TA grant. The balance of USD 84.02 million will be provided by the four LFs (USD 79.1 million in concessional funding from each kind), the four LFs (USD 50.0 million loan) and SREDA (USD 20.0 million in-kind contribution).

The GCF loan has a term of 20 years including a 5-year grace period.

## The estimated total cost of energy efficiency measures is USD 164.8 million of GHG emissions in total over the 20-year lifetime of the investment by offering financial and technical support to RMG businesses for energy-saving equipment and technology upgrades, which can create investment opportunities for energy-saving technology upgrades. In that estimation, 13.1 MtCO₂eq or 76 per cent of the programme's target GHG emission reduction will be achieved in the RMG sector.

The estimation is made taking into account the emission factors of the major sources of energy consumption and the estimated energy efficiency measures and conservation savings that can be achieved by introducing energy efficiency technologies. The All further estimates that 50 per cent of the total funding would be used by large factories. The rest would be directly provided to small and medium-sized enterprises.


---
**Page 165**
---

20. In response to a question from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) on treatment of the existing equipment replaced with energy-efficient equipment funded by the programme, the AE clarified that the replaced equipment might be result in the market if it is still considered economically viable and has not reached its end-of-life. The AE also noted that account the estimated GHG emissions that are likely to continue if the replaced equipment, which is assumed to have a machine life of 10–15 years, is not replaced with energy-efficient equipment. It assumes that the existing equipment has a machine life of 10-15 years and could be replaced and recal read at ports-in-S. Similarly, the programme would be used for >5 years by the new buyer before being sold to S. According to AE, the ongoing emissions from the existing equipment are expected to be 1.5 MCO2-eq in the textile and RMG sectors, respectively.

21. Assuming that the programme will deliver 40% of the energy efficiency potential within the programme is expected to deliver amount to 16.5 MCO2-eq, of which saving in the textile sector is 4.8 MCO2-eq (72 per cent), and that of the RMG sector is 18.3 MCO2-eq (72.4 per cent).

22. The TAP assesses the impact potential of the programme to be medium/high.

### Paradigm shift potential

#### Scale: Medium/High

##### Potential for scaling up and replication

The AE noted that GCF will require concessional financing and technical support to IBCLD, and the four LFs to support investments in energy efficiency in the sectors. Long-term concessional financing will be required from GCF so that loans from IDBG, and the four LFs to the sectoral banks can be provided at concessional rates. The amount of concessional component can be at par with that of conventional equipment when financed with ordinary market-priced loans. In this case, the cost of energy-efficient equipment is expected to be 25 per cent higher than conventional equipment.

The TAP considers that the programme scalability and an largely dependent on securing concessional financing. The scalability of energy efficiency investments cannot be established in the absence of concessional financing. The textile and RMG sectors are dominated by small-scale enterprises, which generally do not have the capacity to operate energy efficiently. Therefore, if the programme is to upscale, the funding requirement would be substantial. As such, the potential for scaling up and replication will be limited unless a large amount of concessional financing is secured.

##### Potential for knowledge and learning

The TA components of the programme will contribute to knowledge creation and dissemination about energy-efficient equipment technologies and their benefits for the textile and RMG sectors in Bangladesh. The lessons learned from the pilot activities will be accumulated and shared through consultations and, more importantly, through the ongoing activities to be carried out with the assistance of consultants during implementation.

##### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The programme provides components that will help to build the energy efficiency market for the textile and RMG sectors in Bangladesh. The loan-disbursement support (create consultant) component is expected to provide a strong push towards the uptake of energy-efficient equipment and technologies. Technology fairs are planned in the programme design


---
**Page 166**
---

## Contribution to the regional framework and policies

### 1.2.4 Contribution of the programme to framework and policies

The programme promotes capacity building of the policymakers in fields related to energy efficiency in industry, especially in the textile and RMG sectors in Bangladesh.

Component 5 of the programme is to be implemented jointly by AE and SREDA offers technical assistance to the government for formulating sector-specific action plans, guidelines, and strategies conducive to promoting energy efficiency, as well as training of government officials and other stakeholders.

The TAP views the paradigm shift potential of the programme as medium/high.


## 1.3 Sustainable development potential: Scale: Medium/High

### 1.3.1 Environment co-benefits

#### 1.3.1.1 Environment co-benefits

The programme will promote reduction in the use of fossil fuels (natural gas, diesel and coal) as a primary energy source for power generation in Bangladesh.

Energy consumption in the textile and RMG sectors is skewed towards use of natural gas and diesel as fuel for power generation due to limited availability of grid electricity. Although large factories are now using natural gas to fulfill their power supply needs, small RMG factories rely heavily on diesel generators to fulfill their power requirements. The share of natural gas has increased significantly by 2.440 per cent, while the shares of natural gas and grid electricity have declined by 17.583 and 7.75 per cent, respectively.


### 1.3.2 Economic co-benefits

The programme will help the industry to improve production and capacity utilization and reduced production costs, which will lead to improved productivity and competitiveness.

The industrial sector is a major contributor to GDP (40 per cent) and employment in Bangladesh’s export. It also generates more than 65 per cent of the country's industrial employment. Employment in the RMG industry is concentrated in small-scale industries because 99 per cent of ancillary activities such as dyeing, printing and manufacturing zippers and labels are carried out locally.

The textile and RMG sectors have a significant potential for energy efficiency improvement by enhancing energy efficiency in the industry which contributes more than 13 per cent to the nation's GDP. The sector is also the largest sector of employment. Although the industry has a workforce of over two million people, most of them are engaged in small-scale units and hence generate are likely be indirect. As such, the TAP considers the sustainable development potential of the programme as medium/high.


---
**Page 167**
---

1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

### Economic and social development level of the country and affected population

- Bangladesh remains a least developed country although its economy recorded remarkable growth since 2010 as a result of strong private consumption and investment.
- Bangladesh is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change, especially in terms of coastal erosion and sea-level rise. It is listed in the top 10 most affected countries of the world in the Climate Risk Index for 2017.
- The topography of Bangladesh consists mainly of lowlands with a few hills in the north-eastern region at an elevation of about 35 meters above sea level. All land types except highlands are exposed to monsoon flooding for part or all of the year. Floodplains, including the Ganges and Brahmaputra river basins, central, south-eastern and north-eastern regions are subject to regular flooding at different times during the year due to heavy rainfall, cyclones and storm surges.
- It is estimated that the impact of climate change could result in an annual loss of 2 per cent of GDP by 2050 and 9.4 per cent of GDP by 2100.

### Alternative sources of financing

- The programme requires total financing of USD 367 million, of which a USD 200 million loan will be provided on concessional terms to be received from GCF.
- The GCF loans will be priced at a concessional rate and have a tenor of 20 years including a 5-year grace period.

### Public sector investments in Bangladesh are presently focused more on supply-side interventions, such as power generation and transmission. However, there is limited access to financial resources available to drive public sector investments for demand-side interventions, especially towards energy efficiency and conservation.

- Under the current market scenario, the industry may access commercial credit at an interest rate of 9–15 per cent based on creditworthiness and cost history.
- Although bank lending has been increasing in recent years, it is still not sufficient to meet the demand for financing as a high-risk credit. There are several loan facilities for energy efficiency and conservation promoted by various financial institutions but the lack of time required for approval have hampered smooth implementation of loan projects.

### The TAP notes that alternative sources of financing are not currently available, especially in the first two terms.

### Needs of other financing institutions and implementing capacity

- Although the Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 (2015) prepared by SREDA and the Power Division under the Ministry of Power, Energy and Mineral Resources confirms the potential and importance of energy efficiency in the industry and building sector, there is a lack of institutional capacity for promoting energy efficient equipment and technologies has been low in Bangladesh due to lack of concessional funding. The TAP notes that there are no dedicated institutions or agencies which act only for financial institutions and industry but also for policymakers in fields related to energy efficiency in industry and buildings.
- The TAP identifies the need for strengthening institutional capacity, including for preparing policies and strategies conducive to promoting energy efficiency, as well as training of government officials.

- The TAP assesses the needs of the recipient of the programme as high.


---
**Page 168**
---

1. **Country ownership**

   - **Existence of a national climate strategy**
     * Bangladesh submitted its intended nationally determined contribution to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in September 2015.
     * Bangladesh ratified the Paris Agreement on April 22, 2016. Its Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) can now be found on the UNFCCC’s interim NDC registry.

   - **Implementation of climate change policies through a progressive approach to developing its economy on a low-carbon pathway**
     * In the INDC Bangladesh committed to reducing GHG emissions in the power, industry and transport sectors by 15 per cent below business as usual (BAU) by 2030.
     * The energy sector is expected to contribute about 48.6 per cent of total emissions; the power sector accounts for 32.9 per cent, and transportation accounts for 17.5 per cent.

   - **Bangladesh’s NDC identified three key sectors: the industrial sector accounts for 40.6 per cent of total emissions; the power sector accounts for 32.9 per cent, and transport accounts for 17.5 per cent**
     * The energy efficiency and conservation master plan (EECM) is one of the three key mitigation interventions together with renewable energy expansion, and transportation infrastructure modernization.
     * This has been embedded in the country’s 5-year planning process.

   - **Coherence with existing policies**
     * The Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2018 (ECP) contemplates a programme of energy efficiency and building energy efficiency across all domestic buildings in industrial and building sectors.
     * This programme contributes directly to the government’s target to achieve a 10 per cent reduction in energy consumption by the industry sector by 2030, and 20 per cent for the power sector.

   - **Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver**
     * IDCOL is the AE of this programme. It is also the EoF of all the financial and technical components except component 3 where the four LFs extend loans to the RMG sector.
     * IDCOL will extend loans to the RMG sector through its own credit line for funding under the programme as per the criteria set prior to loan extension.

   - **IDCOL is a non-banking institution established in 1997 and is owned by the Government of Bangladesh. Its mandate is to provide financial support to the private sector in promotion, development and financing of infrastructure, as well as renewable energy and energy efficiency projects in a sustainable manner through public-private partnership initiatives**

   - **The financial performance of IDCOL is compatible with or better than other non-banking institutions in Bangladesh**
     * The total outstanding loan portfolio of IDCOL increased from BDT 35.7 billion Bangladeshi taka (BTD) in 2015 to BDT 90.876 billion in 2019 (i.e., by 35.7 per cent) while its loan book grew from BDT 4,324 million in 2015 to BDT 11,378 million in the same period.

     * It is rated AA by Credit Rating Agency of Bangladesh.
     * The IDCOL loan portfolio in 2018 included renewable energy projects (32.6 per cent) and infrastructure (including energy efficiency) projects (66.9 per cent).

1. **Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders**

   * As part of the project development, the GCF carried out consultation workshops for key stakeholders including government agencies, financial institutions, representatives from RMG companies, academia, and civil society organizations. Reports of the consultation workshops are available on the GCF website.
   * A no-objection letter issued by the Ministry of Finance, Government of Bangladesh, the GCF national designated authority, is attached to the funding proposal.

2. **Efficiency and effectiveness**

   * As per the AE’s projection, the programme intends to deliver total GHG emission savings of 14.5 MTCO2eq. Accordingly, the cost of GHG emission reductions per tCO2eq is estimated to be USD 2.4 against the total cost of funding of USD 340.5 million.
   * The cost varies between the textile and RMG sectors: USD 18.3 for tCO2eq and USD 19.2 per tCO2eq, respectively, against the programme funding. With respect to GCF funding, it is estimated at USD 25.4 and USD 14.5 per tCO2eq for the textile and RMG sectors, respectively.

3. **Amount of co-financing**

   * The co-financing ratio of GCF financing is estimated to be 0.33.
   * The programme requires a total financing of USD 340.5 million, of which USD 256.8 million is provided by GCF in the form of loan and USD 83.7 million as grant. The remaining amount will be co-financed through the LFs and SREIBs in the form of a loan and in-kind contributions.
   * The total amount of funding to be mobilized for purchase of energy-efficient equipment is expected to be USD 105.4 million, which includes USD 83.7 million from GCF, and four BSRB borrowers to provide equal amounts of funds to their loans. Accordingly, an additional USD 83.0 million can be mobilized through the co-financing ratio contribution. If that amount is included, then the co-financing ratio goes up to 0.65.

4. **Programme/project financial viability and other financial indicators**

   * The programme promotes investment in energy efficiency by providing concessional financing so that the total cost of investment, including equipment, financial, and operational costs are lower than those for conventional equipment.
   * The difference between the two is estimated to be USD 10.5 million or about 23 percent higher when compared with that of conventional equipment. On the basis of a survey, the average payback period for energy-efficient equipment was found to be 4 years as against 7 years than that of conventional equipment.
   * Therefore, the interest rate of the programme is expected to be lower by approximately 10 percentage points in order to make sure that the present values of investment in energy-efficient equipment and conventional equipment become comparable. The loan term will extend up to 10 years to help companies manage yearly cash outflow.

### Industry best practices

The programme will help the textile and BMG sectors to showcase the financial and operational benefits of energy-efficient equipment and technologies and establish energy-efficient practices in Bangladesh. The three technical components include assistance to the EEs and private sector companies to develop their own business cases for EE investments, a technology fair will be organized within three years of the start of implementation, to promote market access for EE products and services, and capacity building for government agencies will be supported so that challenges identified in the programme can be shared and lessons learned can be reflected in developing enabling policies and regulations for investments in energy efficiency.

The TAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness of the programmes as medium.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### (a) The AE shall monitor and report to GCF actual annual energy consumption of each piece of eligible energy-efficient equipment and technology which has been financed by the Programme, from the date of replacement or scrapping of the relevant equipment or technology; and

### (b) The AE shall monitor and report to GCF annual energy consumption of each piece of equipment and technology that has been replaced as a result of the Programme, from the date of replacement or scrapping of the relevant equipment or technology.

### (c) The sixth anniversary of the replacement; or

### (d) The relevant equipment or technology has been scrapped and retired from operation.


The programme offers concessional funding to finance energy efficiency investments for replacement and expansion in the textile and BMG sectors in Bangladesh. The AE assumes that replaced equipment will be replaced with more energy-efficient equipment, which is less efficient than the original equipment. Accordingly, there would be no leakage with respect to emission reductions. However, the AE notes that this assumption may not always hold true as it would not be always the case. Rather, the likely case is that replaced equipment would be purchased for other purposes and not scrapped or retired from operation. This could limit growth, rather than replacement, especially if textile and BMG factories have limited interest in energy efficiency.

The TAP sees a risk that the replaced equipment would be operated longer in total than its expected lifetime so the estimated impact on emission reductions could be larger than what is actually achieved. If this happens, then the actual emission reduction outcome may turn out to be less. To validate and confirm the leakage of emission reductions, it is imperative that actual energy consumption of all equipment replaced is actually monitored so that UCF can reasonably estimate the impact of the programme on emission reductions.

Without the strong monitoring plan, the TAP would recommend that the Board consider a request to the AE to ensure all of the replaced equipment to be scrapped and retired from operation.

# Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings (FP150)

## Proposal name: Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garments (TRG) sectors in Bangladesh

### Accredited entity: Infrastructure Development Company Limited (IDCOL)

#### Impact potential
As rightfully mentioned in the ITAP assessment that the textile sector (spinning, weaving, knitting and dyeing), and readymade garments (RMG) sectors are energy intensive industries and have substantial energy savings. The RMG sector is one of most energy-intensive in the industry consuming 15.4 per cent of the total industrial energy demand in Bangladesh. The textile sector follows the RMG sector in terms of energy consumption. IDCOL has been implementing a similar programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garments (TRG) sectors in Bangladesh” to achieve the Fund’s objective and result areas by contributing shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways. The contribution is achieved by providing target market i.e. Textile and RMG sector of Bangladesh with a range of energy efficient technologies, equipment and services which will result in 14,533 MTOE emissions savings as well as provide market participants with appropriate information on the benefits of adopting energy efficiency initiatives in Bangladesh, as well as to support implementation of energy efficiency initiatives.

#### Paradigm shift potential
The paradigm shift potential is rightly described in the ITAP assessment. IDCOL, as a government owned development financial institution will strongly support government’s target and vision to enable the industrial energy transition to low carbon energy systems and sectoral focus on energy efficiency. The success of the proposed programme will enable replication of similar programmes in other sectors of the country.

#### Sustainable development potential
IDCOL agrees with the remarks made by ITAP regarding the programme's sustainable development potential. The large-scale impact through this programme will have large and important development benefits whose impacts will increase over time as energy prices rise.

#### Needs of the recipient
Bangladesh is in dire need of concessional financing, capacity development and marketing activation to realize the potential of industrial energy efficiency. The need of proposed programme is indeed high, especially considering the severe impact that the manufacturing sector has faced due to impacts of COVID-19.

#### Country ownership

The proposed programme is regarded to be (a) well aligned with national development and climate priorities and (b) effective of the national priorities with a focus on appropriate action in the field of mitigation.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

As rightly mentioned in the ITAP assessment the proposed programme is designed to optimize the use of funding available in terms of leverage and sustainability, investing concessional resources optimally in bankable projects. The programme will also help to create a conducive environment for scaling up among the market actors. Furthermore, the programme will help the textile and RMG sectors to showcase financial and operational merits of energy efficiency equipment and technologies, and establish energy efficient practices in Bangladesh.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

The ITAP recommended in its assessment that IDCOL has to consider following two conditions:
1) The TF shall monitor and report to GCF actual annual energy consumption of each piece of eligible energy equipment or technology which has been financed by the Programme, for as long as any amount of the related sub-account is outstanding; and
2) The TF shall monitor and report to GCF actual annual energy consumption of each piece of equipment and technology which has been financed under this programme from the date it was replaced until:
a) the sixth anniversary of its replacement;
b) the relevant equipment or technology has been scrapped and retired from operation, whichever is earlier.

IDCOL's response:

IDCOL agrees to the above conditions and will work with GCF to implement a reporting system.

# Executive Summary

The ready-made garment (RMG) sector in Bangladesh plays a key role in the economic development of the country as one of the biggest industrial contributors to the country's gross domestic product and employment generation. The RMG sector is also an important source of export earnings and foreign exchange for the country, especially from the export of garments. Moreover, this sector is one of the major sources of employment for women in Bangladesh, especially for the poor and unprivileged women. Women were the main workforce in the RMG sector before the establishment of ready-made garment factories. The RMG sector has been a key enabler in overall socio-economic development of Bangladesh.

In common perspective, a majority of the 4-5 million people employed in the sector are women (90% of total employment). Women have been playing an important role in the RMG sector for decades and their contribution is increasing day by day. The employment of these women, mostly from rural areas, have provided them with financial freedom and greater social independence. Compared to other industries, women employment rate is higher in RMG sector in Bangladesh. So, for implementing any project in the RMG sector it is important to consider gender equality as a key factor while planning and designing the project interventions. The GCF also promotes environmental, social, economic and development co-benefits through taking a gender-sensitive approach. In this regard, the project team has conducted a Gender Assessment (GA) for the proposed project in Bangladesh during its preparation, implementation and monitoring stages. For this purpose, the study team conducted interviews with various stakeholders including women workers, supervisors, managers, key informants (women and men) and other relevant people to collect information from different categories of women and men. This tool also helped to promote gender – relevant entry points, policies and identify opportunities for enhancing gender equality in this project.

The objective of the Gender Assessment was to identify the gender aspects of the project activities, including selection of employees and their trainings, to collect information from various stakeholders such as workers, supervisors, managers, key informants (women and men) and other relevant people. The tool also helped to promote gender – relevant entry points, policies and identify opportunities for enhancing gender equality in this project.

Through this gender action plan the project aims to ensure that the services and technologies provided by the project/program will be available and accessible to both women and men, equal access to training and other development opportunities, and that there are no barriers or obstacles necessary to participate and benefit fully from the anticipated outcomes of the project and promote leadership capacity of women.

# Introduction

Improved energy efficiency in Bangladesh can lead to greater business growth and profitability at the local level; at the national level it supports GDP growth and enhances energy security, and at a global level it helps mitigate climate change and ensures sustainable global growth.

For industries such as garment manufacturing, textile production, and cement manufacturing, which collectively consume maximum energy. It also reduces their carbon footprint at no additional cost (an important consideration in the face of international trade agreements). The garment sector is one of Bangladesh's largest export earners, while the cement industry (manufacturing sector) consumes maximum energy. As garment and textile sectors consume maximum energy, it also holds a significant potential to reduce energy consumption.

Valuing these benefits, the government has decided to introduce energy efficiency technologies in the garment and textiles sectors with high savings potential starting utilising energy efficient technologies they will be poised to capture huge future economic opportunities. In addition to such economic benefits, adoption of energy efficient technologies can help significantly reduced carbon emissions, social impact by providing well-being and employment will create a more sustainable Bangladesh for future generations.

Based on the existing scenario of RMG sector in Bangladesh, it is evident that adoption of energy efficiency technologies can significantly improve socio-economic impact of the sector as well as reduce environmental impact. Infrastructure Development Company Limited (IDCOL) intends to maximise benefits by developing and implementing a program to support entrepreneurs and businesses in adopting energy efficient technologies and create new investment opportunities for energy saving technology upgrades. IDCOL has solicited financial support from the World Bank under its Energy Sector Support Program. This fund will be then utilised with concessional loans to finance garment business via four selected banks i.e., City Bank Ltd, Southeast Bank Ltd, BRAC Bank Ltd and ILD Finance Ltd.

Ready-made garment (RMG) sector in Bangladesh plays a significant role in the development of the country's economy. The RMG industry contributes to over 8% of Bangladesh's GDP. The RMG industry now contributes over 83% to Bangladesh's export income. Moreover, this sector is one of the major sources of employment for women in Bangladesh. In fact, it has been a key driver of economic development and poverty reduction by providing jobs to more than half of the total workforce in the country's apparel sector since the sector began its journey in the 1970s. The RMG sector has been a key enabler in overall socio-economic development of Bangladesh.

It is common knowledge that women are the backbone of this industry, with over 80% of the workers and more than 60% of the total workforce for the sector are women. The employment of these women, mostly from rural areas, have provided them with financial freedom and greater social independence. Compared to other industries, women employment rate in RMG is higher as compared to men. However, female participation has declined in recent years. According to a survey by Center for Policy Dialogue (CPD), released on March 2018, among garment workers 56.8% are female & 39.20% are male. The survey was conducted across four major RMG clusters of Bangladesh namely Savar, Gazipur, Sylhet and Chotogram. This situation has been created in the sector due to increasing use of technology.

**Page 179**
---

lack of skills of women workers compared to males, increasing interest of men for salary rise, social inequalities and due to labor-intensive nature of the sector. Even, the average age of women working in this sector is lower than that of men. In addition, there is discrimination in areas of service book, working hour including overtime, leaves (casual, sick, annual), separation benefits, pension, social protection like provident fund and group insurance, and representational rights.

After the Rana Plaza incident, more emphasis has been given on the compliance issues in the RMG sector. As all the owners are obliged to ensure health and safety issues for the workers and ensure that they follow the labor laws, there is a lot of pressure from the government on compliance issues while placing order and usually select those that are more complaint. However, due to lack of financial support, many small and medium enterprises often lag behind in terms of providing safe working conditions. Many women workers have to work with dependent children. So, with this programme there will be an opportunity for the RMG units to include better facilities for women and address compliance issues as well. This way they will also be able to attract more international buyers and access better opportunities for future development.

As such, gender equity is crucial to the sustainable development of all sectors of the economy. GCF also gives special attention on ensuring gender equity and addressing gender-specific climate change impacts and vulnerabilities faced by women and men. The GCF Governing Instrument states that: "The Fund will strive to maximize the impact of its funding for adaptation and mitigation projects, programmes and activities in order to achieve maximum co-benefits and taking a gender-sensitive approach." In this regard, this programme aims to assess gender dimensions in RMG sector in Bangladesh during project preparation, implementation and monitoring. The Gender Assessment was conducted through desk reviews, interviews with stakeholders, focus group discussions and field surveys to collect information and analyzed those data. The results were incorporated throughout the other project documents and mainstreamed into the mitigation and adaptation initiatives to obtain in such a way that gender co-benefits are obtained.

### 2 Rational of Gender Assessment

The Gender Assessment will help to understand relationship between men and women, their access to resources, opportunities for the empowerment of women and men. It is a comprehensive assessment entails examining the differences of roles, rights, needs, and opportunities of women and men, boys and girls in this project context. It is a tool that will be used as an important input for planning and implementation strategies for enhancing gender equity in this project. GCF also puts emphasis on the ensuring gender equity in all project level activities. In this regard, a Gender Assessment was conducted for mainstreaming gender into the project level activities. The objective of the Gender Assessment was:

- to identify the gender aspects of the project activities, including selection of employees and their roles
- to collect information from various stakeholders such as workers, supervisors, managers and other relevant parties
- to learn the impact of the project on empowerment of different categories of women and men;

3 Methodology of the Gender Assessment

The assessment studied the engagement of men and women in the RMG sector in Bangladesh, labor force participation, access and control to technology and financial resources, as well as power relations. The study team gathered information through literature review, interviews with experts, key informant interviews (KIIs), focus group discussions, and household survey. Detailed methodology is described below.

3.1 Literature Review

At the inception phase of the project the team performed literature review from secondary sources to understand the baseline situation of the RMG sector in Bangladesh to understand the baseline situation of RMG sector and the broad scope for EE applications in the sector considering gender dimensions. In this regard, the project team analyzed relevant reports, about national policies, strategies, guidelines, etc., related to RMG sector, energy-related risks identified in the RMG sector, historical studies and research on Energy Efficiency (EE) in Bangladesh’s RMG sector, international best practices, potential EE interventions etc considering gender equity.

3.2 Factory Survey and Household Survey

The study team conducted open inquiries of facility managers and workers of RMG factories and community members living around the factory to understand energy efficiency, environment and social issues related to RMG sector focusing on gender. For this purpose, 80 respondents from 20 factories were selected in different districts of Bangladesh including Dhaka, Gazipur, Narayanganj and Chattogram district. In each of the RMG units, key informant interviews (KIIs) were conducted with facility managers and workers and field group discussions were held with community members living around the factory focusing on facility managers. While choosing the respondents for FGD, the study team tried to maintain gender balance by including both male and female representatives. However, during the survey period, it was observed that women were underrepresented as there is no representation of women in the information shared by facility managers.

3.3 Key Informant Interview with Experts

Key informant interviews (KIIs) have been conducted to supplement the information mined from secondary sources as well as to enhance the understanding of the ground reality of the RMG sector and its energy efficiency potential. The KIIs were conducted with government officials, NGOs, INGOs, development partners, academicians, financial institution, monitoring agencies for RMG sector etc. A total of 25 KIIs were conducted based on a questionnaire which contains queries related to:

- Growth prospects of RMG sector
- Compliance with environmental, social and gender (ESG) related requirements
- Energy efficiency in the RMG sector
- Financing energy efficiency initiatives


---
**Page 181**
---

During the KIs the study team tried to maintain a gender balance by including both male and female representatives from each sector. But in most of the cases it was not possible due to absence of female leadership in the sector. However, at least one woman was interviewed from selected stakeholder categories i.e., from selected government ministries, NGO, INGO, development organizations, CSOs as well as environment, social and gender experts were attended the workshop. In the first workshop total 46 participants were present including 12 women and in second workshop there were 45 participants where 11 were female participants. Even though RMG sector occupies a high number of women in terms of employment but very few women are involved in project management or research regarding RMG sector related issues. So, the representation of women was less compared to men in the workshops. The information gathered during literature review and surveys were presented during the stakeholder workshops including presentations on existing issues and concerns relating to Environment, Society and Gender along with the Grameen model. The workshop clarified the framework of the proposed GCF proposal along with the key indicators with the stakeholders’ inputs. As a result of this workshop, some changes in the project documents were made accordingly. The detail findings from the workshops have been presented in a separate document of workshop report.

# 4 Policies and Regulations

## 4.1 National Policy, Strategy and Framework

The government of Bangladesh recognizes the need for coordinated efforts on gender issue and the importance of women's empowerment as development initiatives to increase resilience.

At present Bangladesh is engaged in aligning national legislation and policies with its international commitments to strengthen gender dimensions. Some key policies are available with regard to women’s rights and gender equality, which are discussed below. The following policies are highlighted below. In addition, there is also a set of supporting ‘Rules’ & ‘Regulations’ that include important provisions.

## The Constitution of Bangladesh

The Constitution of Bangladesh has recognized fundamental rights of women workers at work place (Article 23), and social protection activities (Articles 28, 29, 30, 34, 15 and 20(1). (The Constitution of Bangladesh): Bangladesh Constitution provides that the state shall not discriminate against any citizen on the grounds of religion, race, caste or place of birth and women workers are also protected under this article. Article 34 of the constitution states: “It is hereby declared that the State of Bangladesh as a state is responsible for its citizens to ensure the right to work that is the right to guaranteed employment at a reasonable wage having regard to the quantity and quality of works, or remuneration in kind, and the conditions under which such employment is given. The constitution recognizes that everyone shall be paid for work on the basis of the principle from each according to his ability and to each according to his work (Article 30). Article 28 of the Constitution III of Bangladesh Constitution, especially relevant to workers’ rights, prohibits all forms of forced labour and makes it a punishable offence (Article 34) and guarantees the right to freedom of association and the right to form trade unions (Article 38).

## Bangladesh Labour Act, 2006

The BLA has been enacted with a large number of labour rights for protection of women workers. It has various positive provisions relating to labour rights, but due to its various limitations, absence of adequate penalty in statutory law for employers and lack of proper rules of conduct by the garment authorities, there is scope of violation of labor rights in some areas.

The BLA 2006, mainly focus on the following major components:

- **a. Sanitation and Hygiene:** From section 51–59. BLA 2006, the terms and conditions of sanitation, ventilation, lighting and temperature, dust and fume, display of waste effluents, overcrowding, working hours, drinking water and rest facilities are prescribed. In section 59, it is clearly mentioned to provide distinct toilet facility for men and women along with proper disposal arrangements.

- **b. Maternity Benefit:** Every pregnant woman worker can get the maternity benefit of eight weeks preceding the expected day of her delivery and eight weeks immediately following the date of delivery (Section 41). The terms and conditions regarding the entitlement of maternity benefit are also mentioned in Section 47–49.

- **c. Working Conditions for Women:** Employment of women, adolescents or children in special hazardous work (such as the dangerous occupations underground or underwater work) is prohibited or restricted (Section 36, 40, 42 and 79). Furthermore, without the consent of women worker, working between 10:00PM to 8:00AM is not allowed (Section 159).


---
**Page 183**
---

d. **Safety:** According to Section 89, At least one first-aid box for every one hundred fifty workers must be accessible and maintained. Also, for establishments with forty or more workers, a child care room with proper facilities should be provided (Section 94).

e. **Trade Union:** If 20% of the total workers or members are women while establishing trade unions, at least two-thirds of the executive committee should be women (Sections 176a, BLA, 2013).

f. **Indecent behavior:** Despite of the rank or status of a woman worker, no one of the establishment staff can treat or behave in indecent or improper manner with her (Section 332, BLA, 2013).

g. **Industrial Democracy:** If factories occupies 50 or more workers than safety committees must be formed (Section 904, BLA, 2013). A Safety welfare officers shall be appointed and the cost of treatment of occupational diseases will be covered (Section 1503), BLA, 2013. The establishment should provide free medical facilities for workers and their families and workplaces with more than 5000 members or staffs should establish workplace health centers (Sections 876b), BLA, 2013.

Bangladesh Labor Act, 2015: Recognizes on gender equity and has special provision for pregnant women. According to the Bangladesh Labor Rules, 2015, the owner and other workers should have the following responsibilities to a pregnant worker such as:
(a) Such behavior or comment should not be made so that she gets insulted or humiliated
(b) Not to expose her to any work which is likely to endanger her own health.
(c) To remove or post her to any work where this is no hazard.
(d) To give priority to use the lift during the work.
(e) After the delivery, to make opportunity for breastfeeding and ensure the congenial atmosphere for this.

Right to Occupational Safety and Health BLA 2006 provides provisions relating to occupational hygiene, occupational diseases, safety measures, industrial accidents, protection of women and young persons in dangerous occupations and also covers conditions of work, working hours, rest periods, etc. (Sections 57-134, BLA, 2006). Sections 58, 61, 62, 91, 92, 93, 94, 109, BLA, 2006). Most of the provisions lack in standard values and are not specific rather general in nature. In terms of occupational safety and health issues, the status of Bangladesh is very poor and women workers are extremely vulnerable in this regard.

Section 195 of the Bangladesh Labor Act, 2006 (amended 2013) outlines numerous "unfair labor practices." For example, no employer shall "dismiss, discharge, remove from employment or otherwise discriminate against any employee for refusing to accept a quip or threats to injure him in respect of his employment by reason that the worker is or proposes to become, or seeks to persuade any other person to become, a member or officer of a trade union."

The National Women Development Policy (2011) briefly promotes women's role in environmental management and the importance of ensuring facilities for and the security of pregnant women in the event of natural disasters. It emphasizes on elimination of all forms of violence to women including physical and mental abuse and sexual harassment, rape, bribery, family abuse and acid throwing in family, society and in the workplace. The policy enforces


---
**Page 184**
---

participation of women in decision making regarding environment management and pollution control and program implementation. It also ensures gender equal rate of wages, increase participation of women in the labor market, equal opportunity at the workplace, ensured security and removal of disparities in employment.

The policy aims to motivate all employment agencies to give women all kinds of equal opportunities under government quota and employment policy and create necessary environment for their betterment. The policy also aims to increase their position as such and continue their advancement. In addition, it promotes to make necessary reforms in the labor laws and regulations so that they can enjoy the benefits of the contribution of women in the management of natural resources conservation and a safer environment to give them opportunity of equal participation in environment preservation policy and programs. The policy also aims to provide financial assistance and incentives to undertake all out initiative for employment of the educated and literate women labor.

The 7th Five Year Plan (2016-2020) has also highlighted the aspects for worker safety. It was mentioned in the TFYP that higher growth in Bangladesh was benefited from a rising labor force due to the increasing population and an increasing labour force participation rate of the female labor. But wage rates tend to be lower for female workers relative to male and proper employment regulations to facilitate their access to safe working conditions (e.g., provision of safety equipment, child care facilities, etc.) have to be put in place in most areas of employment. The RMG industry has shown a remarkable progress in worker safety among the RMG sector. Thanks to recent coordinated efforts of the Government with the Apparel Manufacturers, and the buyer-driven initiatives. Substantial progress is being made ensuring worker safety standards and improvement in working conditions have been achieved.

Bangladesh has ratified International Labor Organization (ILO) conventions 87 and 98 on freedom of association and collective bargaining and is required to protect the rights contained within them. At the international level, Bangladesh has also ratified the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), as well as different ILO Convention.

Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan 2009 (BCCSAP) has a programme for strengthening gender consideration for climate change management. Under this programme, it is mentioned that gender consideration is needed to be included in all climate responsive activities.

Bangladesh Climate Change and Gender Action Plan (ccGAP-Bangladesh) 2013 has been prepared with aim to ensure gender equality in climate change-related policies, strategies and programmes. This action plan aims at the equal participation of women and men in climate change mitigation practices and low carbon development. Some of the major points that are addressed in the action plan are following:

a. Ensure that gender considerations are added in all stages of reviewing energy and technology policies.

b. Develop gender responsive programmes to reduce GHG emission at household (HH) while ensuring women's access to energy and power technologies.
