## International Policy and Framework

### 4.2 International Policy and Framework

#### 4.2.1 Green Climate Fund (GCF)

The GCF provides clear definitions of differences between key gender terms: Gender, Gender Mainstreaming, and Gender Sensitivity.

In accordance with the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan, the core elements of gender mainstreaming required in GCF project design include:

- Country ownership in terms of alignment with national policies and priorities.
- A multi-stakeholder approach to gender and gender-sensitive approaches build into a gender-sensitive approach to project planning design and implementation arrangements.
- Gender equitable stakeholder consultations based on the gender parameters in the policy.
- Determination that all of the project/programmes can respond to the needs of women and men in view of the specific climate change issue.
- Identification of drivers of change and gender dynamics required to achieve the project/programme adaptation or mitigation goals.

12


---
**Page 186**
---

# Identification and design of specific gender elements to be included in project/programme activities.

## Maximizing gender in GCF projects can be synthesized into a three-pronged approach on a gender policy cycle:

- **Gender analysis:** to understand the social, economic and political factors underlying climate change-exacerbated gender inequality, and the potential contributions of women and men to adaptation and mitigation strategies for climate change;
- **Gender actions:** methods and tools to promote gender equality and reduce gender disparities in climate action; and
- **Gender-sensitive M&E:** measuring the outcomes and impacts of project activities on women's and men's resilience to climate change through gender responsive M&E.

### 4.2.2 World Bank Group

The World Bank has integrated its Gender and Development Policy Framework that gender issues are important dimensions of its poverty reduction, economic growth, human well-being and development effectiveness agenda. According to the gender mainstreaming strategy, "the term 'gender' refers to the socially constructed differences between males and females. The term distinguishes the socially constructed from the biologically determined aspects of being male and female."

The Bank Operational Policy focused Specifically on Gender and Development (OP/BP 4.20) has been developed with the following objectives:

- Providing a country-level, strategic Approach to mainstreaming gender issues
- Country-level Gender Assessment

Integration of gender issues into operations findings in the Country Assistance Strategy (CAS) developed by the bank for each donor country. It draws on and discusses the findings of the Gender Assessment and links policy provisions for gender (OP/BP 4.20) and policy guidance for prevention and response to GBV (OP/BP 2.13).

Integration of gender issues into operations of interventions established under the CAS.

Based on the policy guidelines, a Gender Assessment (whether stand alone as part of a broader assessment or embedded inside the Bank) should typically include the following aspects:

a. A brief description of

i. The different socioeconomic roles of females and males, including their participation in the labor market and household economic activities;
ii. Male-female disparities in access to control over, and use of assets and productive resources;
iii. Male-female disparities in human development indicators;
iv. The relative participation of females and males in development decision-making; and
v. Laws, institutions, programs, norms, and other societal practices that lead (implicitly or explicitly) to gender discrimination and/or gender inequality.


---
**Page 187**
---

b. A review of the country context, including the country’s policies, priorities, legal and regulatory framework, and institutional arrangements for implementing its gender and development goals;

c. Based on (a) and (b) above, suggested gender-responsive policies and interventions that aim to promote gender equality and enhancing economic growth, human well-being, and development effectiveness.

### 4.2.3 Asian Development Bank (ADB)

ADB’s policy on Gender and Development (GAD) adopts mainstreaming as a key strategy in promoting gender equity. The key elements of ADB’s GAD policy include the following:

a. Gender analysis: to assess how gender equality issues affect women and men, and to consider women's needs and perspectives in planning its operations.

b. Gender sensitivity: to ensure that all aspects of project design and implementation take into account a project on men and women, and on the economic and social relationships between them.

c. Gender planning: to formulate specific strategies that aim to bring about equal opportunities for women and men.

Mainstreaming: to consider gender issues in all aspects of ADB operations, accompanied by efforts to encourage women's participation in the decision-making process in development projects.

e. Agenda setting: to assist DMC governments in formulating strategies to reduce gender disparities in areas such as education, health, nutrition, water and sanitation, girls' education, health, legal rights, employment, and income-earning opportunities.

In 2013, ADB adopted a new Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment Operational Plan 2013–2020 that sets out the strategic directions and guiding framework for moving the gender equity agenda forward, and for better aligning gender operations with Strategy 2020.

The outcome of the gender equality operational plan is important to ensure gender equality results of ADB operations. However, it should be noted that a purely removalist approach and priority approach on its own will not be sufficient to narrow persisting gender gaps and inequalities. Targeted public policies and priority investments in women and girls are required to accelerate closing these gaps and achieving gender equality.

Opportunities for direct investment in women and girls to narrow gender disparities will be explored in more detail below:

a. girls’ secondary education and completion;

b. access to productive assets, labor saving technology, jobs, and income earning opportunities;

c. school-to-work transition programs and skills training for employment, especially for young women;

d. business development services for women entrepreneurs; (v) financial services and access to credit;

e. policy and legal reforms to tackle issues of gender-based violence and anti-trafficking.

f. giving women voice in decision-making in both formal and informal institutions.


14

These complimentary and more targeted initiatives are more likely to deliver gender equality outcomes faster and to equip women to better capture and benefit from the opportunities provided through gender mainstreaming.

### 4.2.4 International Finance Corporation (IFC)

Supporting gender equality is a strategic priority for IFC, which provides advice and investments in partner countries and companies that help expand and strengthen the role of women in their economies. This includes:

1. Closing gaps in health and education
2. Supporting more and better job opportunities
3. Helping women gain access to finance, insurance, technology, and land
4. Enhancing women’s leadership roles

IFC works to reduce gaps between men and women in the private sector, enabling companies and economies to improve their performance. Too often, women's private-sector participation is underrepresented, limiting their ability to contribute to their families' economic well-being, profitability, and contribute significantly to shared prosperity. However, women have less access to equal employment opportunities and capital to grow their businesses, experience higher levels of sexual violence, are more likely from owning or investing land or other property, and have less income for the future.

IFC and its partners are addressing these challenges by working with clients to ensure that women and men can equally participate in creating business value and secure influence and income through work. With IFC’s support, women can increase their participation in employment, entrepreneurship, and leadership; societies can reach their economic potential.

In the IFC Performance Standards (PS 1-8), gender inclusion is addressed as a cross-cutting issue across multiple Performance Standards especially for assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts (PS-1) and labor and working conditions (PS-2).

Further, IFC has developed a toolkit for addressing the specific gender-related issues. The toolkit is in a modular guideline that includes four tool suites focused on different issues:

a. **TOOL SUITE 1: Women in Leadership**

   Tool Suite 1 presents a detailed business case for increasing gender balance on boards, in senior management, and in the workforce. It features 16 tools to help companies:
   
   - Assess their gender-balance performance at all levels of their organization
   - Increase gender balance in their workforces
   - Monitor progress toward gender diversity targets

b. **TOOL SUITE 2: Women-Owned Businesses and the Supply Chain**

   Tool Suite 2 presents a detailed business case for increasing the gender diversity of businesses that are part of an OIGM company’s supply chain. It features six tools to help companies:
   
   - Evaluate their current supply chain
   - Develop gender-sensitive criteria for qualified vendors to improve outreach to and development of women-owned businesses


---
**Page 189**
---

### TOOL SUITE 3: Women and Community Engagement

Tool Suite 3 presents the business case for considering the issues, perspectives, and concerns of female community members in a company's community engagement activities.

i. Assess the degree of gender sensitivity in their current community engagement approaches.
ii. Integrate gender into existing community engagement assessments and activities.
iii. Monitor and sustain progress towards a more gender-equitable community engagement strategy and activity portfolio.

### TOOL SUITE 4: Addressing Gender-Based Violence (GBV) in the Workplace

Tool Suite 4 presents the business case for addressing intimate partner violence (IPV), sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA), and sexual harassment. It features seven tools to help companies:

i. Recognize, address, and support victims of workplace sexual harassment.
ii. Support employees who may have experienced IPV and SEA in the community.
iii. Develop a gender-sensitive approach to their engagement portfolio, to recognize and address forms of GBV occurring in the community.
iv. Monitor and sustain progress towards reducing GBV

### 4.2.5 Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA)

JICA has three Strategic Development Objectives (SDOs) for "gender and development," with effective gender mainstreaming as a key strategy to achieve these objectives. These three objectives are introduced below. The first and the second objectives correspond to the 'principal objectives' in DAC classification of aid contribution, while the third corresponds to the 'significant objective' of the same.

JICA envisions "Inclusive and Dynamic Development" in its efforts for promoting human and to provide timely, comprehensive support to those in developing countries. JICA recognizes women as a key agent for change in development and ensures that women have an equal voice and equal opportunities in decision-making processes. JICA aims at increasing women's participation in leadership roles in all stages of natural disasters from DRR to emergency response and recovery phases. JICA also supports the implementation of policies with a gender and diverse group perspective. JICA’s work will be sensitive to the different needs and priorities of women and diverse groups as well as their security as it actively provides support in building societies full of hope and dignity.

### Priority Areas:

i. Women's Economic Empowerment: Promote women's Livelihood, employment, entrepreneurship and business development.
ii. Women's Rights and Gender Equality: Ensure women’s rights and security in conflicts, natural disasters and other crises including Trafficking in persons and Gender based violence
iii. Women's Education and Lifetime Health: Promote women's lifetime health and education to enhance their autonomy

iv. Gender Responsive Governance: Promote gender equal policies, strategies and institutions
v. Gender Responsive Infrastructure: Promote infrastructure to enhance women’s mobility, economic opportunities and well-being
vi. Empowering Women's Capabilities, Eliminating Discrimination and Improving Basic Infrastructure

### 4.3 Gender Related Policies of IDCOL and Local Financial Institutions (LFIs)

#### 4.3.1 IDCOL
IDCOL makes the gender equity and equality a priority to maintain a safe, secure, positive, and collaborative culture in its work environment. IDCOL’s ESMF provides detailed directions on integrating gender issues in development and assessing projects/programs which are following:

a) IDCOL will assess institutional mechanisms and capacities to promote greater visibility of gender and social inclusion considerations in the design of its projects and programs.
b) Ensure that all recipient projects and borrowers under the projects will identify and maximize opportunities for women’s participation in all employment opportunities during project implementation, including through the use of microfinance, with potential adoption of evidence-based gender related targets.
c) Promote adherence to national core labor standards and commitments to gender equality in accordance with the laws and regulations as the legislations and regulations of the Government of Bangladesh.
d) Assess the potential impact/impacts/scenarios/systems/programme on the potential use of other identified women friendly technologies for greater social gender related benefits.
e) Ensure the use of the Gender Action Framework.

This Policy will guide in promoting gender equity, equality and awareness in its internal policies and processes.

Additionally, IDCOL has developed a Gender Policy for its organizational purpose. IDCOL believes that all staff members share a common interest in achieving the objectives of the organization and collaborative effort of the employees will ensure creating an environment that would be conducive to the achievement of these objectives. The Gender Policy will include a systematic approach within the organizational practices in promoting gender sensitiveness, awareness, equity and equality in its internal environment and activities. Therefore, the Gender Policy is intended to provide guidelines for all staff members who are involved in IDCOL’s activities, and adherence to all other relevant laws, guidelines and policies.

There will be four local financial institutions (LFIs) involved in the programme who will channel the lendable funds of IDCOL into microfinance business. These financial institutions are City Bank Ltd., Southeast Bank Ltd., BRAC Bank Ltd. and IDCO Finance Ltd. All of these institutions are well aware of the gender related concerns as they have experience of working

with readymade garments (RMG) sector. RMG sector in Bangladesh is one of those sectors that follows rules and regulations regarding gender quality especially in wage distribution, labor rights and occupational health and safety. Also, the current monitoring system in the RMG sector is well maintained and there is pressure from the international buyers as well. As a result, the RMG sector is one of the most gender sensitive sectors for monitoring. A brief description of the existing policy and experience of the LFs are following:

| IDLC Finance Limited follows the ten Principles of the United Nations Global Compact (UNG) which are derived from Universal Declaration of Human Rights, International Labor Organization (ILO), Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, Convention on the Rights of the Child, Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, Convention on Environment and Development, United Nations Convention Against Corruption. These Principles covers Human Rights, Labor, Environment and Anti-Corruption; and IDLC takes consider gender issues in all these areas. In addition to this, IDLC Finance Limited will consider gender issues to finance Energy Efficient projects under GCF by taking consent from clients considering wage level, overtime, leave policy, workplace health and safety (i.e., protection against harassment), first-aid, freedom of association and collective bargaining provisions, and child labor.

BRAC Bank has always been very much aligned with the gender equality, women empowerment and promoting leadership capacity of women; which is reflected by adhering ESG requirements. The bank has organized framework for the monitoring that follows Environmental & Social Safeguard Policy and Speak-up Policy where gender issues are highlighted with utmost priority.

They have agreed to follow the following process under the guidance of Bank's Sustainable Finance Committee at the working level: Sustainable Finance Unit will carry forward following activities to adhere GCF’s ESG requirements:

1) Review the existing Environmental & Social Safeguard policy and add the new ESG requirement as per GCF’s ESG requirements (if any) by the end of 2023.
2) Rollout capacity building sessions for business and risk assessment officers on the new ESG requirements as per GCF’s ESG requirements.
3) Follow GCF's prescribed methodology as well as already operating ESG risk assessment framework prior financing any under GCF.

4) Yearly performance monitoring of Bank as well as its client's performance in dealing with ESG issues.

Apart from Sustainable Finance Unit, BRAC Bank has their own Credit Inspection Team to monitor client’s performance all at the year. hence conducting half-yearly or annual inspection will be a regular activity for us.


Southeast Bank Limited (SEBL) does not have any specific gender related policy but they consider gender equality in all their project sites. Periodic and surprise inspections are among the keys risk management tools of SEBL. Most of the RMG clients of SEBL work with renowned international buyers who also have certain conditions precedent on gender related matters as per the GCF’s ESG requirements. Hence, SEBL will conduct regular periodic inspection for ensuring proper practice of gender related issues in the project. Also, SEBL will assess the client's willingness and ability to implement gender issues as required by GCF and other regulatory authorities in the appraisal of loan proposal.

# 5 Findings from Gender Assessment

## 1 Introduction

Since its journey in Bangladesh, the RMG sector has created opportunities for people, especially women and disadvantaged women to lead a dignified life. The industry plays a key role in employment generation and in the provision of income to the poor—it employs nearly 4.4 million workers. The sector has also played a significant role in the socio-economic development of Bangladesh by contributing significantly to the country's export earnings, which are the backbone of the sector. It also plays a pivotal role to promote the development of linkage small scale industries, since 99% of the accessories like dyeing, printing, zipping, labels are manufactured in Bangladesh. The RMG sector has been instrumental in promoting women's economic activities, thereby contributing to the country's overall socio-economic development, woman empowerment and poverty reduction.

It has seen tremendous growth and dominated the industrial sector of the country. Currently, there are nearly 4,000 RMG firms (The Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association, 2019) in Bangladesh. Majority of those firms are locally owned with the exception of a few foreign firms located in export processing zones. Nearly half of RMG firms of Bangladesh are located in Dhaka (38%), followed by Chittagong (28.9%), Chattogram (15.1%) and Narayanganj (14.7%). Most of the factories are in different industrial clusters within these cities. The RMG sector is also strategically located near major trade routes for import and export, logistic and banking facilities as well as easier availability of workers. Even within these regions there is a spread of RMG units beyond and beyond the city limits. This has been possible due to the government's decision in EPZs. This is due to the change in government legislation in 2010, allowing foreign direct investment (FDI) outside EPZs as well. Another key characteristic of the RMG sector in Bangladesh is that almost half of the units i.e., 48% are small enterprises which have less than 500 workers.

However, women working in RMG sector are plagued with various problems, some of these are insufficient wage, irregularity in timeliness of wage payment and unsafe working conditions. Women face a number of challenges that may make them feel the need to leave especially in areas like workplace discrimination and career prospect, harassment, work and working hours, safety and security at workplace, health and hygiene, workplace provision, socialization and freedom of association, collective bargaining and social dialogue. Although gender-based wage discrimination is not seen that much now a days, discrimination exists in areas of equal treatment, promotion and job opportunities. Women workers face more discrimination with regard to the scope of promotion and their career prospect is limited. A study shows that only 5-10% of the women workers eventually become supervisors (The Daily Star, March 8).

The Gender Assessment mainly focuses on the existing gender scenario of the RMG sector Bangladesh and tried to identify the problems and suggested measures to overcome those. For the growth and sustainability of the sector, integrating women's participation and contribution is a major concern. In this project, survey was conducted in 20 RMG units of different sizes and locations in Dhaka, Chittagong and Rangpur districts. The study aimed at assessing the existing gender scenario in the RMG sector Bangladesh and identify the existing issues for ensuring gender equity. Along with this, KII, FGD and stakeholder meetings were conducted to get an overall picture of the current gender dynamics of the RMG sector in Bangladesh.

### 5.2 Labor Force Participation

Women in the labor force have been at the forefront of Bangladesh's recent successful economic growth. They make up most of the workers in the ready-made garment (RMG) sector, the country's flagship export-oriented industry. The creation of a large microfinance system has also increased women's access to credit and other financial services which has also changed the economic position of women. Some of the major issues behind the participation of women in the RMG sector in Bangladesh are the following according to a study:

- Women from extreme poor households have a significantly higher probability of participating compared to non-poor. The difference between moderate poor and non-poor is insignificant.
- Poorer women are more likely to participate in low paid wage employment whereas the rich are more likely to be self-employed.
- Presence of young children has a positive effect on self-employment and a negative effect on wage employment.
- Being married has a negative effect. This result may have been influenced by the fact that paid employment dominated by the RMG sector requires long hours of work. The study also found there is a growing acceptance of outside employment among young unmarried women.

In socio-economic context of Bangladesh, women participate less on formal livelihood activities in rural areas. Even if they do, there is discrimination in wages compared to men and socially it is not acceptable for them to work outside the home. This makes paid employment a good opportunity due to lack of industries in rural areas. So, women from rural areas prefer RMG sector as there is good opportunity for women even with no education or skill; they get equal or almost equal pay than male workers. They can earn Tk 100-250 per day and Tk 300 each month. Moreover, they move to new places for work and there they can live independently and without judged by the society. On the other hand, as the women coming from the rural area are not well educated or skilled, they get low paid jobs in RMG sector where employers can hire them with minimum wages. Also, they are more vulnerable, doable and manageable than women in urban areas. They accept without protest the flexible terms of employment and hardly raise their voice against anything. This is why employers prefer to hire them.

Brijmohan, C., Lawson, D., Sajid, S. 2013. "Labor Market Outcomes in Bangladesh: The Role of Poverty and Gender Norms." European Journal of Development Research, Vol. 25, www.journals.palgrave.com/ejdr


---
**Page 195**

Even though the RMG sector has played a tremendous role for women empowerment, there has been discrimination in labour force participation in this sector. From data collected within factories, 4 out of every 5 production line workers are female, whilst just over 1 in 20 supervisors are women. Women are more engaged in sewing and other tasks related to technical knowledge and social responsibilities of women towards their families. Also, women are engaged more in sewing or as helpers compared to men. Less number of women are seen in fabric units and QC departments. A study conducted by the Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics (BBS) showed that Male workers are found to be less in helper and sewing department by about 86% and 78% respectively.

According to the labor force survey (LFS), the female labor force participation rate increased from 2003 to 2006 and then decreased dramatically from 2006 to 2010, reaching 36.1%. The LFS in 2015 showed a further increase of 4 percentage points compared with that in the previous survey between the two surveys. According to a recent survey of Center for Policy Dialogue (CPD), the ratio of female worker participation in the RMG sector, known as a women-driven sector, has seen an increase from 36% in 2015 to 48%. The number of female workers employed by RMG factories in Bangladesh with 3.5 million workers, of which 69.6% are female and 39.2% are male. In the previous survey in 2015, the workers were 38% male and 64% female. The survey was conducted through a stratified random sampling method in small, medium, and large categories in Dhaka, Gazipur, Narayanganj, and Chittagong.

During this study it was observed that the percentage of female workers is less than male workers. This scenario might have occurred because of the recent increase in wages which


---
**Page 197**
---

attracted more male workers to join the labour force in RMG sector. Also, the employers are now becoming interested to hire male workers over females as they would not have to provide additional facilities (i.e., maternity leave, special support for pregnant or lactating mother, workplace safety during night shifts) to men. Moreover, men are available anytime as they do not have family bindings or security related concerns in the perspective of Bangladesh.

### Gender Distribution of the Workers

Figure 5-2 Gender distribution of workers in selected RMG units

The RMG units were selected based on their technical performance mainly and the survey was conducted with the Facility Managers to get an overall idea about the factory. However, in most cases, women are not represented as managers or supervisors at all levels. There was no representation of women in the higher management of the selected RMG units as well. This is a major concern because women constitute 70% of the total workforce and hold several posts in garments sector is negligible. According to a study conducted on women workers in Bangladesh RMG sector by CIPD in 2016, only about 0.5% of managers in RMG enterprises are female. Women represent less than 3% of the top management in RMG sector as well. On the production floor, of the 60.8 females, more women are employed in the sewing section (73.9%) and less women work in the cutting section (22.7%).

### Gender of the Facility managers

Figure 5-3 Gender of facility managers

Lack of training facilities, skill and proper knowledge of technology are the main reasons for female workers to lag behind in the sector; the surveys reveal. Besides, male workers are taking more interests in the sector due to increasing wages. During FGD, female workers said


---
**Page 198**
---

that there are trainers for workers but women do not participate because they feel most of the participants are male. Also, women are promoted less to higher positions. So, the female workers do not feel interested to participate in the trainings. However,

It was revealed from KII with experts that in terms of gender equality, the sector has very low wage discrimination at similar work-levels although some works are exclusive terms of male workers. The sectors, however, has limited participation from women in decision-making positions and 50% of female workers are employed as laborers compared to only 12% of men in terms of market perceptions. Also, women themselves are also less inclined to get into such job roles.

The tapering off the number of female workers can be stopped only by organizing training and skill development works, both by public and private organizations, the experts said. Over 3.31 million people are, at present, working in this sector. Majority of them are laborers. Earlier in 2015, there were about 4.67 million workers in RMG sector but now only 3.31 million female workers 56.86%. The total workers in this sector was 2.99 million at that time. The reason of the decline is mainly due to changing technology; women have no opportunity to rule the machines and hence they are not interested to work in RMG sector. According to the findings of the two BBS surveys also revealed that the average growth rate of employment of male workers in the RMG sector has increased 11.54% between 2013 and 2016-2017. On the other hand, female workers are going down by an average of 8.9% during this same period.

Specifically, on women workers in Bangladesh RMG sector, the CPD study reveals that only about 0.5% of managers in RMG enterprises are females and only around 9.3% of HR managers in the RMG sector are female. Only 12.8% of women work at the floor level while 47.6% of women are employed in the sewing section (73.9%) and less women work in the cutting section (22.7%). The study points to the skill level comparison between males and females in terms of knowledge about operating machines. It is found that female workers are proportionately less knowledgeable about operating different machines.

There are numerous estimates on the number of factories and number of labours present. The list below gives the estimates from reputed establishments:

a. Department for International Development (DFID): DFIE has published a full database of 4841 RMG factories in 2017 (DIFE, 2017). According to this data base, i.e., 155,433 workers are currently employed in the RMG industry of them 43% are women.

b. Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA): As per BGMEA, there were about 6820 factories in operation employing about 4 million workforces of them 3.2 million are women (BGMEA, 2017).

c. Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics (BBS): BBS conducts Labour Force Survey which is carried out every five years. According to the latest survey conducted by BBS for the country there are 3.1 million workforces employed in the RMG factories of them 1.7 million are men and 1.4 million are women (QLFS, 2017). i.e., 54% and 46% men and women respectively.

Government policies can affect female labor force participation rates, positively or otherwise. Some important policies are already in place and appear to have had good effect. This various


---
**Page 199**
---

programs that have been implemented for increasing female enrollment in primary and secondary schools have been notably effective in eliminating the gender education gap Bangladesh already has a relatively generous maternity leave policy. Employers are required to provide weeks of paid maternal leave, as specified by the 2006 Labor Law. While in principle this is a good policy, it has not had any significant impact on female employment. It is unlikely to have any effect at all on anything but formal paid employment which limits it to a small minority of working women. No systematic evaluations appear to have been made of the impact of maternity leave and other related policies that are not granted at most RMG factories and that most workers are not aware of their rights in this regard.

### 5.3 Constraints for Female Employment

The survey conducted with the mission, ILO/GEA, SKRMA, RMCA factories, development partners, NGOs, and communities asked mainly about their views on the present status of women in the RMG sector, the challenges of female employment and the strategies that can be adopted to raise female labour force participation in Bangladesh.10

Figure 5-4 presents the seven major constraints to employing women as mentioned by the stakeholders. These include lack of education, relatively skill and knowledge-based work, safety, social norms, discrimination and segregation, long working hours/overtime, lack of transport facilities and the care burden.

Male-female equal contribution is very important for social coherence and balance. However, due to social constraints, female workers have face problems and sometimes have to sacrifice their career in order to take care of their family. This is one reason why many women do not join RMG sector. The owners also prefer to employ a male worker rather than a female worker. This is mainly because:

- Lack of skill in female workers.
- Female workers need more breaks after marriage or after having children.
- Social norms are more assured for female workers and mostly they do not want to stay for night shifts.

Most of the female workers in the RMG sector are very young, 18–29 years and do not tend to work in the sector for a long period. This is mainly due to occupational hazard, health issues, damage to eyesight, having children etc. Also, employers do not prefer aged experienced workers as they are more likely to reduce their wages or hire higher females at lower wages.

However, social and gender issues are better than previous because maternity leave, and other facilities are given to the workers in most of the RMS units.

From social perspective, male ratio is higher in management sector. It will be solved by creating awareness and providing training programs. There are some barriers like mind set up, lack of equity, lack of awareness, etc. It will be solved in future. BKMEA also provide lactating mother facility to the female workers. Female workers get KPI bonus of 1000 rupees per month (total 36000 take-ups in two years) for extra care of mother (Government is paying the amount) and also female pregnant workers get maternity leave.

Currently, rate of educated women has increased in the textile sector with the increased education level. The majority of the female workers in this sector have mostly completed at least primary education with a good percentage of completion of eighth grade and above as well. However, women usually get less opportunity for technical skill development trainings due to time constraints and other reasons. In addition, they are not given enough opportunities than men and can learn new skills easily. This situation makes women lag behind from their male counterparts in terms of job promotion and career advancement. As a result, women have to be stagnant in a position for years or lose their job. To ensure increased work engagement, continuous gender-sensitive trainings for all workers is needed. In this way female workers will not be dropped off automation due to lack of skills.

Jobs in the garments sector are also found among several sections and there are different workers involved here. At initial level the workers do not need much technical skills. But in later stages higher technical knowledge is required for operating modern machines. No worker, male or female, can learn all these skills at once. Female workers usually only learn those after several trainings. The problem lies in gender inequality because male workers are given more opportunities and involved in decision making than female workers.


---
**Page 201**
---

So, it is essential to ensure that both women and men get access to skill development opportunities. Also, the higher management need to change their concept of sex-segregation of work and provide equal scope for both genders.

The project will increase awareness of the women working in the sector regarding their rights and encourage them to raise their voice whenever they face any injustice. Also, monitoring mechanism will be at place so that it can be assessed whether the management is addressing issues to ensure gender equality.

### 5.4 Educational Status

In RMG sector, at the worker level recruitments are done through interview and by skill test of the worker. No standard certification system exists to assess the skill level of the worker. RMG employers prefer at least class 5 level education while recruit workers and at least class 3 for the technical staff. According to a survey conducted by Centre for Policy Research (CPR), released on Mar 18, among garment worker 60.8% are female &amp; 39.20% are male.

The survey was conducted on 2270 workers in 193 factories in Dhaka, Gazipur, Narayanganj &amp; Chittagong districts of Bangladesh during the period from Dec 2016 to Mar 2017.

Among the female workers surveyed, most have attained some form of education (SSC or HSC level, primary level or education up to class ten) and 4% have no formal education at all.

Three-fourths of the sampled facility managers have education higher than HSC and one-fourths of the facility managers have education qualification of HSC or below.

In RMG sector the practice of formal training is still not so popular. Only technicians and managerial staff undergo some formal training system but for the workers and supervisors the skills are acquired through on-the-job training and informal learning. The majority of the workers are trained only by their own experience. Only a small number of entrepreneurs have been investing for developing the skills of their workforce.

The apparel industry in Bangladesh needs to draw the attention of the youth of the country, to show them the huge career opportunities that can be found in the RMG sector. This could include a stronger emphasis on providing young people with a basic understanding of the sector, including the various levels of employment opportunities at the floor level, to management and beyond.

In education, there is an urging need for greater levels of knowledge transfer between education and business. Many western countries have clear and well-developed knowledge transfer partnerships in place which sees ideas developed in colleges and universities being shared


---
**Page 203**
---

with the industry Bangladesh also needs to think carefully about how it can develop an education system to provide the RMG sector with the industry leaders of the future who will allow the sector to compete at the higher end of the value chain and address the need for competent middle management. It is unfortunate that the RMG industry in Bangladesh often hires managers from outside the country, which means that they are not able to tap into an abundance of potential talent domestically available—if only they were nurtured the right way. Around 13 universities of the country's garments factories have employment forecasts in the top positions for next year, according to a report by GMRD in 2018. However, in the absence of a skilled workforce, particularly in merchandising, design and marketing, as well as in quality control, Bangladesh is unable to compete with other countries that export from China, Japan, India, and Pakistan to fill the gap. Out of 126 universities (46 public and 80 private) there are two universities in the country—BGMEA University of Textiles and Engineering (BUTEX) and Bangladesh University of Textiles (BUTEK)—that have been established for the garments and textiles industry specifically. Although through the numbers are not up to par, these two universities are producing skilled graduates for the garment industry. However, other public and private universities should also create courses relevant to the RMG sector as it is a potential industry in the country.

Moreover, current industry leaders have a vital role to play here, visiting schools and colleges and providing input to government agencies about the long-term needs of the RMG industry. There is an urgent need to develop a cadre of upper level managers. The country's business school graduates are not equipped with the skills that RMG can develop training programmes for lower level managers to become upper level managers. Bangladesh should stop dependence on expatriate managers. Producing high skilled workers for the RMG industry will contribute to the economy by reducing the amount of foreign remittance that leaves the country.

At present the demand for skilled workers is increasing. Besides, it is becoming difficult for new women to get involved in this sector due to its labor-intensive nature of work. Women are quite reluctant to enter into this sector as they have to work long hours and switch from private to level make women skilled, it will be difficult for them to sustain in this profession. Moreover, Woman worker cannot work more than seven years on average. A number of experts have also expressed with the similar sake a large section of women workers quit jobs after having a child.

### 5.5 Gender Perspectives of Energy Efficiency

Different opinions were gathered on gender perspectives of energy efficiency from the stakeholder engagement workshop where a wide range of energy efficiency practitioners including regulators, policy makers, experts, academics, researchers and garment industry representatives participated in the discussion. A total of 102 people participated. During the discussion it was identified that the women who are joining mainly in the small scale units are not aware about the energy efficient practices nor expertise is being created. With increased automation and use of advanced technology in machines, already a decreasing trend of female workers is seen in the past 4 years.


---
**Page 204**
---

might reduce even more if they cannot compete with the male workers in terms of technical knowledge and skills. Some recent studies also showed similar types of findings.

According to a study, it was considered that women workers are more vulnerable than their male counterparts because their jobs are in automation in 5-wage sectors in Bangladesh including the readymade garment industry. It said women in low-wage occupations with less education are likely to be impacted more due to high risk of automation in readymade garment and textile industries. The study also mentioned that women workers were working in informal, irregular and seasonal employment sectors at the beginning of Fourth Industrial Revolution (4IR).10) The research also mentioned that the women workers were not getting any training or education after completing their secondary level of education. To overcome this situation, more trainings and knowledge sharing activities need to be promoted especially for the female workers as they are generally the ones who lack a sense of education and technical skills compared to the male workers in the context of Bangladesh.

Another issue identified during stakeholder consultation was considering special needs of men and women while installing any energy efficient equipment. For instance, replacing regular lights with LED lights can increase lighting coverage more than doubling lighting efficiency. However, some studies by BKMEA showed that workers face difficulties with color shading when LED lights are used and it ultimately caused eyesight damage especially to women in the readymade garment industry who work under low light conditions. So there should be some standard for choosing energy efficient machinery and equipment has no negative impact on health.

### 5.6 Decision Making

Bangladesh RMG sector plays a significant role to reduce poverty and to increase women empowerment. The RMG sector has been envisaging in the country since long time as a catalyst for sustainable development and growth. Apart from job opportunities, the RMG sector gave underprivileged women to step out of their homes and live independently. Through working in the sectors, women workers have gained financial independence which improved their family quality. Also, higher education can give increased wage which encouraged a lot of poor and underprivileged women to complete secondary (SSC) and higher secondary (HSC) level of education. Women empowerment also increases women’s participation in decision making at family level. Moreover, this sector plays a significant role in changing mindset of women. These working women realize the importance of education and tend to spend more on their children’s education.

However, there is still a gap in terms of decision making at the workplace. Workers’ organization/committees of different forms are available at workplaces but most of the women workers generally are not members of the organization/committees available at workplaces. Moreover, majority are not members of any organization/association at workplace.


---
**Page 205**
---

202

While women workers are increasingly becoming representatives at the factory level, they are still not being equally represented. Also, leadership positions in union confederations and federations are still largely dominated by men.

This is partly due to widely-recognized gender ideologies, as well as to the reality that women are still expected to assume the majority of childcare responsibilities after they return home, discouraging them from serving as trade union representatives.

### 5.7 Wage and Benefits

In every five year, the government forms a new Minimum Wage Board for the RMG sector to formulate a new wage structure for apparel workers. The board is usually led by a senior district judge or magistrate. In addition to representative from the owners and workers in the board, it also consists of three independent members. The representatives of owners and workers are elected through their respective organizations. The government of Bangladesh gazette sets Taka 78,000 as the minimum monthly pay for RMG workers setting Taka 97,711 as the minimum monthly pay in 2019.

The survey results showed that in RMG sector there is no gender-based discrimination in terms of wages and other benefits. In terms of gender equality, the sector has very low wage discrimination between male and female workers. However, women are paid less than their male counterparts. Also, there is fair compensation provided to workers as per their pay grades. Most of the workers generally get festival bonus. Women workers get no lunch break. However, except lunch time they work for 12 hours.

However, knowledge among the women workers about calculation of overtime compensation is poor. Three in every four women do not know the calculation method. So, the authority can manipulate them easily. Also, there is a discrimination in disbursement of salary on time. According to the survey, 50% of female workers get their salaries after 14 days and sometimes even up to 30 days after the due date. But in case of female workers there is often a delay in salary disbursement. Moreover, on terms of trainings and other opportunities women are less promoted. As a result, they get lower pay than men.

This is one of the reasons why there are few women in the supervisor and managerial positions.

### 5.8 Workplace Environment

The study also revealed that overall working conditions for women are favorable - wage discrimination and discriminations of gender in the sector is largely absent; special benefits are provided for women with special needs (like infrastructure for lactating mothers, maternity leaves etc.). The incidence of workplace harassment does exist and in case any such cases are reported, there is a robust redress mechanism in place. Also, examples of child labor are also limited.

However, the scenario is only present in large scale factories and mostly absent in medium and small-scale factories. The workers from medium and small factories still suffer from unrelenting working condition; not having standard working hours, inadequate breaks during working hours and inadequate worker safety. Workplaces of the women workers are not free


---
**Page 207**
---

from harassment. In other words, women workers experience different forms of harassment at workplace. Of the different forms, verbal abuse/harassment is the most common one. The reason behind this contrasting level of compliance between large scale and medium/small scale units is the growing concern of the buyers in social, health, safety and gender considerations.

Sexual harassment has decreased in the garments sectors in recent years due to strict rules and monitoring at the factories. Most of the large enterprises are highly compliant ensuring safety and security of their workers and there is functional sexual harassment complaint committee on paper, but their functionality and transparency are questionable. However, in all cases the workers have less trust on the committees and most of the time do not file any complaints. The number of women workers who are aware about the existence of existing committees for sexual harassment and the rate of sexual harassment is decreasing.

Post Rana Plaza, there was a severe market and media pressure on the buyers i.e. large apparel brands to fulfill their social responsibility and ensure better working conditions for the workers in Bangladesh. In response to this pressure, the buyers have suggested compliance with safety and health norms from the manufacturing units and would withdraw business in case these norms are not met. The Accord10 and Alliance11 were developed and actively implemented by the buyers to comply with their social responsibility in the areas of improvement. This prompted the large industries, which are in direct business with the buyers, to comply with safety and health norms from medium scale units as well as for medium scale units indirectly since they rarely have forward linkages with the final buyers and mostly supply inputs for these large-scale units. Hence, there is a large scope of improving the social, health and safety conditions for the medium and small-scale units.

Women workers are mostly deprived of some protection related facilities at workplace. High prevalence of pregnancy and child birth among workers concerning the social protection facilities are a striking feature in this regard for a long time. However, the situation is changing now. At present the factories try to comply with existing regulations and provide facilities to female workers.


10 The Accord is an independent, legally binding agreement between brands and trade unions to work towards a safe and healthy garment and textile industry in Bangladesh.
11 The Alliance for Bangladesh Worker Safety (alliance) is a legally binding, five-year commitment to improve safety in Bangladeshi ready-made garment (RMG) factories.

## Social beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes

Participation of women is increasing in the Bangladeshi labor market, but socio-culturally constructed norms envisage that women and men's position is not equal. Women tend to be concentrated in certain low-paying, low-skilled sectors such as the garment industry, and within it, they are mainly employed in jobs like cutting, sewing, ironing, buttoning, and helpers. Due to stereotypes regarding women’s and men’s aspirations, preferences, capital accumulation, employers’ perceptions of women's and men's skills and attitudes are affected. This trend has been further reinforced by the last decade or so when more and more technology-intensive occupations become increasingly skilled and better paid, employment opportunities for female workers in these sectors have decreased while all other female workers are decreased in Bangladesh in the RMG sector mainly because of—

- Increased wage structure attracts male workers to this job and owners prefer male over female workers.
- Due to automation and lack of skill in female workers.
- Female workers are more likely to leave their jobs or after having children. This is also a reason why owner prefer male workers more.
- Safety issues need to be assured for female workers and mostly they do not want to stay for night shift.

Moreover, most of the female labors in the garment industries are mainly involved in cutting, sewing, hemming, ironing, buttoning, and tidying. This is obviously seen as a gender-based skills compared to men whereas man mainly occupy supervisor or managerial positions. Women workers are also excluded from decision-making processes on new designs and fashions. Women workers whom called "unknolied" are identified as marginal and low-paid labors and excluded from organizational and managerial training. Thus, women are marginalized in managerial roles because gender stereotypical beliefs and practices.

Like many other countries, the participation of women in market economy especially in the managerial positions is still very limited in Bangladesh and mostly affected on limits by gender nature or stereotypes. Nevertheless, when men and women are in similar professional positions, they receive equal pay for work done. Women's wages in Bangladesh although government job payment system is equal for men and women, but non-government jobs salary is varied for men and women, especially in managerial position. Male than female mainly occupy supervisor or managerial positions in the garment industries and organizations, which represent that strong gender stereotypes exist in the organizations. Besides these, women’s pregnancy and child rearing are further barrier to achieving stable or top most

[1] Habiba T (2004) Paid Work and Socio-Political Consciousness of Garment Workers in Bangladesh. Journal of Contemporary Asia 34: 145-160.


---
**Page 211**
---

position of an organization[10]. Trade Union is still dominated by male, whereas women’s position is as a response[4][5].

It was observed from the Kii and field survey that there is less participation of women in management positions and technical positions. From social perspective, male ratio is higher in management positions and roles. It will be solved by creating awareness and providing training programs. There are some barriers like mind-set or cognitive biases against women, lack of self-confidence among women. These can be overcome through training.

### 5.10 Access to Information

There is wide variation in practice of leave provisions. Among several types of leave weekly holiday is the most enjoyed. Many workers are even unaware about the practice of leave at their workplaces. Night duty is mandatory for many women workers. In other words, when night duty is mandatory, there will be no leave for them. There have been reports that some workers who lack awareness in this regard. Lack of awareness has also been among many workers regarding practice of taking written consent from workers for night duty. However, largest proportion of workers are unaware about the practice of taking written consent form.

Not all employers generally do provide workers with information regarding rights at workplaces. One in every four workers claims that their employers do not inform workers in this regard. RMG workers who have faced/observed accidents (pricking of needle during work, cutting finger or hand) are unaware about the practice of taking written consent from them. About 40 percent workers have this experience. There are also workers, though few, who don’t know whether any accident took place at their workplaces. Most workplaces maintain/have made available a notice board for informing workers regarding rights and responsibilities. However, still that some factories have not made enough arrangement. Many workers do not know whether organizations/committees exist at workplaces.

Presently, most of the garments have their own anti-sexual harassment policies and any issues regarding sexual harassment are dealt with strictness. See, number of cases of sexual harassment has decreased in recent years. However, in many cases the victims do file complaint due lack of trust for just getting justice or they feel embarrassed. Also, they do not know whether there is a procedure to deal with such complaints or how to follow these procedures. To address these issues, sexual harassment should be added during the grievance redressal. Also, awareness of the workers should be increased about the committees and the complaint filing process.

# 5.11 Women and Health

According to ILO, employers must ensure basic standards for health and safety in the workplace. In Bangladesh labour Law-2009, specified the rights of health and hygiene in the section (51, 52, 53, 54, 56, 57, 60). Despite the specific code in the sections, health and safety violations are common in garment factories. Women in Bangladesh are more disabled than men due to poor living conditions. The factory is not well ventilated; most of the closed windows, light and air is not sufficient in RMG factories. Toilets are not sufficient for the crowd. So, toilet remain dirty all the time proper water supply can’t properly supplied harmful gas emission from the toilet for huge crowd and the environment of factory remains stinky most of the time.

Most of the female RMG workers live in urban slums or semi slums where clean drinking water, hygiene and sanitation health care are inaccessible due to scarcity and their low wages. Also, studies show that RMG workers have poor living conditions. The female workers working in fabrics section (stitching, colouring, dye etc) are exposed to toxic elements and inhale dust of fabric which cause different breathing and respiratory diseases and from the exposure of chemicals they suffer from skin problems like dermatitis, eczema, gastric, dysentery, hepatitis, diabetes, respiratory problem, gynecological problems, tuberculosis, asthma, bronchitis, etc. The RMG workers are exposed to heat for a long working hour lead them to suffer from other than thermal effects such as neck pain, shoulder pain, knee pain, back pain, joint pain, tenderness, ligaments and bones. Also, the noisy environment of RMG factories causes hearing loss in female workers. Most RMG workers receive only BD 200 as medical allowance as per wages boards, 2010 but their average monthly medicine expense is BD 497.5 which is more than double what they are provided.

Few RMG workers get the day care and even in many garment factories, mothers do not have space or time to breastfeed. Breastfeeding rates by working women in garment factories can be as low as 10 percent (UNICEF, 2014). Workplaces of these workers are not gender sensitive and they do not have access to sanitary napkins at workplace; most of the factories also do not arrange women doctors. Unwanted physical contact and sexual harassment by lineman, supervisor and other male workers is a common problem in RMG factories. The RMG employers often threaten female workers about babies to retain their job to their poor economic condition.

# 5.12 Access to Financial Services by RMG Workers

Even though the majority of the RMG workers are females, their wages are not up to mark to satisfy them even after working for a long time (including overtime). Consequently, they do not


---
**Page 213**
---

210

have access to basic needs such as nutritious food or better clothing, medicines etc. A study conducted on female Garment workers in Chittagong Metropolitan area identified the insecure condition of the female workers who are still trapped in the vicious cycle of poverty.[4]

A recent study conducted by Care Bangladesh indicates that female RMG workers want to use financial services, for benefits that include loans, savings, their own bank/Bkash accounts, etc.[5] According to the IFC data, there is a stark gender gap in access to financial services among RMG workers. Only 30% of women have formal bank accounts compared to 65 percent of men.[6] The study also indicated that many female RMG workers may not have full control over their earnings and savings. According to the IFC, giving women more say to exercise greater management of her finances may empower her and facilitate greater savings and more financial control. Furthermore, due to the limited amount of wages, they cannot save much for the future.

Given the economic significance of the readymade garment sector, which is dominated by female workers, digitizing wage payments through MFS can have a significant impact on women's financial inclusion. Almost about 90% of factories pay their workers in cash, which is a limitation to financial inclusion and savings.[7] However, if RMG factories use MFS accounts to receive their salaries, they can avail a broad range of financial services. Through IFC’s initiative, over 70,000 female workers benefitted from digital wage payments to their mobile phones through the Bangladesh Bank's Bkash service in 2016.[8] This enables them to send money, build savings and make payments securely. However, non-availability of financial literacy among women is a major barrier for awareness about accounts, and difficulty in covering fixed costs incurred by MFS providers are among major constraints in wage digitalization in readymade garment industry in Bangladesh.[9]

5.13 Leadership Opportunities for Women in RMG Sector

In Bangladesh, textile and garments sector is entirely male dominated. Even though the present elected president of BGMEA is a woman, there is no existing database on women entrepreneurs or women involved in the higher management positions in the textile and garments industry.

According to the Global Gender Gap Report 2020, companies with female-majority ownership in Bangladesh are only 1.7%, whereas firms with female top managers are 4.8%. During the period of 2015-2019, women’s representation in senior management positions as per the IFC report, women in leadership and in organizational decision-making in businesses as well as in the company's board are very few, because of social stigma towards women. # Gender Inclusion Practices in Bangladesh

Equal rights for women can be best secured by a combination of legislative measures as well as awareness of the business owners and their management about the advantages to be gained through employment of workers based on their capabilities rather than gender or other personal attributes. Recent government initiatives including amendments to the Labour Law in 2013 and the introduction of the Women’s Safety Act in 2015, the establishment of the Bangladesh Tripartite Consultative Committee (TCC) in 2014, and the introduction of training initiatives including those related to effective workplace suspension policies and women’s safety at work have all contributed significantly. The efforts by the BIGMEA, SKMEA and BEF, with support from ILO-ITC since 2014, to establish a culture of occupational safety and health and respect at the factory level, go some way to improving the situation.

Equal responsibility within the RMG industry has been greatly helped by initiatives launched by the Bangladesh government and other bodies, which include the recently formed Tripartite Consultative Committee (TCC) that addresses labour and workplace related issues in the RMG sector. The TCC was established under the Labour Act 2016 to replace the former ILO-ITC committee. It was established specifically to deal with all unresolved or disputed issues within the workplace. Initiatives such as these, together with the work undertaken with the guidance of Accord on Fire and Building Safety (AFBS) and SA8000, have ensured that social, environmental and sustainable standards are not only considered the norm, but also the minimum level that any RMG entity today has to abide by in order to gain necessary compliance.

Promoting equal participation of men and women in trade unions can play a crucial role in ensuring rights and interests of workers especially raising women workers’ issues such as sexual reproductive health rights, sexual harassment, adopting a gender approach to OSH, leadership roles for women etc. The RMG sector has made significant progress by including all policies and programmes, including collective bargaining to promote women’s rights. However, Trade unions' activities must be focused, robust, based, efficient and aligned with regulatory frameworks and the international labour standards provisions provided in the Bangladesh Labour Act (2006) and the Labour Rules (2016).

Bangladesh RMG industry is subject to the harsh scrutiny regarding all aspects of health and safety at work, compliance and structural standards following the tragic events of April 23, 2013 at Rana Plaza. The Bangladesh government has taken several measures in this regard since then, implemented since that fateful day, have ensured that the RMG sector has greatly improved and now has 7 of the 14 ILO-OSH Conventions ratified by Bangladesh. This is a significant shift towards international standards throughout the industry. Level of compliance is high for those industries which have realized the business need of complying with safety regulations. In this regard, it should be noted that the RMG sector in Bangladesh has been suffering from the disastrous effect the lack of safety can have on not only the workers but also on overall business prospects of the RMG units. The key reasons for non-compliance with applicable


---
**Page 216**
---

regulations would extra cost of compliance which in some cases are an added burden to the limited financial capacity. In current market scenario, with business margins getting constrained, this added cost is leading to disparity in competitive advantage – with non-compliant companies having spare resources for creating market outreach. However, awareness and understanding about the importance of compliance has increased significantly; if compliance will further increase in the days ahead, with market pressure increasing as well.

However, the investment to achieve such improvements is not always recognized by the immediate customer, and there is an unhealthy culture that regards Bangladesh as a low-cost source. It is important for the industry to recognize this fact and also to respect the investments and the advances made by the RMG industry in Bangladesh and also realize that these changes have come about through significant investment in both plant and machinery and that, in a world of escalating raw material costs, some price levels are artificially suppressed.

# 7 Legal and Administrative Framework Protecting Promoting Gender Equality in Bangladesh

Bangladeshi women have been struggling to establish their rights in family, society and in the state. In reality, women are still facing discrimination, exclusion and injustice and have a negligible representation at all levels of government and public administration in Bangladesh. Discriminatory laws and policies hinder formal equality and socio-political conditions prevent women from exercising their rights.

The implementation of women development policy and work plan is the basic responsibility of the Government. This responsibility can be discharged on sound basis through building up a well-organized, efficient and transparent system. Having under-representation of women at different levels, the government of Bangladesh has been implementing different steps to ensure gender equality in all spheres of life. Since the implementation of Vision 2021 and 2041, a momentum has been created for taking forward Bangladesh to a middle and high-income level respectively. The GoB has undertaken various programs to materialize Vision 2021 and 2041 and to ensure gender equality in all spheres of life as well as in the overall development. The Seventh Five-Year Plan (2016-2021) of the Bangladesh government considers women's engagement in political and economic activities as a crosscutting issue and one of its major goals is to achieve gender equality. In order to ensure gender equality, the government has shown its commitment to attaining the Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 5 on achieving gender equality and empowering women and implementing the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Platform for Action.

There is a dedicated ministry, the Ministry of women and Children Affairs (MoWCA) (for the development and welfare of women in the country. Also, the Ministry of Labour and Employment has been working with labor and social issues have particular concern on ensuring gender equality in the employment sector and to achieve that they promote different types of capacity building programs for women workers. The GoB has also implemented various programs to alleviate poverty of women, stop abuse of women, stop trafficking of women, and ensure safety in all areas including their workplace with a view to ensuring full and equal participation of women in economic activities. The Ministry of Women and Children Affairs and Employment have taken initiatives to provide capacity building trainings and leadership programs to women working in RMG sector78 BGMEA & BMA also have training and capacity building programs for women workers. The GoB has also been promoting potential female workers for obtaining higher education by providing them scholarships.

Bangladesh has already substantially achieved the MDGs as it has achieved gender parity in primary and secondary education at the national level, among other successes of the MDGs. Bangladesh has made significant progress towards achieving the SDG 5 on gender equality by ensuring their equal and active participation in the mainstream socio-economic activities and removing the various impediments to their empowerment. In order to attain the SDG goal of gender equality, the government of Bangladesh has been implementing different initiatives so


---
**Page 218**
---

that gender equality and women empowerment can be ensured in the country. The following are some important initiatives of the government.

Since 2009 the gender-sensitive budget has been planned by the Government of Bangladesh to ensure the participation of women in various programmes. At present, all the ministries have adopted gender-responsive budgeting and have made accountable contributions towards spending their allocation for the economic and social empowerment of women. Allocation for women under the budget is Tk 368.7 million (0.59% of total budget) for the current fiscal year.

To ensure gender equality and protection of and security to women, the government of Bangladesh has formed several acts and policies. These include:

> Maternity Act, 1924: The Act provides that every woman given power to take steps by linking Section 509 of the Bangladeshi Penal Code in the schedule of Mobile Court Act. This would result and prevent eve teasing and sexual harassment of the girls and women.

> Citizenship Act (amended), 1980: The provision for giving citizenship by birth to children born outside Bangladesh was introduced under this Act on 25th June, 2006.

> Dowry Prohibition Act, 1980: Prevention of financial obligation to provide gifts and assets

> Women and Children Repression Prevention Act, 2000: To ensure the safety and protection to women at homes and their surroundings.

> Domestic Violence (Prevention and Protection) Act, 2010: Prevention of domestic violence, protection of women and children from domestic violence

> Prevention and Suppression of Human Trafficking Act, 2012: To prevent trafficking for sexual exploitation and other purposes.

> High Court (2009) ruled to include mother's name in all official documents, school admission forms, either alone or with that of the father. Consequently, climate change adaptation policy is being developed for women due to the lack of climate adaptive capacity.

> Legal Aid Act, 2010: An Act to provide legal aid for the litigants who are incapable of seeking justice due to financial insolvency, destitution, helplessness and for various socio-economic conditions.

> Anti-Human Trafficking Act, 2010:

> Human Trafficking (Deterrence and Suppression) Act, 2012: The Act aims to restrain and suppress human trafficking, to ensure the protection of victims of the offence. Any person who is found guilty under this act shall be punishable under this act, if any offence has been committed regarding human trafficking, anyone can file a complaint with the police or prosecution of the Code of Criminal Procedure (1896), to the police or Tribunal and whose case may be conceded for the security purpose.

8 Alignment of the Project with GCF Gender Assessment Guideline

According to GCF guideline, gender analysis needs to take place early in the planning process so that an understanding of gender roles and power relations is built into the project. All project components should be analyzed for their impact on women and men as well as other groups within the overall situation analysis. In this project survey was conducted in selected 20 RMG factories among 70 workers and 20 facility managers. The survey questions were based on education, work experience, gender roles, power relations, participation in decision making process, existing facilities on the factory, gender considerations etc.

The project reviewed the existing policies, strategies and frameworks related to labor participation especially considered gender aspects both at national and international level. The project also considered the existing laws and regulations that affect women workers. In addition, the project considered common held beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes related to gender, the division of labor among women and men and the participation between women and men in decision making process. The survey was conducted through focus group discussions at the stakeholder engagement workshops. Kil and factory surveys it was ensured that both male and female participants take part in the discussions and give their valuable opinion. However, data were collected from 100% of the male respondents as they are more likely to be present in higher management, research or academic line. Still the survey tried to ensure participation of female respondents as much as possible.

During the first and second workshops respectively 15% and 11% female participants were present respectively. The workshops were designed to share ideas around a wide range of energy efficiency practitioners including industry policy makers experts, academics, researchers and government sector entrepreneurs, as well as environment, social gender experts. The workshop was attended by representatives from the government agencies like BGEA, BKMEA, garment units along with development partners NGOs, academic and research organizations and other stakeholders who were interested in issues related to RMG sector in Bangladesh especially environmental, social and gender concern.

During Kil 25% respondents were women and 50% of the respondents had knowledge and/or experience on working with gender related issues in the RMG sector in Bangladesh. Especially, environment and energy efficiency management (EEM) Ministry of Environment & Forest Climate Change (MEFCCC), EECB, BKMEA, BGEA and BKMEA under Ministry of Foreign Affairs shared their views and knowledge regarding gender considerations in RMG sector. The workshop was attended by representatives from BGEA and BKMEA who had first-hand experience on gender issues and providing capacity and knowledge building training to both male and female workers, promote female workers with potential for leadership roles and provide training on health and safety as well as health and safety related issues. The researchers and academics were also directly involved in addressing gender issues in the RMG sector and working for gender equality within the sector.

During factory survey more than 40% respondents were female workers. However, during the survey both facility managers there were no female respondents as the selected field units did not have any female facility managers till the survey was conducted.


---
**Page 221**
---

Moreover, the project considered the existing gender inequalities and situation of women and men in the interventions related energy efficiency and emphasize more on capacity building on energy efficiency for both male and female workers. Also, access to financial resources and control over productive assets are important factors that need to be addressed. In addition, it will address differential needs/priorities of women and men in the context of the project.

The project will ensure the following issues in the gender action plan:

- Services and technologies provided by the project/program will be available and accessible to both women and men.
- Equal access for the women and men from vulnerable communities to information and opportunities necessary to participate and benefit fully from the anticipated outcomes of the project.
- Ensure capacity development activities of disadvantaged groups
- Encourage women entrepreneurship in RMG sector
- Encourage women participation in public sector and private sector in higher management
- Promote leadership capacity of female workers

The information gathered from the gender analysis/assessment should be considered in all stages of the project cycle: design, formulation, implementation, monitoring and evaluation. The project will ensure that the gender equality is mainstreamed into the project activities, looking at ways the project/program can address gender inequalities that emerge from the project; ensure the differential needs of women and men are addressed; ensure women and men have equal access to resources and opportunities for participation in decision-making; ensure equal participation of women and men in management arrangements and as beneficiaries, partners and key stakeholders; and ensure women’s equal participation in decision – making processes.

Moreover, the Ministry of Women and Children's Affairs (MoWiCA) and Ministry of Labour and Employment (MoLE) will be involved in the project activities. While developing the training modules for capacity building, consultations will be done with these respective ministries as well to bring their inputs on gender equality issues. The MoWiCA and MoLE can also provide opportunities regarding capacity building of industrial workers, especially underprivileged women who help to develop the proposed actions in a sustainable way.
