9 Grievance Redressal System (GRS)

Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) is recognized worldwide as one of the important indicators of an organization's proficiency and efficiency. Grievance redressal is, in fact, a governance-related management process. In this case, the GRM will help to identify whether the stakeholders are satisfied with the completion of the sub-projects or whether they are facing any negative impacts due the implementation and operation of the sub-projects.

Management staff in the RMG sector come from varied backgrounds and do not necessarily have professional management trainings to understand and address issues related to well-being of people. Therefore, it is important that the system should be user-friendly.

In this project the End Borrower would ensure that grievance redress procedures are in place and would monitor those procedures to ensure that grievances are handled properly. The End Borrower will establish a procedure to answer sub-project-related queries and address complaints. This will include mechanisms for resolving disputes, including disagreements regarding the assessment and mitigation of environmental and social impacts.

Generally, the grievance redress committees (GRC) are of two types: (i) formal courts of appeal and (ii) informal committees. The former is a judicial body that has the power to order compensation but may reduce the problem significantly. Grievance Redress Committee (GRC) will be formed.

The GRC will ensure proper presentation of facts and grievances, as well as impartial hearing of complaints and disputes. The GRC will meet at least once every quarter to discuss each case and fix a date for hearing and notify the aggrieved persons to submit necessary documentary evidence in support of their claims. The GRC will also be responsible for keeping record of complaint. Additional details regarding the functioning of GRC is presented in the SMF.

A general guideline for the GRM will be developed by the LFs in consultation with the MoWCA and BGMEA. It should be ensured that the GRM within the RMG units are aligned with the GCP Independent Redress Mechanism as well as the Accredited Entity's Grievance Redress Mechanism (e.g., contact details, accessibility and basic procedures of such mechanisms, etc.).

Grievance Redress Committee (GRC) will be formed and established at each project site. Grievance response focal point will be available at each shelter site for instant response to an aggrieved person. The GRC will consist of the members from the PIU (Please refer Gender Inclusive Project Implementation Unit). It is recommended that women participate in monitoring of the activities under the program and representation by workers (one male and one female). This GRC will be responsible for reporting to the LFs, District Grievance Redress Committee (DGRC) and the LGSM. The Grievance Redress Committee (GRC) will be same for ESF & gender issue.

The following issues should be considered during a successful GRM:

User manual: An easy to understand instruction document should be provided for the GRM for the general user wishing to avail the proposed central GRM.

User-friendliness: The system should not be too complicated either in technical terms or in language for the general public irrespective of education background.


---
**Page 223**
---

End-to-end tracking: Tracking of grievance both by the complainant and the authority for grievance redressal is very important.

Confidentiality, security and anonymity: Keeping complainant's identity a secret is very important to ensure their safety. Encouraging employees to lodge their grievances anonymously would also ensure confidentiality and security.

Progress monitoring: Grievance should be addressed within 7 days of receiving. A grievance redressal system is no good if the grievances are not addressed regularly. When the grievances are pending for a long time then the employees will lose faith from the system and stop sharing their issues.

Feedback option: There should be a feedback option to measure client satisfaction and collect opinions on the effectiveness of the system.

**Page 224**

# Recommendations

The issue of gender equality has immense significance as it is not only a fundamental human right but also a necessary foundation for a peaceful, prosperous and sustainable world.[28] Providing women and girls with equal access to education, health care, decent work, and recreation will help them become more productive members of society. This will lead to more stable economies and benefit societies and humanity at large. Implementing new legal frameworks regarding the rights of women and girls is crucial for ending gender-based discrimination prevalent in many countries around the world. Thus, several initiatives are required to be taken to augment the process of attaining gender equality in Bangladesh.

In order to create a gender equitable environment at the workplace, the following initiatives can be implemented:

- Create enabling environments for hiring workers based on their skills rather than gender.
- Ensure compliance by issuing guidelines and ensuring effective implementation of different laws and regulations in terms of wages and other facilities.
- Ensure training and capacity development workshops for both male and female workers.
- Assure that women are not forced to leave their jobs due to social pressure or responsibilities.
- Capacity development of female RMG workers to avail financial services and awareness on economic activities.
- Advocacy needs to be done with the family members to engage women in the family and community level economic activities so that women can be financially independent and take care of their families.

Building a network among women, influential community and educational leaders is important for creating an enabling environment by bringing change into society.

- Provide safe transportation facilities especially for women so that they can join work in different shifts as well as men without getting concerned about security issues.
- Provide health insurance and other amenities to workers for increasing their health which will eventually increase their performance.
- Ensure special provisions for pregnant and lactating mothers and maternity leave should be an entitlement.
- Provision of daycare facilities by the factory authority as well as community.
- Facilitate gender responsive and socially inclusive training in the RMG units.
- Enhance women's participation in decision-making processes.

Take initiatives to foster equality in the decision-making process and encourage participation of both male and female workers in trade bodies or labour unions for development of policies.

Implement different programmes which would facilitate women’s access over resources.

Organise capacity-building events for industry personnel on gender sensitivity and equal opportunities.

Ensure proper occupational health and safety for all the workers irrespective of gender.

Ensure harassment-free working environment for the workers regardless of gender.

# 11 Gender Action Plan

## 11.1 Outline

A project/program-specific Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a tool used to ensure gender mainstreaming is clearly visible in project/program design and implementation. The project GAP is not a separate component; it mirrors the logical framework of the project and is an integral part of project/program design. GAPs include clear targets, gender design features and mechanisms to ensure implementation of the gender design elements.

The GAP presents:
- Strategies to address gender issues in the project/program
- Targets and design features included in the project/program to address gender concerns and ensure tangible benefits to women and men, especially from vulnerable communities.
- Mechanisms to ensure implementation of the gender design elements;
- Gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation indicators.

Below is presented a GAP that contains impact, outcome and output statements, gender-responsive activities, gender-performance indicators and targets, timeline, responsibility lines, and approximate budgetary allocations against each activity.

11.2 Monitoring of Gender Action Plan

Ensuring the sustainability of the gender action plan is a major concern and to achieve this a proper monitoring system will be in place. At the sub-project level, there will be Project Implementation Units (PIU) who will be responsible for the proper implementation of the gender related interventions. The PIU will submit periodic monitoring reports (yearly) to the project management unit (PMU). The PMU will then monitor whether the PIUs are following the guidelines and processes participating in monitoring of the activities under the program. The LFs will then monitor whether the end borrowers (i.e., RMG units) are following the policies and guidelines regarding gender related concerns as per the plan implemented ensuring gender equality and women empowerment within the sector.

The selected LFs involved in the program have experience on monitoring gender related issues. Most of their RMG clients work with renowned international buyers who have certain conditions for the supply of goods. In order to meet these conditions, they follow certain guidelines to ensure gender equality and women empowerment. Thus, the LFs are well aware of the gender related concerns and are capable of monitoring the overall gender related activities within the sector.

For the Gender Action Plan of this programme, the LFs have agreed to conduct onsite as well as offsite periodic inspection for ensuring proper practice of gender related issues in the projects. In the appraisal of loan proposal, they will assess the client's willingness and ability to implement the gender action plan. The LFs will also monitor whether the borrowers are cooperative in terms of gender related concerns and they will establish a monitoring mechanism within their organization. The LFs will conduct annual assessment after the project implementation is completed. Based on the findings from this assessment, it will be assessed whether the end borrowers have implemented the gender issues mentioned in the gender action plan and created enabling environment of increased women empowerment.

The structure of the PIU will be same as proposed in the ESMF.

# Introduction

Government of Bangladesh committed to various energy consumption reduction targets in the past few years and adopted EBC programs like Energy Management Program, EE Labeling Program, EE Buildings Program, target at large energy consuming entities and equipment in the industrial, residential and commercial sectors. The textile sector has been a major hub for electricity demand and ready-made garments production1) has one of the world’s fastest growing economies, largely driven by its exports in textile and readymade garments. The textile sector alone contributes to above 12% in GDP with employment of more than 3 million people.

To meet the demands from world over, the sector has been growing and expanding rapidly over the last few years. With concentration of more production units, the electricity consumption in the sector is increasing and so is the employment of the workforce. The textile labour market has its own set of challenges such as low wages, long working hours, poor working conditions, gender pay ratio disparities. Besides, the industrial challenges for the workforce are low wages, exhausting long shifts, lack of safety, unsafe, hazardous working conditions, sexual harassment/discrimination and lack of human resource protection.

Evidently, deriving from the above-mentioned facts, gender inequalities affect more women than men in this case, given the fact, women are more personally, socially, economically and politically challenged. Women constitute 70% of the workforce in Bangladesh’s textile sector but they are in abundant supply at a very low cost. However, in textile units, women employment is less due to the manufacturing intensive processes2). Stereotypically it isn’t favoured by owners to employ women, due to their lower productivity and higher absenteeism rates than men in textile units. Therefore, presence of women is very less and the rest few that are working as textile units as directors, managers, supervisors, etc., are mostly from upper class families with better education and legal rights, house/asset ownership, bank credits, education and skill development, economic resources, and health services.

Bangladesh’s Third National Communication (TNC) to UNFCCC clearly states that Bangladesh is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world and the challenges posed by climate change make the lives and livelihoods of millions of people increasingly complex and vulnerable. Bangladesh is prone to extreme weather events such as cyclones, floods, droughts, etc., and these impacts are enhancing vulnerabilities for women due to the lack of climate adaptive capacities. Struggles in coping up with the impacts of climate disasters, living in extreme unhealthy working conditions, and low wages make women more vulnerable to poverty, malnutrition, unhygienic clothes and drapes that reduce the possibility of escaping the plan of climate event and man-made disasters like fire, are few to name a few.

To understand the delicate balance between energy efficiency and gender equality, there is a need to understand the importance of energy efficient processes and equipment to influence the life of workers both socially and economically by deep diving into the socio-economic status of the textile workforce which determines the human and economic development in the past and the upon technology


1) Raw material for RMG sector is fabric’s produced locally. Hence, RMG is a textile dependent sector.
2) As quoted by Bangladesh Textile Mills Association (BTMA). Available at https://www.kmthilaka.com/
3) Primary data sources (field survey and interviews)
4) TNC
(https://unfccc.int/sites/default/files/resource/TNC%20Report%202016%28Review%29%202003_01_2019.pdf)

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment Report

### 1.1. Programme and Project Relevance

The programme for promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for textile sector of Bangladesh aims to reach out to the business establishments in the textile sector, particularly women-owned enterprises, to provide them with market resources to avail investment opportunities for energy saving technology upgrades. The programme objectives have clearly been laid out to ensure direct and indirect benefits to the vulnerable and underprivileged groups including women and children. The programme is designed as a gender mainstreaming tool encouraging gender equality throughout the phases which particularly involves women in consultations, decision making and enabling gender specific benefits during the implementation of the project.

During the design and development of the programme, gender equity has been maintained at the stakeholder consultation and workshop key informants at planning and management level (women in the management team) have been included. Women respondents have been included and preferred to answer the survey questions related to facilities and challenges at their workplaces (in different sections in textile units).

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment Report

## Development of a GCF proposal which is gender sensitive and proposes a gender action plan to be incorporated for the implementation of the programme.

### Knowledge sharing, poverty reduction, development, economic growth, sustainable value chain and provision of affordable and clean energy in order to improve the quality of life and welfare, lowering their sectoral and climate related vulnerabilities

#### 1.2. GCF's guideline on gender equality

The Green Climate Fund has a mandate to support a paradigm shift to low-emission and climate-resilient development and to mainstream gender equality into its objectives and guiding principles, through engaging governments and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, implementation and implementation of strategies and activities to be financed.

The Gender Policy and Action Plan was adopted in March 2015 by the GCF board. The policy states that a gender perspective is essential for achieving the paradigm shift. Impacts of climate change are borne differently by man and women and existing gender inequalities are likely to be exacerbated; greater vulnerability of women to climate change stems from gender norms and discrimination that result in a lack of access to resources, information and services; women have less control over land and other productive assets; fewer legal rights; lesser mobility and lesser political and professional representation.

### 1.2.1 Gender equality and equity requirements

The overall objective of the gender policy is to ensure that by adopting a gender-sensitive approach, the Fund will efficiently contribute to gender equality and achieve greater and more sustainable climate change outcomes.

It is a mandatory requirement of the gender initiatives under the GCF programme to satisfy the basic requirements i.e. gender equality and equity, inclusiveness, accountability, country ownership through engagement with all stakeholders including women; stakeholder inclusion; enhancing competencies through fund's institutional framework and equitable resource allocation for equal benefits for men and women through fund's climate action.

* Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Fund Projects: A practical manual to support the integration of gender equality in climate change projects (https://www.greenclimatefund.org/documents/20182/39456/Guidelines_GCF_Toolkit_Mainstreaming_Gender.pdf/8b6d1803-87f8-46a4-9aeb-0d601796ca7f)
* Gender Policy and Action Plan (https://www.greenclimatefund.org/documents/20182/28486/G2F_18_08_17_Gender_Policy_and_Action_Plan.pdf/a5b23939-9be4-4c31-a816-7f0800d77f95)

## Figure 1: The Fundamental Principles of GCF's Gender Policy

Mainstreaming gender in GCF programmes can be synthesized into an approach which mandates a gender analysis to assess social, economic and political factors underlying climate change-exacerbated gender inequality, action plans with methods and tools to promote equality, and gender sensitivity of the monitoring and evaluation framework. This will ensure that gender is considered as one of the outcomes and impacts of programme activities on women and men’s resilience to climate change.

In line with GCF's gender policy, GGCR has conducted a gender assessment and has ensured gender balance during all activities conducted under the PFP for this programme. The gender specific needs and priorities of both men and women have been assessed and taken up as an action plan as instructed by the GCF.

## Methodology

### Gender Assessment

Gender assessment is done to investigate gender roles and power relations in relation to the programme design and identify multiple causes of vulnerability, including gender inequality. It is a tool used to identify and build on the diverse knowledge and capacities within communities/households that can be leveraged for climate change adaptation.

This document is based on the review of national data and statistics, progress reports and government published documents; websites (ministries, departments, associations, international agencies and other organizations); interviews with key informants; focus group discussions; secondary data analysis; and semi-structured interviews with the stakeholders and gender specialists. The data and information obtained from the secondary sources is equated from the primary sources (site visit, field survey, worker and community interview, etc.). The sample size for this study was 59 workers in total: 30 female and 29 male facility engineer; 48 facility workers (male and female) & Community. The textile facilities were picked through random sampling. Once selected, these factories were visited for the survey and the workers were picked randomly from each factory. Stratification of sample was done as follows: Strata 1: Female and Strata 2: Male and then picked randomly. The sample was stratified in order to maintain a gender balance.

## 3. A Local Context Snapshot: Gender sensitivity and equity in textile sector in Bangladesh

Textile sector being the major recruiter in Bangladesh has a men-women ratio of 70:30. Some of the textile units are still using manual processes, which is not only time-consuming but also unsafe for women belonging to the most vulnerable sections of the society. However, both women and men labour have increased vulnerability due to wage and income gaps, access to finance, lack of rights for land and property, and limited access to education.

International statistics show that Bangladesh has a Gender Inequality Index Rank of 139th out of 187 and a Global Gender Gap Index Rank 72nd. These are the functions of reproductive health (maternal mortality ratio and adolescent birth rates), employment (share of parliamentary seats held by women and share of population in the labour force who have completed secondary education and have voted at last election/barron forces participation rate).

Hence, Bangladesh has a long way to go for which programmes that incorporate gender-sensitive approach and development. Development of this sector will have direct and indirect benefits for women currently employed and those that seek employment which will show in 5-10 years.

### 3.1 Gender and energy efficiency in textile sector

There exists a delicate relationship in gender and energy efficiency. It is essential to look at the trends of employment, which shows that there are more female workers than males. The employment of men in featured textile industry because of the common perceptions like processes in the textile units are more dangerous for women and also because of the nature of work such as handling and loading. Women experience occupational segregation, as most of which are employed are given lighter, sitting jobs. This leads to insufficient skill development and possibly lower wages for women employed.

The equipment and processes currently being used in textile sector in Bangladesh are labour and energy intensive. In most cases, smaller and medium textile units do not undertake energy audits and continue to use decade-old technologies. Quite evidently, lack of skills and use of age-old, labour-intensive processes lead to high energy consumption and low productivity.

In the past, buyers to bring down their costs, often overlooked the working conditions of their manufacturing labour class and exploited their skills for low wages. The government of Bangladesh has been making stringent rules and amendments. In the coming time to counter these challenges. Recently, it has been observed that the international buyers like H&M, IKEA, Ezra, Puma, etc., to meet

**Page 250**

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment Report

## GREEN CIRCLE FUND GRANTING PROPOSAL

their sustainability standards/commitments and CSR targets14, have started pushing the sewers/suppliers to maintain certain standard of employee facilities and safety15. The textile mills have been pushed further to reduce the gender gap in employment and promotion. In addition, a number of their buyers, mills are undertaking initiatives like equal wages, child care centres, maternity and paternity leave, flexible working hours, etc., to improve women’s participation in the workforce and awareness. Some of the reputed international brands demand “state of art” machinery and technology upgrades to be introduced in the mills, manufacturing their products, to maintain brand competencies and quality standards. The introduction of new energy efficient, world-class technologies and equipment that are user friendly has also created more opportunities for men and women employees alike16. However, the textile mills who have invested in energy-efficient machinery and technology are only a few key industries, while many other due to lack of financial support and expertise, lag much behind. There’s a need of financial assistance from the government for the adoption of technology upgrades in textile sector to open new opportunities for men and women both.

## 3.2 Perceptions and stereotypes

Perceptions continue to enhance gender inequality in the sector. Most of the key informants and survey respondents hold negative attitudes and views regarding the issue. The major perceptions/misconceptions for women, that continue to exist are:

- **Inability to perform labour intensive assignments**: The labour-intensive jobs are thus assigned to men.
- **Long, uncertain maternity leaves**: Major concern for hiring managers is the long leaves women employees take after childbirth.
- **Women are Technologically challenged**: Common perception is that the women can’t operate on complicated machinery due to their poor understanding of technology, mechanics and proper maintenance.
- **There’s no direct relationship between gender and energy efficiency**: Planners often tend to overlook the potential of new, user-friendly energy efficient equipment which will facilitate women empowerment.

## Inefficient to be involved in decision making

In a male-dominated sector, participation of women is very low. Women are not represented at all levels of management, including women entrepreneurs, MBOs and CEOs that are actively involved in planning and management.

### Restriction on mobility

Most women in rural regions are discouraged to go beyond the boundaries of their homes and villages due to social norms and restrictions which often permeate boundaries. Barriers on mobility have also reduced work opportunities.

Women both skilled and unskilled work in mills and perform domestic chores as well. Most of the existing misconceptions are led by the patriarchal structure of society which limit women empowerment. The adoption of new energy-efficient machinery could break many barriers for women to be better able to compete with men and progress financially.

## Institutional arrangements (Legal and administrative)

Women hold the rank of Prime Minister, speaker of parliament, opposition leader and Foreign Minister. Bangladesh women have experienced a series of legal political empowerment. The empowering policy, laws and institutions have been discussed below.

### 3.2.1 Policy

Bangladesh National Women Development Policy of 2011* mandates to incorporate gender perspective in matter of innovation, import and application of new technology. Further, to take initiatives to purge the harmful effects, if any, from the technology by conducting research on it if the application of such technology is likely to have adverse impact on interest of women and make necessary legislations, in favour of the women in the field of technology.

Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCCSAP), 2009* is a 10 year program (2009 – 2018) of the country to meet the challenge of climate change. BCCSAP addresses the needs of poor and vulnerable groups, including women.

Bangladesh Climate Change and Gender Action Plan (LGNCA), 2013 by Ministry of Environment and Forests determines the climate-vulnerabilities and gender-action plan for four priority areas namely food security social protection and health; comprehensive disaster management plan; infrastructure; mitigation and low carbon development.

The National Action Plan* to prevent violence against women and children (2013-2025) encompasses all pertinent legal practices, policies and programs of the Ministry of Women, Peace and Security Agenda: Prevention, Participation, Protection, and Relief and Recovery.

### 3.2.2 Laws

The government has undertaken various programs to materialize Vision 2021* by establishing rights of women, their empowerment and mainstreaming them in the overall development. Amendments have been made to the existing laws and new legislations have been made for protection of women and children:

- Mobile Court Act, 2009: The executive magistrate was given power to take steps by linking Section 509 of the Bangladesh Penal Code in the schedule of Mobile Court Act. This would result in a faster disposal of cases.
- Citizenship Act (amended), 2006: The provision for giving citizenship by mother to child was made by the national parliament by amending the citizenship act in 2009.
- Dowry Prohibition Act, 1961: To prevent the practice of dowry and other related offences and gifts and assets
- Women and Children Repression Prevention Act, 2000: To ensure the safety and protection to women at homes and their surroundings.

High Court has ruled that a woman’s name is to be in all official documents, school admission forms, either alone or with that of the father. Consequently, climate change and its impacts are enhancing vulnerabilities for women due to the lack of climate adaptive capacities.

The Bangladesh Labour Act, 2013: The law provides for equal remuneration in employment and provides for equal pay amongst men and women.

Bangladesh Labour Code, 2014: The code sets out minimum standards and punishments, prevents child labour and fire safety, wages, holiday and bonuses. Especially for women, there is a relation as the rules prohibits extreme jobs like lifting, carrying or discharging weights of more than 35 kg. Women are not allowed to work in mines and factories where it is completely prohibited. Inclusion in the apprenticeship program; provision of separate toilets for women; provision of child care on Creches and common form for women working in night shifts.

### 5. The National Council for Women and Children Development (NCWCD)
Chaired by the Honourable Prime Minister to provide guidance through laws and rules for protection of women and children and monitor the implementation of critical policy decisions.

### 6. Women in Development Focal Point (wIDFP)
Focal points Mechanism established in 1990 has a designated focal point from each ministry to ensure women concerns are integrated in policy, planning, budgeting, and monitoring and evaluation processes through committees. The wIDFPs of all ministries led to add gender concerns within the policies and plans of their respective ministries through its three components:
- WIDFP Implementation and Evaluation Committee (WIDIFPEC)
- Women Development Focal Point Network Committee (WDFIFNC)
- Women Development Focal Point Network Committee (WDFIFNC)

### 7. District and Upazila Levels
District Women Affairs Officer and Upazila Women Affairs Officer in coordination with other concerned officers shall discharge their responsibilities for the implementation of all the programs undertaken for the development of women.

### 8. Grassroots Level
In village and Unions, the women have been organized as self-sustained units under the guidance of a District Women’s Development Organization under different government organizations.

### 9. NGOs and Social Organizations
NGOs and social organizations have also been empowered with assistance from these institutions to change social attitude towards women and children through various activities and programmes aimed at empowerment of women.

### 10. International Co-operation
International, regional and sub-regional co-operation is encouraged through bilateral and multilateral financial and technical co-operation and exchange of information and technology aimed at empowerment of women.

### 11. Helpline Centres
Helpline Centres for violence against women and children Helpline No: 10921

Ministry of Finance in 2003, established a monitoring and reporting facility named Recurrent, Capital, Gender, and Poverty model which assesses the percentage of allocated funds that contribute to poverty reduction and gender equality. Women’s Preparatory Committee and other similar committees for women have been established to give legal guidance and conduct training for prevention of women and children abuse.

Most recently Bangladesh’s women launched a resistance movement to combat GBV* with a series of Social Movements from October 2016. Since the turn of the century, Bangladesh has weathered many conflicts with neighbouring states, India and Myanmar, as well as internal conflicts. In addition to these conflicts, women still face domestic violence and acid attacks.

## 3.1.4 Accords and Conventions

Bangladesh has been actively participating in many international human rights conventions, treaties, agreements and instruments and endorsed the Beijing Platform for Action (BPFA). It also adopted the United Nations Millennium Declaration [2000] and committed to the goals and targets known as the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). Few of the key relevant commitments are listed below.

### Chittagong Hill Tracts Peace Accord

The strategic location of women in human resource in conflict resolution was a challenge especially in case of the Chittagong Hill Tribes region in Bangladesh where many women were widowed during the conflicts, and they had to bear the burden of raising their children and taking care of their families. The accord gives decision-making bodies. The Chittagong Hill Tracts Peace Accord23 also known as Chittagong Hill Tracts Treaty, 1997 mandated to have 50(30%) seats for women in every Hill District Council and one-third of the total seats.

#### Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)

Bangladesh ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women in 1984 and has submitted seven progress reports on its implementation and participated in meetings of the Committee on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination.

#### ACCORD

The Accord on Fire and Building Safety was developed to ensure that no worker needs to face fire, building collapse or other hazards that can be prevented with reasonable health and safety measures. This agreement was signed in the immediate aftermath to the Rana Plaza building collapse on 24 April 2013 which killed 1,133 workers and critically injured thousands more. The Accord protects workers from:

- Refuse unsafe work
- Fire hazards due to their factory Safety Committee
- File a complaint when they see a safety problem in their factory
- Protection against reprisal for reporting safety-related matters
- Freedom of association and the right to form or join a safety committee

### Situational analysis: understanding vulnerabilities through Gender Assessment

The situational analysis is based on the real-time data collected on field through survey interviews with the managers, workers and other stakeholders.

Approximately two-thirds (67%) of all women workers are under the age group of 18 to 45 years of age. Most of them tend to leave this sector during mid-40s or early 50s.

## 4.1.6 Average work hours

The survey indicates that there are three: 8-hour shifts in medium and large textile units and small textile units usually have a 7 or 8-hour shift. Women mostly serve only a 5-hour shift during day hours.

As per the Labour Act, no adult worker shall ordinarily work or be required to work in an establishment for more than 48 (forty-eight) hours in a week; however, any deviation may happen as long as the clauses are not violated.

## 4.1.7 Distribution of income, average income and expenditure

Besides their main income source i.e., textile unit workers that come from farming background make money through agricultural produce, local shops in the area, etc. The analysis from the survey data speaks volumes about the disparities in income and wages for men and women (see Figure 5).

| Monthly Income from Job at Textile Unit: Men and Women |

Figure 5: Gender wise break down of the monthly income of textile worker group

The above graph reveals that women workers are paid around Bt10 less on average than their male counterparts. Lack of skills/technical knowledge and experience, adverse due to social and prejudiced selective recruitment practices, and lack of access to credit facilities for most of them work are the most frequently listed reason, as per the field survey. Government of Bangladesh through the Labour Rules, Labour Law and Women Development Policy have initiated change, but the disparities continue to exist in this sector.

## 4.1.8 Female led households: housing ownership and tenancy

Female head-households are those where women provide for the basic needs of the family like buying food (clothing, etc.). According to the survey, about 30% of female-headed households reported that women provide for the basic needs of the family which is close to the national statistics. Supplemented saying most of them live in rented houses, government and other agricultural farms. Most of the respondents reported that they are living in rented houses through their village. The men and women respondents gave an average estimate of their monthly rents which varies from 2,500 BDT to 4,500 BDT a month depending on their requirement (no. of family members and assets).

## 4.1.9 Savings and Loan

According to the Global Findex Database 2017[25], Bangladesh has only 38% gender gap in account ownership. Above 90% women and men textile workers do not have a savings and resort to local lending in times of needs. The workers that do have savings, have it either in banks or local cooperatives. Women who are married and live with their families tend to save more than those who are single. The frequency and intensity of social safety nets determine the savings behaviour.[26] As per the responses, 80% men and 75% women do not have loans and those who do, have personal loans, loans for house, vehicle and education.

### 4.1.10. Provident fund/Insurance

All the respondents stated that their employers do not give or facilitate provident fund opportunity. The respondents also reported that they do not have any kind of insurance either personal or provided by the employer. Recently, the labour rules have mandated the provision of Provident Fund by the employer and laid out cases for the same and also for the obligatory group insurance policy by the employer.

## 4.2 Health and Safety Assessment

### 4.2.1 Health issues faced by men and women in textile units

It’s known that the textile units have a low air quality index and a high suspended particulate matter which, in turn, can cause respiratory diseases and other lung diseases. Besides, there’s heat and chemicals in the work premises that the employers work with.[27] As reported by the workers, there are frequent instances of burning from the heated machinery and effluents and chemical reactions by the dyes and chemicals.

### 4.2.3 Health camps and medical facilities (like dispensary) and health insurance

Men and women in large industries reported that they enter conduct health camps in association with government doctors. The camps are held at the factory premises, and there is a 10-15 functional dispensaries in the large units that are looked after by government certified doctors. While the workers in small and medium textile units reported that there are no health camps or in-house medical facilities made available by their employer and they are directed to local medical practitioner in the vicinity.

In case of accidents and injuries at workplace in larger units, if the damage cannot be handled by a resident doctor, the victims are rushed to the nearby medical facility and the finances are covered by the employer. The respondents shared there are no health insurance being provided by the employers.

### 4.2.3 Maternity leaves

Respondents shared that there is a provision of maternity leaves for 2 to 4 months and the Government has also rationalised the maternity leave for 6 months and the female workers can also opt for maternity benefit during their absence. However, there isn’t provision for paternity leaves.

### 4.2.4 Violence at work or abuse

Men and women reported that not having been abused at work in large and medium units; however, in smaller units a few instances of violence above have been reported. Most of the instances of verbal

## Injuries and Accidents

Of the total respondents, 25% of women and men did mention having experienced an injury at work, while 15% of women and men shared their experiences of having witnessed accidents and injuries due to fire or other occupational hazards like heated boilers, hot drying machines and hot dry material taking off the drying machines for coating and finishing, chemical dyes, generators and electric circuits. The Labour Rules has set a limit on compensation in case of injury not covered by the employer if the accident/injury takes place at workplace. The Labour Rules has set rules in this regard. However, it is important to note that the compensation can be provided through the Labour Court. Also, the owner shall place Material Safety Data Sheet (MSDS) of dangerous materials in an easily noticeable place so that the employed worker can be well informed about the possible hazards.

### Safety precautions, training and tools

Typical risks in textile factory have previously been discussed to prevent accidents and injuries and reduce the intensity of damage. The following guidelines that needs to be followed while operating on machinery include: manual labour, dealing with electricity or heat.

There needs to be protection for respiratory health, eyes, fingers and limbs, for which the employer must mandatorily provide with necessary safety attire like safety shoes, helmets, goggles, masks, hand gloves etc. The employer should also ensure that the workers are well aware of how to use these materials and ensure their usage. Also, Employment of children, teenage labours in the activities that are declared as dangerous from time to time by the Inspector General or its representatives is prohibited.

The following issues were discussed with the respondents and their responses were recorded.

### Sanitation and access to basic facilities

#### Access to drinking water, washrooms and sanitation facilities (toilets, sanitary napkins, etc.)

As per the site visits, the drinking water available at the worksite are from the government’s water pipeline network. The size of the textile units determines whether the water is further processed by in-house water filters. Out of 20 units that were visited 17 units had water purification facilities.

Similarly, hygiene and washroom facilities are dependent on the size of this textile units. The larger the set-up and workforce, the better facilities are provided. As observed, separate washrooms are provided if the facilities are medium to large scale and have a bigger workforce.

However, Labour Rules state 'there will be provision of pure drinking water in easily accessible and suitable places for workers at all times'. This rule is intended to ensure that workers adopt a hygienic way' and lays down rules for Toilets and washrooms- the number, location and cleanliness of the toilets and wash room should be in accordance and in case of female workers, one toilet for each 25 females up to the first 100 and one for each 50 next.

## 6. Bridging Gaps: gender sensitivity and applicability of the programme

As per the GCCF’s Gender Policy, the following must be considered for the Gender Action Plan intended to:

### 6.1 Inclusivity in stakeholder approach (planning, management and implementation)

Infrastructure development programmes with the GCCF have a mandate to have a gender sensitive approach. This is reflected in all activities including stakeholder engagement and consultations to operationalise fair and balanced programme actions. Since, there has been a proactive engagement of the stakeholders from inception in planning and designing (consultations and surveys) of the programme, the existing issues in terms of gender parity are being addressed.

#### 6.1.1 First stakeholder workshop

A gender balanced scoping workshop on “Importance of energy efficiency in textile sector and its influence on women empowerment and gender” was conducted at Dhaka on 29th May 2019 to engage with stakeholders from ministries, associations, industry and civil society organisations and discuss issues related to gender parity in the sector. Relevant stakeholders could communicate their concerns and suggestions for the programme (gender sensitive approach in planning, designing, and implementation). The discussions on average male-female ratio, roles of gender in management and decision making, and other relevant aspects were held. The discussion on a participatory method approached, paved way for an integrated programme that’s socially benefitting to the vulnerable group.

#### 6.1.2 Second stakeholder workshop

A validation cum consultation workshop for dissemination of the findings of the gender assessment done through various stakeholder engagement methods (field survey, worker interview, manager interview, focus group discussion with women and men) was conducted in Dhaka on Environment, Social and Gender in Textile Sector of Bangladesh” on 29th July 2019 in Dhaka. Most of the distinguished stakeholders from ministries, associations, trade unions, industries, academicians and development professionals participated in the workshop to state views and comment on the observations and conclusions of the exercise.

### 6.2 Monitoring Interventions and Evaluation

Gender sensitive vision and objectives has led to specific lag-frame indicators to embed gender inclusion in all activities. The proposed lag frame will measure the performance of the gender-based activities. The action plan will present:
